922:                       7. Subgraph Deletion
923: 
924: All the previously considered local reduction problems can be reformulated as
925: questions for the existence of a subgraph X in some k-connected graph G such
926: that κ(G − V (X)) > k − |V (X)| (alternatively: κ(G − E(X)) > k − |E(X)|),
927: where, in addition, X respects some size- or connectivity conditions, or, more
928: generally, X belongs to a certain class H of graphs. For example, when asking
929: for a k-contractible edge in a graph G we ask for a subgraph X ∼
930:                                                                = K2 of G such
931: that κ(G − V (X)) > k − 2, and H would be the class consisting of K2 .
932: As we have seen, such an X need not to exist. Is there one which keeps the
933: connectivity high? Let us formulate this more precisely. Given a class H of
934: 
935:                                        19                            June 12, 2018
936: 
937: graphs, does there exist a function f (k) such that every f (k)-connected graph
938: G admits a subgraph X from H such that G − V (X) (alternatively: G − E(X))
939: is k-connected?
940: For every non-empty finite graph class H, such an f obviously exists (take
...
1008: Conjecture 7.3 is true for k ≤ 4, where the optimal values are f (2) = 5 [82],
1009: f (3) = 9 [64], and f (4) = 8 [34]. These values are sharp, and they are not
1010: monotone in k. Moreover, every cubic 3-connected graph on at least 13 vertices
1011: has a contractible subgraph on 5 vertices, and it is maybe interesting to see that
1012: the ‘local character’ of the proof yields a generalization to 3-connected graphs
1013: of average degree at most 3 + 1/132 [44]. The conjecture is wide open in general.
1014: For example, it is not known whether there exists a k such that its restriction
1015: to k-connected graphs is true. However, if we do not insist that H as in the
1016: statement is connected, then there is a positive result [36]:
1017: 
1018: Theorem 7.4 For every ℓ ≥ 1 there exists an f (ℓ) such that every 3-connected
1019: graph on at least f (ℓ) vertices contains a subgraph X on ℓ vertices such that
1020: G − V (X) is 2-connected.
1021: 
1022: If we delete edges instead of vertices then there is the following positive result
1023: from [44] (based on a Theorem of Lemos and Oxley [47]): For every ℓ ≥ 1
1024: there exists an f (ℓ) such that every 4-connected graph on at least f (ℓ) vertices
1025: contains a path or a star X on ℓ vertices such that G − E(X) is 2-connected.
1026: Mader generalised the statement of Theorem 7.4 to higher connectivity, as fol-
1027: lows [59]:

1152: t-connected subgraph T . Since S := G − E(T ) is a supergraph of H, it follows
1153: that the edge sets of S and T partition E(G) in the desired way.
1154: The first non-trivial case of Conjecture 8.2 has been settled by Thomassen [78]:
1155: 
1156: Theorem 8.4 Every (k + 3)-connected graph G contains an induced cycle C
1157: such that G − V (C) is k-connected.
1158: 
1159: The proof is by induction on V (G) of the stronger statement that there is an
1160: induced cycle C such that every vertex not in V (C) has at most 3 neighbors
1161: in V (C) (such that G − V (C) is k-connected). Obviously, every triangle would
1162: serve as such a C, so that we may assume that G is triangle free. By Theorem
1163: 5.3, G contains a contractible edge e. From a cycle in G/e with the desired
1164: properties it is the easy to obtain one in G. — Along these lines, let me also
1165: point to Mader’s result that every (k + 2)-connected graph contains a cycle C
1166: such that G − E(C) is k-connected [52, p. 190]. In fact, C can be taken as an
1167: induced cycle here.

1170: 
1171: It seems to be extremely difficult to prove local versions of this statement. For
1172: example, the statement that ‘there is a function g such that every for every edge
1173: e of every g(k)-connected graph G there exists an induced cycle C containing e
1174: such that G − V (C) is k-connected’ is equivalent to Conjecture 7.6, as has been
1175: observed by Thomassen. Even the following, weaker problem, is open:
1176: 
1177: Conjecture 8.5 For every k, there exists an h(k) such that every for every ver-
1178: tex v of every h(k)-connected graph G there exists an induced cycle C containing
1179: v such that G − V (C) is k-connected.
1180:

69: Jerrum, Sinclair and Vigoda [25], Barvinok [2], and the references contained therein).
70:    From the mathematical perspective, there are two main lines of research centered on the perma-
71: nent: the study of permanents and probability (see, e.g., Friedland et al. [10]); min/max questions
72: concerning a number of inequalities, peaking with the proofs by Egorychev [8] and Falikman [9]
73: (see also [30]) of the 1926 van der Waerden’s conjecture about permanents of doubly stochastic
74: matrices.
75:    When focusing our attention on physics, we may divide into at least four groups the known
76: applications of the permanent: the many aspects of the dimer problem, some uses involving the
77: hafnian, Monte Carlo generators, and, more pertinent to our discussion, linear optical networks for
78: quantum information processing. Intuitively, the permanent tends to be related to bosons while
79: the determinant to fermions. A list of applications follows.
80:    The problem of computing the permanent of a (0, 1)-matrix is the same as the problem of counting
81: the number of perfect matchings in a bipartite graph. This translates into the dimer problem
82: [22, 19]. The problem is traditionally related to models for adsorption of diatomic molecules on
83: crystal surfaces, mixtures of molecules of diﬀerent sizes and the cell-cluster theory of the liquid state
84: (see, e.g., Welsh [42]). New applications occur in the study of conﬁgurations of melting crystals
85: (Okounkov et al. [27]), BPS black holes (Heckman and Vafa [18]), and quiver gauge theories
86: (Hanany and Kennaway [14]).
87:    The hafnian of a matrix A, denoted by hf(A), is a polynomial generalizing the permanent as
88: the pfaﬃan generalizes the determinant. This notion was originarily introduced by Caianiello to
89: express the perturbation expansions of boson ﬁeld theories [5].

116: simpler problem. As we have mentioned earlier, the permanent does not have any known geometric
117: interpretation. The present work contributes in this direction, by interpreting the permanent as an
118: angle between vectors (or a cosine, to be more precise).
119:    We remark on a historic note that was pointed out to us by an anonymous referee regarding
120: Conjecture 1 (see below). A special case of the proof was considered in Ref. [30]. This manu-
121: script [45] contains a proof for situations in which the symmetric state has an expansion (in either
122: the computational basis or the symmetric basis |S(n, ~k)i deﬁned below) which requires only positive
123: coeﬃcients. This was shown independently in Ref. [19] by a completely diﬀerent method. Subse-
124: quently, a more general proof (with extended results) using elementary matrix theory appeared in
125: Ref. [20], in which it was recognized that a proof was already given earlier in the context of the
126: theory of tensor norms and homogeneous polynomials over Banach spaces, e.g., see Ref. [9].
127:    The rest of the paper is structured as follows. In Section 2, we deﬁne the geometric measure of
128: entanglement. In Section 3, we state and prove our results. For the sake of clarity, we shall also
129: include a few examples.
130: 
131:                               2. Geometric measure of entanglement
132:    The geometric measure of entanglement used here was ﬁrstly introduced by Shimony [42] in
133: the setting of bipartite pure states. It was generalized to the multipartite states by Barnum and
134: Linden [1], and further extended by Wei and Goldbart [48]. The intuition beyond the measure
135: consists of thinking about entanglement as an angle between two states: namely, the state in
136: analysis and a product state, i.e., a state with zero entanglement. Crucially, the product state is

440:   We conclude by asking the following question and then providing a partial answer:
441: Conjecture 1. Is it true that in order to obtain the maximal overlap of any permutation invariant
442: state, we can assume the product state to be a tensor product of the same single-party state?
443:    As we have pointed out in the historical note in the Introduction, this answer is yes. Here we give
444: simple proof for certain classes of permutation invariant states, beyond the basis states discussed
445: above. For the general proof, we refer to Refs. [9, 20].
446:    The ﬁrst class we consider is the case where the coeﬃcients c~k ’s are nonnegative. To obtain
447: the maximal overlap for the corresponding state |ψi, we can as well set the coeﬃcients in the
448: unentangled state |φi to be nonnegative, as the goal is to maximize the overlap between |ψi and
449: |φi. Thus we have

517:     It takes 8 real parameters to describe the generic permutation invariant states for three qubits.
518: If we start with 4 arbitrary nonnegative coeﬃcients and supplement with arbitrary U(2) transfor-
519: mations (which have 4 real parameters), we have then 8 real parameters in total. The number boils
520: down to 6 in both cases, if we take into account normalization and global phase. This counting
521: suggests that the statement in Conjecture 1 may be true, which we now know it is correct. We
522: note that there have been applications using this to search for maximal entangled states; see, e.g.,
523: Ref. [43], in which the above GHZ and W states belong to such a family.
524: 
525: Problem 1. Given that the answer to Conjecture 1 is correct, can we then derive a more general
526: permanent inequality than that of Carlen, Lieb and Loss?
527: 
528:    We give our reasoning of why such a general inequality might exist. The inequality of Carlen, Lieb
529: and Loss has enabled us to prove a partial answer to Conjecture 1 for nonnegative symmetric states,
530: but not the most generality. The more general permanent inequality if it existed would enable us
531: to establish the proof of Conjecture 1 in the most generality and would be such an inequality that
532: we seek. Since Conjecture 1 is correct, we expect a more general permanent inequality might exist.

556:   Appendix A. Ramsey’s numbers for well-founded trees and order
557:                              type of set systems
558:    Let Xi be a quasi-ordering with the maximal order type otp(Xi ) < ω and Li be a
559: set system with the order type dim Li < ω (i = 1, 2). Let Ra(n, m) be the Ramsey
560: number [11] of n and m. Then we have
561:     (1) [3, Lemma 6] For the memberwise union L1 ∪     e L2 = {L1 ∪ L2 ; L1 ∈
562:         L1 , L2 ∈ L2 },
563:                            e L2 ) + 1 < Ra(dim L1 + 2, dim L2 + 2).
564:                     dim(L1 ∪
565:     (2) [3, Theorem 8] otp (X1 ∩ X2 ) < Ra(otp(X1 ) + 1, otp(X2 ) + 1).
566:    We wish to generalize these two for general ordinal numbers. To directly gener-
567: alize the proof argument of the two, we pose a following question:
568: Question 4. Prove or disprove: “If we color a well-founded tree T0 of order type
569: α with red and black, then either there is a well-founded tree T1 of order type β
570: such that T1 is homeomorphically embedded into the red nodes of T0 , or there is
571: a well-founded tree T2 of order type γ such that T2 is homeomorphically embedded

39:                                              A well quasi-ordering [16] (wqo for short) is, by definition, a quasi-ordering
40:                                           (X, ) which has neither an infinite antichain nor an infinite descending chain.
41:                                           Wqos are employed in algebra [16], combinatorics [23, 35], formal language the-
42:                                           ory [7, 9, 14, 32], and so on.
43:                                              Wqos and related theorems such as Higman’s theorem [16], Ramsey’s theo-
44:                                           rem [6], and so on are sometimes used in computational learning theory. In
45:                                           [22, 29, 39], a sufficient conditions for set systems being learnable is studied with
46:                                           Ramsey’s theorem, In [38], for a set system L, Shinohara-Arimura considered the
47:                                           unbounded unions of L, that is, the class L<ω of arbitrary finite unions of members
48:                                           of L, and then used Higman’s theorem to study a sufficient condition for it being
49:                                           learnable. In [10], de Brecht employed wqo to calibrate mind change complexity
50:                                           of unbounded unions of restricted pattern languages. Motivated by [22, 29, 39], a
51:                                           somehow systematic study on the relation between wqos and a class of learnable
52:                                           set systems is done in [3], as follows:

138: It is considered in the study on the learnability of language classes such as a class
139: of regular pattern languages [38]. We characterize the set systems L such that L<ω
140: are again fesss, and then we prove that for every bess L, L<ω is an fess.
141:    We remark that another importance of set system L<ω . We conjecture that the
142: order type of an fess is the supremum, actually the maximum, of the order types
143: of the “linearizations” of the fess. This conjecture corresponds to a proposition
144: useful in investigating wqos:
145: Proposition 2 ([12]). The order type of a wqo is the maximum order type of the
146: linearizations of the wqo.
147:    A “linearization” of an fess L seems to be a subfamily of L<ω .
148:    We hope that the study on fesss and besss are useful in solving problems (e.g.
149: decision problem of timed Petri-nets [1, 2], the multiplicative exponential linear
150: logic [11]) which are related to wqos and fesss but hard to solve with conventional
151: arguments for wqos and bqos.

URL https://arxiv.org/pdf/math-ph/0201040v1 FILE sources/565da1080a17988b.txt
21:                                       ample is the lattice based on the Sierpinski gasket. We introduce a new renormalization
22:                                       map which appears to be a rational self-map of a compact complex manifolds. We re-
23:                                       late some characteristics of its dynamics with some characteristics of the spectrum of
24:                                       our operator. More specifically, we give an explicite formula for the density of states
25:                                       in terms of the Green current of the map, and we relate the indeterminacy points of
26:                                       the map with the so-called Neuman-Dirichlet eigenvalues which lead to eigenfunctions
27:                                       with compact support on the unbounded lattice. Depending on the asymptotic degree
28:                                       of the map we can prove drastic different spectral properties of the operator. Hence,
29:                                       this work aims at a generalization and a better understanding of the initial work of
30:                                       physisits Rammal and Toulouse on the Sierpinski gasket (cf [31], [30]).

145: the generalization, this brings new understanding of the models. In particular, the

148: Sierpinski gasket (where the renormalization map involved was 1-dimensionnal), such

160: the dynamics of rational maps in higher dimensions. It is interesting to note that many
161: of the key-notions in this field (such as the degrees of the iterates, the indeterminacy

343:    So, the measures µ and µN D give important information on the spectral properties
344:                       ±
345: of the operators H<∞>     . Hence, these two measures deserve to be understood and our
346: aim in this text is to describe the relations that exist between these measures and the
347: dynamics of a certain renormalization map that we construct.
348:    There are two renormalization maps, closely related, which play an important role.
349: We do not define precisely these maps in this introduction, we just describe some
350: of their properties. The first one, denoted by T , is defined on the space SymG , the
351: space of G-invariant symmetric operators on CF , and is a rational map (i.e. T Q, for
352: Q in SymG is rational in the coefficients of Q). For example in the case of the the
353: Sierpinski gasket SymG ∼ C2 : indeed, if W0 is the space of constant functions on F
354: and W1 its orthogonal supplement (for the natural scalar product on CF ), then any
355: element of SymG can be written thanks to 2 coordinates (u0 , u1 ) ∈ C2 under the form
356: u0 p|W0 + u1 p|W1 where p|W0 and p|W1 are the orthogonal projections respectively on W0
357: and W1 . We do not define explicitely the map T in this introduction (cf section 2 and
358: 3.1), but this map is easy to compute; for example in the case of the Sierpinski gasket,
...
449: and µN D . With the iterates of g we can associate an asymptotic degree d∞ (called
450: the dynamical degree, cf section 4.3) and we show in theorem (IV.1) that the following
451: dichotomy holds:
452:    – If d∞ < N , then S is a countable sum of currents of integration on hypersurfaces
453:       of LG . In this case µN D = µ, thus the spectrum is pure point with compactly
454:       supported eigenfunctions, almost surely in ω. (We also show that the number of
455:                                        +       N D | grows like nd∞ ).
456:       Neuman only eigenvalues, i.e. |ν<n> − ν<n>
457:    – If d∞ = N , then the current S does not charge hypersurfaces; it is the Green
458:       current of the map g. In particular, it is null on the Fatou set of g. Moreover,
459:       generically (in a sense made precise in theorem (IV.1)) we have µN D = 0, i.e.
460:       there does not exist N-D eigenfunctions.
461: Note that a similar dichotomy theorem was shown in [17] for the particular class of
462: decimable fractals, for which there exists a 1-dimensional renormalization map that
463: relate the spectrum on different scales (but the relation with the N-D spectrum was
464: not made).
465: 
466:    Let us now make some remarks. The renormalization map we consider here is not the
467: same as the one considered in our previous work [34]. The introduction of this new map
468: is the key-point that allows us to handle the case of lattices based on general finitely
469: ramified self-similar sets. In particular, this map is not defined on a projective space.
...
469: ramified self-similar sets. In particular, this map is not defined on a projective space.
470: This induces several difficulties, for example the notion of degree is more complicated
471: and is related to the action of the map on some cohomology groups. But there are
472: several facts that seem to indicate that the map we consider is the good renormalization
473: map: the first one is that the indeterminacy points of g, which have a crucial influence
474: on the dynamical properties of g, have a clear meaning in terms of the Neuman-Dirichlet
475: eigenvalues of our operator. The second is that the map g behaves well in the non-
476: degenerate case, i.e. when d∞ = N : to be more precise, the map g is algebraically
477: stable (cf definition in appendix), and this allows us to define the Green current; this
478: is not the case for other maps we could consider (cf section 4.5 where we compare
479: different renormalization maps) and which are birationnally equivalent to g. Finally,
480: let us mention that considering expressions like exp(ηQη) in the Grassman algebra
481: is very natural in the context of supersymmetry, and the theory of supersymmetry
482: appeared to be very useful in the context of random Schrödinger operators (cf for
483: example [25], [45], [46]).
484:    The important question to determine the type of spectrum of the operator on the
485: infinite lattice remains largely open: with our technics we are only able to character-
486: ize a small part of the spectrum, namely the part of the spectrum which corresponds
487: to compactly supported eigenfunctions (i.e. N-D eigenfunctions). It would be very
488: interesting to determine the almost sure type of the spectrum of the operator (for
489:

2748:                         4. Regularity of the density of states
2749:   We first state a conjecture and show how it can be related to results on Lelong
2750: numbers of the Green current.
2751: Conjecture IV.1. — The measure µ − µN D is continuous, i.e. it does not charge any
2752: point.
2753:    We introduce the notion of Lelong numbers of a psh function. Let u be a psh function
2754: in a neighbourhood U ⊂ Cn of 0. Then the Lelong number of u at 0 is defined by
2755:                     ν(u, 0) = max{c ≥ 0 s.t. u(z) ≤ c log kzk + O(1)}.

2761: The Lelong number of a positive closed (1, 1) current T is defined by ν(T, p) = ν(u, p)
2762: for any local psh potential u of T , T = ddc u. On C it is easy to see that the Lelong
2763: number at p of a positive closed (1, 1) current (i.e. a positive measure) is the mass of
2764: the point p.

3299:    The main open problem is to understand the almost sure Lebesgue decomposition
3300: of the operator H<∞> . Let us be more precise. Consider a typical blow-up ω, in par-
3301: ticular for which ∂F<∞> = ∅. The Hilbert space D<∞> can be decomposed into
3302: three parts Hac , Hsc , Hpp such that the restriction of H<∞> to these subspaces is
3303: respectively absolutely continuous, singular continuous or purely ponctual (i.e. this
3304: means that the spectral measure of any function in these subspaces is resp. abso-
3305: lutely continuous, singular continuous or purely ponctual). The spectrum of H<∞>
3306: and the spectrum of the restriction of H<∞> to Hac , Hsc , Hpp are respectively denoted
3307: by Σ(ω), Σac (ω), Σsc (ω), Σpp (ω) (they a priori depend on ω). Proposition 2 of [36]
3308: states that these sets are almost surely constant in ω, i.e. equal to deterministic sets
3309: Σ, Σac , Σsc , Σpp for almost all blow-up ω.
3310: One can be more precise and split the Hilbert space Hpp into two parts: the first ,that

...
3327: different components of the spectrum by Σ· = suppµ· . The question is wether it is
3328: possible to compute these measures from some caracteristics of the map R, as we com-
3329: puted µ and µN D from its Green function and the asymptotic multiplicites of its zeros.
3330: In particular, it would be interesting to understand at which condition there may be
3331: a pure point component Σ̃pp not created by the Neuman-Dirichlet eigenfunctions (i.e.
3332: at which conditions there are L2 eigenfunctions which are not generated by compactly
3333: supported eigenfunctions). But all these questions seem to be very difficult.
3334:    Let us now make few remarks and a conjecture. When N > d∞ we know that
3335: the spectrum is pure point with compactly supported eigenfunctions, i.e. that Σac =
3336: Σsc = Σ̃pp = ∅. Consider now the case where N = d∞ . We showed in this text
3337: that the N-D spectrum is related to the zeros of R, i.e. to the indeterminacy points
3338: of g. Considering that eigenfunctions of H<∞> which are not with compact support
3339: are in some sense approximated by functions with compact support which are not far
3340: from being eigenfunctions we can ask the following very imprecise question: does the
3341: existence of a pure point component in the spectrum different from the N-D component,
3342: i.e. Σ̃pp 6= ∅ imply that the iterates of g on φ(λ) approach the indeterminacy points,

3615:           B. Dynamics of rational maps on the projective space Pk
3616: B.1. Definitions, indeterminacy points. — Let Pk be the complex projective
3617: space of dimension k and π : Ck+1 \ {0} → Pk the canonical projection. A point z in
3618: Pk can be represented in homogeneous coordinates by
3619:                                      z = [z0 : · · · : zk ],
3620: where [z0 : · · · : zk ] denotes the point π(z0 , . . . , zk ). Consider now a homogeneous
3621: polynomial map R : Ck+1 → Ck+1 of degree d, i.e. R = (R0 , . . . , Rk ) where the Ri ’s
3622: are homogeneous polynomials of degree d in the variables (z0 , . . . , zk ). Suppose that
3623: the Ri have no common factor. It is natural to associate a map f on the projective
3624: space Pk such that the following diagram commutes:
3625:                                                R
3626:                                     Ck+1 −−−→ Ck+1
3627:                                               
3628:                                      π        π
3629:                                      y         y
3630:                                                 f
3631:                                      Pk     −−−→        Pk
3632: The map f can be defined only on the set Pk \ I where I = π{z, R(z) = 0}. The set I
...
3629:                                      y         y
3630:                                                 f
3631:                                      Pk     −−−→        Pk
3632: The map f can be defined only on the set Pk \ I where I = π{z, R(z) = 0}. The set I
3633: is called the set of indeterminacy points of f and is an analytic subset of codimension
3634: at least 2 (indeed if all the Ri ’s vanish on an analytic hypersurface then a polynomial
3635: can be factorized in R). Usually one writes f in homogeneous coordinates as
3636:                                      f = [R0 , . . . Rk ].
3637: The function f is a rational map of Pk (a precise meaning to this notion is given at the
3638: beginning of appendic C) and the polynomial map R, which is called the lift of f to
3639: Ck+1 , exists and is unique (up to a multiplicative constant) for any rational map of Pk .
3640: The degree of f is defined as the degree of R, i.e. d. The map f is holomorphic on Pk
3641: if and only if I = ∅. The map f is said to be dominating if its differential is generically
3642: surjective. If f is dominating and if S is a positive closed current of bidegree (1, 1)
3643: with potential u on Ck+1 we define the pull-back f ∗ S as the current with potential u◦R.
3644: 
3645:    At a point p ∈ I the image of f can be defined as a subset of Pk : let Bp be the subset
3646: of Pk defined by
3647:                                 Bp = ∩ǫ>0 f (B(p, ǫ) \ I)

3649: 66
...
3656: p can be sent into a subvariety of dimension strictly smaller. These phenomenons are
3657: new compared to the situation of 1-dimensional complex dynamics. This results in
3658: the fact that the degree of the iterates of f do not necessarily grow like dn . Let us
3659: explain this clearly: suppose for example that a hypersurface V is sent by f on a point
3660: of indeterminacy. Then the map R2 is null on V and a hence a polynomial can be
3661: factorized in R2 . The degree of f 2 is then smaller than degree(f )2 since f 2 is lifted to
3662: a polynomial map with degree smaller than that of R2 . In general, we can always write
3663:                                        Rn = hn Rn ,
3664: where hn is a homogeneous polynomial and Rn a homogeneous polynomial map, with
3665: no common factor, of degree dn = dn − deg(hn ). The map Rn is a reduced lift of f n ,
3666: thus the degree of f n is dn . We say there there is decreaseness in the degree if dn < dn .
3667:

3669: degree d and d′ . The degree of f ◦ g is smaller than dd′ and equal to dd′ if and only if
3670: there does not exist any hypersurface V such that g(V \ Ig ) ⊂ If .

3690: B.2. Green function and Green current. The algebraically stable case.—
3691: Let f be a rational map, algebraically stable, with degree d. We denote by In the set
3692: of indeterminacy points of f n , we have In ⊂ Im for n ≤ m, and we set I∞ = ∪∞ n=1 In .

3695:        p such that f|Un is equicontinuous.

3697:      – The Julia set is the complement of the Fatou set.
3698:      – A point p is normal if there exist neighbourhoods U of p and V of I such that
3699:        f n (U ) ∩ V = ∅ for all n. We denote by N the set of normal points.

3768:      C. Iteration of meromorphic maps on compact complex manifolds
3769: C.1. Definitions, indeterminacy points. — Let X and Y be compact complex
3770: manifolds. Denote by π1 : X × Y → X and π2 : X × Y → Y the projection on
3771: the first and second components of X × Y . A meromorphic function f : X → Y is
3772: defined by its graph Γf ⊂ X × Y , an irreducible subvariety of X × Y for which the
3773: first projection is a proper modification, i.e. such that there exists a proper subvariety
3774: V ⊂ X such that π1 is a biholomorphism from Γf \ π1−1 (V ) to X \ V . We denote by
3775: If ⊂ X the set of points of indeterminacy, i.e. the set of points where π1 has no local
3776: inverse. The set If is an analytic subset of codimension at least 2. Of course, at a point
3777: x ∈ X \If the image by f is defined by f (x) = π2 (π1−1 (x)). When x is an indeterminacy
3778: point the image by f is an analytic set defined by f (x) = π2 (π1−1 ({x})). The map f is
3779: holomorphic on X \ If . The map is said to be dominating if π2 is surjective.
3780:    If g : Y → Z is another meromorphic map then the graph Γg◦f is defined as the
3781: closure:

29:                                       a smooth projective variety (more precisely, this variety is isomorphic to a product
30:                                       of Lagrangian Grassmanian). We relate some characteristics of the dynamics of its
31:                                       iterates with some characteristics of the spectrum of our operator. More specifically,
32:                                       we give an explicit formula for the density of states in terms of the Green current
33:                                       of the map, and we relate the indeterminacy points of the map with the so-called
34:                                       Neuman-Dirichlet eigenvalues which lead to eigenfunctions with compact support on
35:                                       the unbounded lattice. Depending on the asymptotic degree of the map we can prove
36:                                       drastically different spectral properties of the operators. Our formalism is valid for
37:                                       the general class of finitely ramified self-similar sets (i.e. for the class of pcf self-similar
38:                                       sets of Kigami, cf [25]). Hence, this work aims at a generalization and a better under-
39:                                       standing of the initial work of the physicists Rammal and Toulouse on the Sierpinski
40:                                       gasket (cf [35], [34]).

187: the generalization, this brings new understanding of the models. In particular, the
188: renormalization map involved is now multidimensional and certain notions which are
189: specific to the dynamics in higher dimension and which were hidden in the case of the
190: Sierpinski gasket (where the renormalization map involved was 1-dimensional), such
191: as the notion of indeterminacy points (which corresponds to the singularities of the
192: map), the degree of the iterates, enter the discussion and play an important role. In
193: comparison with our previous work, [38], the main progress that allows us to handle
194: the general case is the construction of a new renormalization map. This renormal-
195: ization map is a rational map defined on some compact Kähler manifold. It is of the
196: type of the maps considered in [13], [12], and our techniques rely heavily on recent
197: works of Fornaess Sibony, Diller Favre, Guedj (cf [45], [16], [13], [12], [14]) on the
198: dynamics of rational maps in higher dimensions. It is interesting to note that many
199: of the key notions in this field (such as the degrees of the iterates, the indeterminacy
200: points, the Green current) find a significance related to the spectral properties of our
201: operators. In particular, we are able to give an explicit expression for the density of
202: states in terms of the Green current of the map and we prove that the molecular states
203: of Rammal (called Neuman-Dirichlet eigenvalues in the text) correspond exactly to
204: the indeterminacy points of the map.

512: and µN D . With the iterates of g we can associate an asymptotic degree d∞ (called
513: the dynamical degree, cf section 4.3) and we show in theorem (4.1) that the following
514: dichotomy holds:
515:     – If d∞ < N , then S is a countable sum of currents of integration on hypersurfaces
516:       of LG . In this case µN D = µ, thus the spectrum is pure point with compactly
517:       supported eigenfunctions, almost surely in ω. (We also show that the number
518:                                           +       ND
519:       of Neuman only eigenvalues, i.e. |ν<n>   − ν<n>  |, grows like nd∞ ).
520:     – If d∞ = N , then the current S does not charge hypersurfaces; it is the Green
521:       current of the map g. In particular, it is null on the Fatou set of g. Moreover,
522:       generically (in a sense made precise in theorem (4.1)), we have µN D = 0, i.e.
523:       there does not exist N-D eigenfunctions.
524: Note that a similar dichotomy theorem was shown in [20] for the particular class of
525: decimable fractals, for which there exists a 1-dimensional renormalization map that
526: relate the spectrum on different scales (but the relation with the N-D spectrum was
527: not made).

529:    Let us now make some remarks. The renormalization map we consider here is
530: not the same as the one considered in our previous work [38]. The introduction of
531: this new map is the key point that allows us to handle the case of lattices based on
532: general finitely ramified self-similar sets. In particular, this map is not defined on a
...
532: general finitely ramified self-similar sets. In particular, this map is not defined on a
533: projective space. This induces several difficulties, for example, the notion of degree is
534: more complicated and is related to the action of the map on some cohomology groups.
535: But there are several facts that seem to indicate that the map we consider is the good
536: renormalization map: the first one is that the indeterminacy points of g, which have
537: a crucial influence on the dynamical properties of g, have a clear meaning in terms
538: of the Neuman-Dirichlet eigenvalues of our operator. The second is that the map g
539: behaves well in the non-degenerate case, i.e. when d∞ = N : to be more precise, the
540: map g is algebraically stable (cf definition in appendix), and this allows us to define
541: the Green current; this is not the case for other maps we could consider (cf section
542: 4.5 where we compare different renormalization maps) and which are birationally
543: equivalent to g. Finally, let us mention that considering expressions like exp(ηQη)
544: in the Grassman algebra is very natural in the context of supersymmetry, and that
545:

3182: 4.4. Regularity of the density of states
3183:   We first state a conjecture and show how it can be related to results on Lelong
3184: numbers of the Green current.
3185: 
3186: Conjecture 4.1. — The measure µ − µN D is continuous, i.e. it does not charge any
3187: point.
3188: 
3189:    We introduce the notion of Lelong numbers of a psh function. Let u be a psh
3190: function in a neighborhood U ⊂ Cn of 0. Then the Lelong number of u at 0 is defined
3191: by
3192:                   ν(u, 0) = max{c ≥ 0 s.t. u(z) ≤ c log kzk + O(1)}.
3193: The Lelong number of a positive closed (1, 1) current T is defined by ν(T, p) = ν(u, p)
3194: for any local psh potential u of T , T = ddc u. On C it is easy to see that the Lelong
3195: number at p of a positive closed (1, 1) current (i.e. a positive measure) is the mass of
3196: the point p.
3197:    Coming back to our situation, we suppose that we are in the lattice case and
3198: that d∞ = N (otherwise µ − µN D = 0). We remark that the Lelong number of
3199: ddc ln kRn ◦ φk at λ is the order of vanishing ord(Rn ◦ φ, λ). By proposition (2.3), ii),
...
3198: that d∞ = N (otherwise µ − µN D = 0). We remark that the Lelong number of
3199: ddc ln kRn ◦ φk at λ is the order of vanishing ord(Rn ◦ φ, λ). By proposition (2.3), ii),
3200:                                                       ND
3201: we know that this order of vanishing is equal to ν<n>    ({λ}). Hence, we see that the
3202: conjecture is equivalent to
3203:                             1
3204:                       lim       ν(ddc ln kRn ◦ φk, λ) = ν(ddc G ◦ φ, λ).
3205:                      n→∞ N n
3206: 
3207: Since the current S = ddc G is the limit of the sequence of currents N1n Sn with po-
3208: tential N1n ddc ln kRn k the question is equivalent to know whether the limit of the
3209: Lelong numbers of the restriction of the currents Sn to a curve is equal to the Lelong
3210: numbers of the restriction of the limit S. There is, at the present time, only one result
3211: on Lelong numbers of Green currents: it says, cf [13] theorem 2.4.6, that the Lelong
3212: numbers of the Green current are null on the complement of the indeterminacy points
3213: of the iterates: with our notations it means that the Lelong number of S is null when
3214: the Lelong numbers of the Sn are null. Unfortunately, it cannot be applied easily
3215: to our case. Firstly, because it does not state that the Lelong numbers of S is the
3216: limit of the Lelong numbers of N1n Sn at any point, and secondly, because we need
3217: information on the restriction of the Green current of S to the curve φ(λ), which can
3218: be strictly bigger than the Lelong number of S. Nevertheless, it could certainly be
...
3216: limit of the Lelong numbers of N1n Sn at any point, and secondly, because we need
3217: information on the restriction of the Green current of S to the curve φ(λ), which can
3218: be strictly bigger than the Lelong number of S. Nevertheless, it could certainly be
3219: possible, with a little more work, to prove a result in the generic case, but we prefer
3220: to leave the general result as a conjecture.

3758:         REMARKS, QUESTIONS AND CONJECTURE.
3759: 
3760: 
3761: 
3762: 
3763:     The main open problem is to understand the almost sure Lebesgue decomposition
3764: of the operator H<∞> . Let us be more precise. Consider a typical blow-up ω, in
3765: particular for which ∂F<∞> = ∅. The Hilbert space D<∞> can be decomposed into
3766: three parts Hac , Hsc , Hpp such that the restriction of H<∞> to these subspaces is
3767: respectively absolutely continuous, singular continuous or purely punctual (i.e. this
3768: means that the spectral measure of any function in these subspaces is resp. absolutely
3769: continuous, singular continuous or purely punctual). The spectrum of H<∞> and
3770: the spectrum of the restriction of H<∞> to Hac , Hsc , Hpp are respectively denoted
3771: by Σ(ω), Σac (ω), Σsc (ω), Σpp (ω) (they a priori depend on ω). Proposition 2 of [40]
3772: states that these sets are almost surely constant in ω, i.e. equal to deterministic sets
3773: Σ, Σac , Σsc , Σpp for almost all blow-up ω.
3774: One can be more precise and split the Hilbert space Hpp into two parts: the first ,that
...
3786: corresponding to the different components of the spectrum by Σ· = suppµ· . The
3787: question is whether it is possible to compute these measures from some characteristics
3788: of the map R, as we computed µ and µN D from its Green function and the asymptotic
3789: multiplicities of its zeroes. In particular, it would be interesting to understand at
3790: which condition there may be a pure point component Σ̃pp not created by the Neuman-
3791: Dirichlet eigenfunctions (i.e. at which conditions there are L2 eigenfunctions which
3792: 
3793: 78               CHAPITRE 6. REMARKS, QUESTIONS AND CONJECTURE.
3794: 
3795: 
3796: 
3797: 
3798: are not generated by compactly supported eigenfunctions). But all these questions
3799: seem to be very difficult.
3800:     Let us now make few remarks and a conjecture. When N > d∞ we know that
3801: the spectrum is pure point with compactly supported eigenfunctions, i.e. that Σac =
3802: Σsc = Σ̃pp = ∅. Consider now the case where N = d∞ . We showed in this text that
3803: the N-D spectrum is related to the zeroes of R, i.e. to the indeterminacy points of g.
3804: Considering that eigenfunctions of H<∞> which are not with compact support are in
3805: some sense approximated by functions with compact support which are not far from
3806: being eigenfunctions we can ask the following very imprecise question: does the exis-
...
3803: the N-D spectrum is related to the zeroes of R, i.e. to the indeterminacy points of g.
3804: Considering that eigenfunctions of H<∞> which are not with compact support are in
3805: some sense approximated by functions with compact support which are not far from
3806: being eigenfunctions we can ask the following very imprecise question: does the exis-
3807: tence of a pure point component in the spectrum different from the N-D component,
3808: i.e. Σ̃pp 6= ∅ imply that the iterates of g on φ(λ) approach the indeterminacy points,
3809: in a sense to be made precise. This leads to propose the following conjecture.
3810: Conjecture 6.1. — Consider the case d∞ = N . Assume that the condition of propo-
3811: sition 4.5 is satisfied for all λ ∈ R, i.e. that for any λ ∈ R there exists two open subsets
3812: U ⊂ C and V ⊂ LG such that λ ∈ U , ∪∞                           n
3813:                                              n=1 Ign ⊂ V and g (φ(U )) ∩ V = ∅. Is it true
3814: that in this case Σpp = ∅?
3815: Remark 6.1 : It is already known from theorem 3.1 that under these conditions
3816: ΣN D = ∅.
3817: 
3818:    One of the main problem is the lack of examples where computations are possible.
3819: There are very few examples where the spectral type of the operator can be analyzed.
3820: The case of the Sierpinski gasket is now well understood and computations in this
3821: example are easy. In this case N > d∞ and so the Neumann-Dirichlet eigenfunctions
3822: are complete. The case of nested fractal is also understood, in this case also N > d∞ .
3823: We present the spectral analysis of the self-similar Sturm-Liouville operator on the
...
3821: example are easy. In this case N > d∞ and so the Neumann-Dirichlet eigenfunctions
3822: are complete. The case of nested fractal is also understood, in this case also N > d∞ .
3823: We present the spectral analysis of the self-similar Sturm-Liouville operator on the
3824: real line in [41]. In this case we can prove that Σpp = ∅.
3825:    But we have no example where Σ̃pp 6= ∅, i.e. where there is a pure point component
3826: not induced by the N-D spectrum. We think it could be interesting to understand the
3827: following situation (at least by numerical computations), which could be a candidate
3828: for Σ̃pp 6= ∅. Consider the Sierpinski gasket and take G = {Id}, the trivial group as
3829: group of symmetries of the picture. In this case the renormalization map T is defined
3830: on the bigger space SymF of symmetric matrices on F = {1, 2, 3}. The subvariety
3831: LG is the Lagrangian Grassmanian L3 . There is then no reason that N > d∞ . We
3832: would bet, on the contrary, that N = d∞ in this case. The question is the following:
3833: what happens if we take for the initial operator A<0> and initial measure b<0> a small
3834: perturbation of the usual discrete Laplace operator and of the uniform measure? Does
3835: the spectrum remains pure point or not?

4087: B. Dynamics of rational maps on the projective space Pk
4088: B.1. Definitions, indeterminacy points. — Let Pk be the complex projective
4089: space of dimension k and π : Ck+1 \ {0} → Pk the canonical projection. A point z
4090: in Pk can be represented in homogeneous coordinates by
4091:                                     z = [z0 : · · · : zk ],
4092: 
4093: 84                                     APPENDIX
4094: 
4095: 
4096: 
4097: 
4098: where [z0 : · · · : zk ] denotes the point π(z0 , . . . , zk ). Consider now a homogeneous
4099: polynomial map R : Ck+1 → Ck+1 of degree d, i.e. R = (R0 , . . . , Rk ) where the Ri ’s
4100: are homogeneous polynomials of degree d in the variables (z0 , . . . , zk ). Suppose that
4101: the Ri have no common factor. It is natural to associate a map f on the projective
4102: space Pk such that the following diagram commutes:
4103:                                              R
4104:                                   Ck+1 −−−−→ Ck+1
...
4107:                                    y          y
4108:                                               f
4109:                                       Pk −−−−→ Pk
4110: The map f can be defined only on the set Pk \ I where I = π{z, R(z) = 0}. The set I
4111: is called the set of indeterminacy points of f and is an analytic subset of codimension
4112: at least 2 (indeed if all the Ri ’s vanish on an analytic hypersurface then a polynomial
4113: can be factorized in R). Usually one writes f in homogeneous coordinates as
4114:                                    f = [R0 , . . . , Rk ].
4115: The function f is a rational map of Pk (a precise meaning to this notion is given at
4116: the beginning of appendix C) and the polynomial map R, which is called the lift of f
4117: to Ck+1 , exists and is unique (up to a multiplicative constant) for any rational map
4118: of Pk . The degree of f is defined as the degree of R, i.e. d. The map f is holomorphic
4119: on Pk if and only if I = ∅. The map f is said to be dominating if its differential
4120: is generically surjective. If f is dominating and if S is a positive closed current of
4121: bidegree (1, 1) with potential u on Ck+1 we define the pull-back f ∗ S as the current
4122: with potential u ◦ R.
4123: 
4124:   At a point p ∈ I the image of f can be defined as a subset of Pk : let Bp be the
4125: subset of Pk defined by
4126:                             Bp = ∩ǫ>0 f (B(p, ǫ) \ I)
4127: where B(p, ǫ) is the ball of center p and radius ǫ. Then Bp is an analytic subset
...
4130: of dimension p can be sent into a subvariety of dimension strictly smaller. These
4131: phenomenons are new compared to the situation of 1-dimensional complex dynamics.
4132: This results in the fact that the degree of the iterates of f do not necessarily grow
4133: like dn . Let us explain this clearly: suppose, for example, that a hypersurface V is
4134: sent by f on a point of indeterminacy. Then the map R2 is null on V and a hence a
4135: polynomial can be factorized in R2 . The degree of f 2 is then smaller than degree(f )2
4136: since f 2 is lifted to a polynomial map with degree smaller than that of R2 . In general,
4137: we can always write
4138:                                       Rn = hn Rn ,
4139: where hn is a homogeneous polynomial and Rn a homogeneous polynomial map, with
4140: no common factor, of degree dn = dn − deg(hn ). The map Rn is a reduced lift of
4141: f n , thus the degree of f n is dn . We say that there is decreaseness in the degree if
4142: dn < dn .

4242:       C. ITERATION OF MEROMORPHIC MAPS ON COMPACT COMPLEX MANIFOLDS                     87

4247: C. Iteration of meromorphic maps on compact complex manifolds
4248: C.1. Definitions, indeterminacy points. — Let X and Y be compact complex
4249: manifolds. Denote by π1 : X × Y → X and π2 : X × Y → Y the projection on
4250: the first and second components of X × Y . A meromorphic function f : X → Y is
4251: defined by its graph Γf ⊂ X × Y , an irreducible subvariety of X × Y for which the
4252: first projection is a proper modification, i.e. such that there exists a proper subvariety

4253: V ⊂ X such that π1 is a biholomorphism from Γf \ π1−1 (V ) to X \ V . We denote

4270: Definition A.1. — A dominating meromorphic map f : X → X is said to be
4271: analytically stable if Γf ◦ Γf n = Γf n+1 , i.e. there does not exist an hypersurface
4272: V ⊂ X and an integer n such that f n (V ) ⊂ If .

30:                                       three types of Grassmannians: complex Grassmannians, Lagrangian Grassmannians,
31:                                       orthogonal Grassmannians). We relate some characteristics of the dynamics of its
32:                                       iterates with some characteristics of the spectrum of our operator. More specifically,
33:                                       we give an explicit formula for the density of states in terms of the Green current
34:                                       of the map, and we relate the indeterminacy points of the map with the so-called
35:                                       Neuman-Dirichlet eigenvalues which lead to eigenfunctions with compact support on
36:                                       the unbounded lattice. Depending on the asymptotic degree of the map we can prove
37:                                       drastically different spectral properties of the operators. Our formalism is valid for
38:                                       the general class of finitely ramified self-similar sets (i.e. for the class of pcf self-similar
39:                                       sets of Kigami, cf [25]). Hence, this work aims at a generalization and a better under-
40:                                       standing of the initial work of the physicists Rammal and Toulouse on the Sierpinski
41:                                       gasket (cf [35], [34]).

193: the generalization, this brings new understanding of the models. In particular, the
194: renormalization map involved is now multidimensional and certain notions which are
195: specific to the dynamics in higher dimension and which were hidden in the case of the
196: Sierpinski gasket (where the renormalization map involved was 1-dimensional), such
197: as the notion of indeterminacy points (which corresponds to the singularities of the
198: map), the degree of the iterates, enter the discussion and play an important role. In
199: comparison with our previous work, [38], the main progress that allows us to handle
200: the general case is the construction of a new renormalization map. This renormal-
201: ization map is a rational map defined on some compact Kähler manifold. It is of the
202: type of the maps considered in [13], [12], and our techniques rely heavily on recent
203: works of Fornaess Sibony, Diller Favre, Guedj (cf [45], [16], [13], [12], [14]) on the
204: dynamics of rational maps in higher dimensions. It is interesting to note that many
205: of the key notions in this field (such as the degrees of the iterates, the indeterminacy
206: points, the Green current) find a significance related to the spectral properties of our
207: operators. In particular, we are able to give an explicit expression for the density of
208: states in terms of the Green current of the map and we prove that the molecular states
209: of Rammal (called Neuman-Dirichlet eigenvalues in the text) correspond exactly to
210: the indeterminacy points of the map.

410:    There are two renormalization maps, closely related, which play an important role.
411: We do not define precisely these maps in this introduction, we just describe some
412: of their properties. The first one, denoted by T , is defined on the space SymG , the
413: space of G-invariant symmetric operators on CF , and is a rational map (i.e. T Q,

518: and µN D . With the iterates of g we can associate an asymptotic degree d∞ (called
519: the dynamical degree, cf section 4.3) and we show in theorem (4.1) that the following
520: dichotomy holds:
521:     – If d∞ < N , then S is a countable sum of currents of integration on hypersurfaces
522:       of LG . In this case µN D = µ, thus the spectrum is pure point with compactly
523:       supported eigenfunctions, almost surely in ω. (We also show that the number
524:                                           +       ND
525:       of Neuman only eigenvalues, i.e. |ν<n>   − ν<n>  |, grows like nd∞ ).
526:     – If d∞ = N , then the current S does not charge hypersurfaces; it is the Green
527:       current of the map g. In particular, it is null on the Fatou set of g. Moreover,
528:       generically (in a sense made precise in theorem (4.1)), we have µN D = 0, i.e.
529:       there does not exist N-D eigenfunctions.
530: Note that a similar dichotomy theorem was shown in [20] for the particular class of
531: decimable fractals, for which there exists a 1-dimensional renormalization map that
532: relate the spectrum on different scales (but the relation with the N-D spectrum was
533: not made).
534: 
535:    Let us now make some remarks. The renormalization map we consider here is
536: not the same as the one considered in our previous work [38]. The introduction of
537: this new map is the key point that allows us to handle the case of lattices based on
538: general finitely ramified self-similar sets. In particular, this map is not defined on a
...
538: general finitely ramified self-similar sets. In particular, this map is not defined on a
539: projective space. This induces several difficulties, for example, the notion of degree is
540: more complicated and is related to the action of the map on some cohomology groups.
541: But there are several facts that seem to indicate that the map we consider is the good
542: renormalization map: the first one is that the indeterminacy points of g, which have
543: a crucial influence on the dynamical properties of g, have a clear meaning in terms
544: of the Neuman-Dirichlet eigenvalues of our operator. The second is that the map g
545: behaves well in the non-degenerate case, i.e. when d∞ = N : to be more precise, the
546: map g is algebraically stable (cf definition in appendix), and this allows us to define
547: the Green current; this is not the case for other maps we could consider (cf section
548: 4.5 where we compare different renormalization maps) and which are birationally
549: equivalent to g. Finally, let us mention that considering expressions like exp(ηQη)
550: in the Grassman algebra is very natural in the context of supersymmetry, and that
551:

3207: 4.4. Regularity of the density of states
3208:   We first state a conjecture and show how it can be related to results on Lelong
3209: numbers of the Green current.
3210: 
3211: Conjecture 4.1. — The measure µ − µN D is continuous, i.e. it does not charge any
3212: point.

3219:    We introduce the notion of Lelong numbers of a psh function. Let u be a psh
3220: function in a neighborhood U ⊂ Cn of 0. Then the Lelong number of u at 0 is defined
3221: by
3222:                   ν(u, 0) = max{c ≥ 0 s.t. u(z) ≤ c log kzk + O(1)}.
3223: The Lelong number of a positive closed (1, 1) current T is defined by ν(T, p) = ν(u, p)
3224: for any local psh potential u of T , T = ddc u. On C it is easy to see that the Lelong
...
3228: that d∞ = N (otherwise µ − µN D = 0). We remark that the Lelong number of
3229: ddc ln kRn ◦ φk at λ is the order of vanishing ord(Rn ◦ φ, λ). By proposition (2.3), ii),
3230:                                                       ND
3231: we know that this order of vanishing is equal to ν<n>    ({λ}). Hence, we see that the
3232: conjecture is equivalent to
3233:                         1
3234:                     lim    ν(ddc ln kRn ◦ φk, λ) = ν(ddc G ◦ φ, λ).
3235:                    n→∞ N n
3236: 
3237: Since the current S = ddc G is the limit of the sequence of currents N1n Sn with po-
3238: tential N1n ddc ln kRn k the question is equivalent to know whether the limit of the
3239: Lelong numbers of the restriction of the currents Sn to a curve is equal to the Lelong
3240: numbers of the restriction of the limit S. There is, at the present time, only one result
3241: on Lelong numbers of Green currents: it says, cf [13] theorem 2.4.6, that the Lelong
3242: numbers of the Green current are null on the complement of the indeterminacy points
3243: of the iterates: with our notations it means that the Lelong number of S is null when
3244: the Lelong numbers of the Sn are null. Unfortunately, it cannot be applied easily
3245: to our case. Firstly, because it does not state that the Lelong numbers of S is the
3246: limit of the Lelong numbers of N1n Sn at any point, and secondly, because we need
3247: information on the restriction of the Green current of S to the curve φ(λ), which can
3248: be strictly bigger than the Lelong number of S. Nevertheless, it could certainly be
...
3246: limit of the Lelong numbers of N1n Sn at any point, and secondly, because we need
3247: information on the restriction of the Green current of S to the curve φ(λ), which can
3248: be strictly bigger than the Lelong number of S. Nevertheless, it could certainly be
3249: possible, with a little more work, to prove a result in the generic case, but we prefer
3250: to leave the general result as a conjecture.

3790:         REMARKS, QUESTIONS AND CONJECTURE.
3791:

3794: 
3795:     The main open problem is to understand the almost sure Lebesgue decomposition
3796: of the operator H<∞> . Let us be more precise. Consider a typical blow-up ω, in
3797: particular for which ∂F<∞> = ∅. The Hilbert space D<∞> can be decomposed into
3798: three parts Hac , Hsc , Hpp such that the restriction of H<∞> to these subspaces is
3799: respectively absolutely continuous, singular continuous or purely punctual (i.e. this
3800: means that the spectral measure of any function in these subspaces is resp. absolutely
3801: continuous, singular continuous or purely punctual). The spectrum of H<∞> and
3802: the spectrum of the restriction of H<∞> to Hac , Hsc , Hpp are respectively denoted
3803: by Σ(ω), Σac (ω), Σsc (ω), Σpp (ω) (they a priori depend on ω). Proposition 2 of [40]
3804: states that these sets are almost surely constant in ω, i.e. equal to deterministic sets
3805: Σ, Σac , Σsc , Σpp for almost all blow-up ω.
3806: One can be more precise and split the Hilbert space Hpp into two parts: the first ,that
...
3818: corresponding to the different components of the spectrum by Σ· = suppµ· . The
3819: question is whether it is possible to compute these measures from some characteristics
3820: of the map R, as we computed µ and µN D from its Green function and the asymptotic
3821: multiplicities of its zeroes. In particular, it would be interesting to understand at
3822: which condition there may be a pure point component Σ̃pp not created by the Neuman-
3823: Dirichlet eigenfunctions (i.e. at which conditions there are L2 eigenfunctions which
3824: 
3825: 78               CHAPITRE 6. REMARKS, QUESTIONS AND CONJECTURE.
3826: 
3827: 
3828: 
3829: 
3830: are not generated by compactly supported eigenfunctions). But all these questions
3831: seem to be very difficult.
3832:     Let us now make few remarks and a conjecture. When N > d∞ we know that
3833: the spectrum is pure point with compactly supported eigenfunctions, i.e. that Σac =
3834: Σsc = Σ̃pp = ∅. Consider now the case where N = d∞ . We showed in this text that
3835: the N-D spectrum is related to the zeroes of R, i.e. to the indeterminacy points of g.
3836: Considering that eigenfunctions of H<∞> which are not with compact support are in
3837: some sense approximated by functions with compact support which are not far from
3838: being eigenfunctions we can ask the following very imprecise question: does the exis-
...
3835: the N-D spectrum is related to the zeroes of R, i.e. to the indeterminacy points of g.
3836: Considering that eigenfunctions of H<∞> which are not with compact support are in
3837: some sense approximated by functions with compact support which are not far from
3838: being eigenfunctions we can ask the following very imprecise question: does the exis-
3839: tence of a pure point component in the spectrum different from the N-D component,
3840: i.e. Σ̃pp 6= ∅ imply that the iterates of g on φ(λ) approach the indeterminacy points,
3841: in a sense to be made precise. This leads to propose the following conjecture.
3842: Conjecture 6.1. — Consider the case d∞ = N . Assume that the condition of propo-
3843: sition 4.5 is satisfied for all λ ∈ R, i.e. that for any λ ∈ R there exists two open subsets
3844: U ⊂ C and V ⊂ LG such that λ ∈ U , ∪∞                           n
3845:                                              n=1 Ign ⊂ V and g (φ(U )) ∩ V = ∅. Is it true
3846: that in this case Σpp = ∅?
3847: Remark 6.1 : It is already known from theorem 3.1 that under these conditions
3848: ΣN D = ∅.
3849: 
3850:    One of the main problem is the lack of examples where computations are possible.
3851: There are very few examples where the spectral type of the operator can be analyzed.
3852: The case of the Sierpinski gasket is now well understood and computations in this
3853: example are easy. In this case N > d∞ and so the Neumann-Dirichlet eigenfunctions
3854: are complete. The case of nested fractal is also understood, in this case also N > d∞ .
3855: We present the spectral analysis of the self-similar Sturm-Liouville operator on the
...
3853: example are easy. In this case N > d∞ and so the Neumann-Dirichlet eigenfunctions
3854: are complete. The case of nested fractal is also understood, in this case also N > d∞ .
3855: We present the spectral analysis of the self-similar Sturm-Liouville operator on the
3856: real line in [41]. In this case we can prove that Σpp = ∅.
3857:    But we have no example where Σ̃pp 6= ∅, i.e. where there is a pure point component
3858: not induced by the N-D spectrum. We think it could be interesting to understand the
3859: following situation (at least by numerical computations), which could be a candidate
3860: for Σ̃pp 6= ∅. Consider the Sierpinski gasket and take G = {Id}, the trivial group as
3861: group of symmetries of the picture. In this case the renormalization map T is defined
3862: on the bigger space SymF of symmetric matrices on F = {1, 2, 3}. The subvariety
3863: LG is the Lagrangian Grassmanian L3 . There is then no reason that N > d∞ . We
3864: would bet, on the contrary, that N = d∞ in this case. The question is the following:
3865: what happens if we take for the initial operator A<0> and initial measure b<0> a small
3866: perturbation of the usual discrete Laplace operator and of the uniform measure? Does
3867: the spectrum remains pure point or not?

4119: B. Dynamics of rational maps on the projective space Pk
4120: B.1. Definitions, indeterminacy points. — Let Pk be the complex projective
4121: space of dimension k and π : Ck+1 \ {0} → Pk the canonical projection. A point z
4122: in Pk can be represented in homogeneous coordinates by
4123:                                     z = [z0 : · · · : zk ],
4124: 
4125: 84                                     APPENDIX
4126: 
4127: 
4128: 
4129: 
4130: where [z0 : · · · : zk ] denotes the point π(z0 , . . . , zk ). Consider now a homogeneous
4131: polynomial map R : Ck+1 → Ck+1 of degree d, i.e. R = (R0 , . . . , Rk ) where the Ri ’s
4132: are homogeneous polynomials of degree d in the variables (z0 , . . . , zk ). Suppose that
4133: the Ri have no common factor. It is natural to associate a map f on the projective
4134: space Pk such that the following diagram commutes:
4135:                                              R
4136:                                   Ck+1 −−−−→ Ck+1
...
4139:                                    y          y
4140:                                               f
4141:                                       Pk −−−−→ Pk
4142: The map f can be defined only on the set Pk \ I where I = π{z, R(z) = 0}. The set I
4143: is called the set of indeterminacy points of f and is an analytic subset of codimension
4144: at least 2 (indeed if all the Ri ’s vanish on an analytic hypersurface then a polynomial
4145: can be factorized in R). Usually one writes f in homogeneous coordinates as
4146:                                    f = [R0 , . . . , Rk ].
4147: The function f is a rational map of Pk (a precise meaning to this notion is given at
4148: the beginning of appendix C) and the polynomial map R, which is called the lift of f
4149: to Ck+1 , exists and is unique (up to a multiplicative constant) for any rational map
4150: of Pk . The degree of f is defined as the degree of R, i.e. d. The map f is holomorphic
4151: on Pk if and only if I = ∅. The map f is said to be dominating if its differential
4152: is generically surjective. If f is dominating and if S is a positive closed current of
4153: bidegree (1, 1) with potential u on Ck+1 we define the pull-back f ∗ S as the current
4154: with potential u ◦ R.
4155: 
4156:   At a point p ∈ I the image of f can be defined as a subset of Pk : let Bp be the
4157: subset of Pk defined by
4158:                             Bp = ∩ǫ>0 f (B(p, ǫ) \ I)
4159: where B(p, ǫ) is the ball of center p and radius ǫ. Then Bp is an analytic subset
...
4162: of dimension p can be sent into a subvariety of dimension strictly smaller. These
4163: phenomenons are new compared to the situation of 1-dimensional complex dynamics.
4164: This results in the fact that the degree of the iterates of f do not necessarily grow
4165: like dn . Let us explain this clearly: suppose, for example, that a hypersurface V is
4166: sent by f on a point of indeterminacy. Then the map R2 is null on V and a hence a
4167: polynomial can be factorized in R2 . The degree of f 2 is then smaller than degree(f )2
4168: since f 2 is lifted to a polynomial map with degree smaller than that of R2 . In general,
4169: we can always write
4170:                                       Rn = hn Rn ,
4171: where hn is a homogeneous polynomial and Rn a homogeneous polynomial map, with
4172: no common factor, of degree dn = dn − deg(hn ). The map Rn is a reduced lift of
4173: f n , thus the degree of f n is dn . We say that there is decreaseness in the degree if
4174: dn < dn .

4279: C. Iteration of meromorphic maps on compact complex manifolds
4280: C.1. Definitions, indeterminacy points. — Let X and Y be compact complex
4281: manifolds. Denote by π1 : X × Y → X and π2 : X × Y → Y the projection on
4282: the first and second components of X × Y . A meromorphic function f : X → Y is
4283: defined by its graph Γf ⊂ X × Y , an irreducible subvariety of X × Y for which the
4284: first projection is a proper modification, i.e. such that there exists a proper subvariety
4285: V ⊂ X such that π1 is a biholomorphism from Γf \ π1−1 (V ) to X \ V . We denote
4286: by If ⊂ X the set of points of indeterminacy, i.e. the set of points where π1 has no
4287: local inverse. The set If is an analytic subset of codimension at least 2. Of course,
4288: at a point x ∈ X \ If the image by f is defined by f (x) = π2 (π1−1 (x)). When x is an
...
4285: V ⊂ X such that π1 is a biholomorphism from Γf \ π1−1 (V ) to X \ V . We denote
4286: by If ⊂ X the set of points of indeterminacy, i.e. the set of points where π1 has no
4287: local inverse. The set If is an analytic subset of codimension at least 2. Of course,
4288: at a point x ∈ X \ If the image by f is defined by f (x) = π2 (π1−1 (x)). When x is an
4289: indeterminacy point the image by f is an analytic set defined by f (x) = π2 (π1−1 ({x})).
4290: The map f is holomorphic on X \ If . The map is said to be dominating if π2 is
4291: surjective.
4292:     If g : Y → Z is another meromorphic map then the graph Γg◦f is defined as the
4293: closure:
4294:                     Γg◦f = {(x, g(f (x))), x ∈ X \ If , f (x) ∈ Y \ Ig }
4295: One can also consider the graph
4296:           Γg ◦ Γf = {(x, z), ∃y ∈ Y such that (x, y) ∈ Γf and (y, z) ∈ Γg }.

4299: isfied if and only if Γg ◦ Γf is irreducible and this is true if and only if there is no
4300: hypersurface V ⊂ X such that f (V ) ⊂ Ig .

4303: analytically stable if Γf ◦ Γf n = Γf n+1 , i.e. there does not exist an hypersurface
4304: V ⊂ X and an integer n such that f n (V ) ⊂ If .

31:                                       orthogonal Grassmannians). We relate some characteristics of the dynamics of its
32:                                       iterates with some characteristics of the spectrum of our operator. More specifically,
33:                                       we give an explicit formula for the density of states in terms of the Green current

38:                                       for the general class of finitely ramified self-similar sets (i.e. for the class of pcf self-

40:                                       better understanding of the initial work of the physicists Rammal and Toulouse on
41:                                       the Sierpinski gasket (cf [35], [34]).

193: the generalization, this brings new understanding of the models. In particular, the
194: renormalization map involved is now multidimensional and certain notions which are
195: specific to the dynamics in higher dimension and which were hidden in the case of the
196: Sierpinski gasket (where the renormalization map involved was 1-dimensional), such
197: as the notion of indeterminacy points (which corresponds to the singularities of the
198: map), the degree of the iterates, enter the discussion and play an important role. In
199: comparison with our previous work, [38], the main progress that allows us to handle
200: the general case is the construction of a new renormalization map. This renormal-
201: ization map is a rational map defined on some compact Kähler manifold. It is of the
202: type of the maps considered in [13], [12], and our techniques rely heavily on recent
203: works of Fornaess Sibony, Diller Favre, Guedj (cf [45], [16], [13], [12], [14]) on the
204: dynamics of rational maps in higher dimensions. It is interesting to note that many
205: of the key notions in this field (such as the degrees of the iterates, the indeterminacy
206: points, the Green current) find a significance related to the spectral properties of our
207: operators. In particular, we are able to give an explicit expression for the density of
208: states in terms of the Green current of the map and we prove that the molecular states
209: of Rammal (called Neumann-Dirichlet eigenvalues in the text) correspond exactly to
210: the indeterminacy points of the map.

400: ω (actually, our result is more precise than this). Finally, we showed that when the
401: density of states is completely created by the N-D eigenvalues, i.e. when µN D = µ,
402:                           ±
403: then the spectrum of H<∞>       is pure point with compactly supported eigenfunctions,
404: almost surely in ω.
405:    So, the measures µ and µN D give important information on the spectral properties
406:                     ±
407: of the operators H<∞>    . Hence, these two measures deserve to be understood and our
408: aim in this text is to describe the relations that exist between these measures and the
409: dynamics of a certain renormalization map that we construct.
410:    There are two renormalization maps, closely related, which play an important role.
411: We do not define precisely these maps in this introduction, we just describe some
412: of their properties. The first one, denoted by T , is defined on the space SymG , the
413: space of G-invariant symmetric operators on CF , and is a rational map (i.e. T Q,
414: for Q in SymG , is rational in the coefficients of Q). For example, in the case of the
415: Sierpinski gasket SymG ∼ C2 : indeed, if W0 is the space of constant functions on F
416: and W1 its orthogonal complement (for the natural scalar product on CF ), then any
417: element of SymG can be written thanks to 2 coordinates (u0 , u1 ) ∈ C2 under the form
418: u0 p|W0 + u1 p|W1 where p|W0 and p|W1 are the orthogonal projections respectively on
419: W0 and W1 . We do not define explicitly the map T in this introduction (cf section 2
...
518: and µN D . With the iterates of g we can associate an asymptotic degree d∞ (called
519: the dynamical degree, cf section 4.3) and we show in theorem (4.1) that the following
520: dichotomy holds:
521:     – If d∞ < N , then S is a countable sum of currents of integration on hypersurfaces
522:       of LG . In this case µN D = µ, thus the spectrum is pure point with compactly
523:       supported eigenfunctions, almost surely in ω. (We also show that the number
524:                                            +        ND
525:       of Neumann only eigenvalues, i.e. |ν<n>   − ν<n>  |, grows like nd∞ ).
526:     – If d∞ = N , then the current S does not charge hypersurfaces; it is the Green
527:       current of the map g. In particular, it is null on the Fatou set of g. Moreover,
528:       generically (in a sense made precise in theorem (4.1)), we have µN D = 0, i.e.
529:       there does not exist N-D eigenfunctions.
530: Note that a similar dichotomy theorem was shown in [20] for the particular class of
531: decimable fractals, for which there exists a 1-dimensional renormalization map that
532: relate the spectrum on different scales (but the relation with the N-D spectrum was
533: not made).
534: 
535:    Let us now make some remarks. The renormalization map we consider here is
536: not the same as the one considered in our previous work [38]. The introduction of
537: this new map is the key point that allows us to handle the case of lattices based on
538: general finitely ramified self-similar sets. In particular, this map is not defined on a
...
538: general finitely ramified self-similar sets. In particular, this map is not defined on a
539: projective space. This induces several difficulties, for example, the notion of degree is
540: more complicated and is related to the action of the map on some cohomology groups.
541: But there are several facts that seem to indicate that the map we consider is the good
542: renormalization map: the first one is that the indeterminacy points of g, which have
543: a crucial influence on the dynamical properties of g, have a clear meaning in terms
544: of the Neumann-Dirichlet eigenvalues of our operator. The second is that the map g
545: behaves well in the non-degenerate case, i.e. when d∞ = N : to be more precise, the
546: map g is algebraically stable (cf definition in appendix), and this allows us to define
547: the Green current; this is not the case for other maps we could consider (cf section
548: 4.5 where we compare different renormalization maps) and which are birationally
549: equivalent to g. Finally, let us mention that considering expressions like exp(ηQη)
550: in the Grassman algebra is very natural in the context of supersymmetry, and that
551:

3237: Since the current S = ddc G is the limit of the sequence of currents N1n Sn with po-
3238: tential N1n ddc ln kRn k the question is equivalent to know whether the limit of the
3239: Lelong numbers of the restriction of the currents Sn to a curve is equal to the Lelong
3240: numbers of the restriction of the limit S. There is, at the present time, only one result
3241: on Lelong numbers of Green currents: it says, cf [13] theorem 2.4.6, that the Lelong
3242: numbers of the Green current are null on the complement of the indeterminacy points
3243: of the iterates: with our notations it means that the Lelong number of S is null when
3244: the Lelong numbers of the Sn are null. Unfortunately, it cannot be applied easily
3245: to our case. Firstly, because it does not state that the Lelong numbers of S is the
3246: limit of the Lelong numbers of N1n Sn at any point, and secondly, because we need
3247: information on the restriction of the Green current of S to the curve φ(λ), which can
3248: be strictly bigger than the Lelong number of S. Nevertheless, it could certainly be
...
3246: limit of the Lelong numbers of N1n Sn at any point, and secondly, because we need
3247: information on the restriction of the Green current of S to the curve φ(λ), which can
3248: be strictly bigger than the Lelong number of S. Nevertheless, it could certainly be
3249: possible, with a little more work, to prove a result in the generic case, but we prefer
3250: to leave the general result as a conjecture.
