3795:     The main open problem is to understand the almost sure Lebesgue decomposition
3796: of the operator H<∞> . Let us be more precise. Consider a typical blow-up ω, in
3797: particular for which ∂F<∞> = ∅. The Hilbert space D<∞> can be decomposed into
3798: three parts Hac , Hsc , Hpp such that the restriction of H<∞> to these subspaces is
3799: respectively absolutely continuous, singular continuous or purely punctual (i.e. this
3800: means that the spectral measure of any function in these subspaces is resp. abso-
3801: lutely continuous, singular continuous or purely punctual). The spectrum of H<∞>
3802: and the spectrum of the restriction of H<∞> to Hac , Hsc , Hpp are respectively de-
3803: noted by Σ(ω), Σac (ω), Σsc (ω), Σpp (ω) (they a priori depend on ω). Proposition 2 of
3804: [40] states that these sets are almost surely constant in ω, i.e. equal to deterministic
3805: sets Σ, Σac , Σsc , Σpp for almost all blow-up ω.
3806: One can be more precise and split the Hilbert space Hpp into two parts: the first ,that
...
3818: eral parts corresponding to the different components of the spectrum by Σ· = suppµ· .
3819: The question is whether it is possible to compute these measures from some char-
3820: acteristics of the map R, as we computed µ and µN D from its Green function and
3821: the asymptotic multiplicities of its zeroes. In particular, it would be interesting to
3822: understand at which condition there may be a pure point component Σ̃pp not cre-
3823: ated by the Neumann-Dirichlet eigenfunctions (i.e. at which conditions there are L2
3824: 
3825: 78               CHAPITRE 6. REMARKS, QUESTIONS AND CONJECTURE.
3826: 
3827: 
3828: 
3829: 
3830: eigenfunctions which are not generated by compactly supported eigenfunctions). But
3831: all these questions seem to be very difficult.
3832:     Let us now make few remarks and a conjecture. When N > d∞ we know that
3833: the spectrum is pure point with compactly supported eigenfunctions, i.e. that Σac =
3834: Σsc = Σ̃pp = ∅. Consider now the case where N = d∞ . We showed in this text that
3835: the N-D spectrum is related to the zeroes of R, i.e. to the indeterminacy points of g.
3836: Considering that eigenfunctions of H<∞> which are not with compact support are in
3837: some sense approximated by functions with compact support which are not far from
3838: being eigenfunctions we can ask the following very imprecise question: does the exis-
...
3835: the N-D spectrum is related to the zeroes of R, i.e. to the indeterminacy points of g.
3836: Considering that eigenfunctions of H<∞> which are not with compact support are in
3837: some sense approximated by functions with compact support which are not far from
3838: being eigenfunctions we can ask the following very imprecise question: does the exis-
3839: tence of a pure point component in the spectrum different from the N-D component,
3840: i.e. Σ̃pp 6= ∅ imply that the iterates of g on φ(λ) approach the indeterminacy points,
3841: in a sense to be made precise. This leads to propose the following conjecture.
3842: Conjecture 6.1. — Consider the case d∞ = N . Assume that the condition of propo-
3843: sition 4.5 is satisfied for all λ ∈ R, i.e. that for any λ ∈ R there exists two open subsets
3844: U ⊂ C and V ⊂ LG such that λ ∈ U , ∪∞                           n
3845:                                              n=1 Ign ⊂ V and g (φ(U )) ∩ V = ∅. Is it true
3846: that in this case Σpp = ∅?
3847: Remark 6.1 : It is already known from theorem 3.1 that under these conditions
3848: ΣN D = ∅.
3849: 
3850:    One of the main problem is the lack of examples where computations are possible.
3851: There are very few examples where the spectral type of the operator can be analyzed.
3852: The case of the Sierpinski gasket is now well understood and computations in this
3853: example are easy. In this case N > d∞ and so the Neumann-Dirichlet eigenfunctions
3854: are complete. The case of nested fractal is also understood, in this case also N > d∞ .
3855: We present the spectral analysis of the self-similar Sturm-Liouville operator on the
...
3853: example are easy. In this case N > d∞ and so the Neumann-Dirichlet eigenfunctions
3854: are complete. The case of nested fractal is also understood, in this case also N > d∞ .
3855: We present the spectral analysis of the self-similar Sturm-Liouville operator on the
3856: real line in [41]. In this case we can prove that Σpp = ∅.
3857:    But we have no example where Σ̃pp 6= ∅, i.e. where there is a pure point component
3858: not induced by the N-D spectrum. We think it could be interesting to understand the
3859: following situation (at least by numerical computations), which could be a candidate
3860: for Σ̃pp 6= ∅. Consider the Sierpinski gasket and take G = {Id}, the trivial group as
3861: group of symmetries of the picture. In this case the renormalization map T is defined
3862: on the bigger space SymF of symmetric matrices on F = {1, 2, 3}. The subvariety
3863: LG is the Lagrangian Grassmanian L3 . There is then no reason that N > d∞ . We
3864: would bet, on the contrary, that N = d∞ in this case. The question is the following:
3865: what happens if we take for the initial operator A<0> and initial measure b<0> a small
3866: perturbation of the usual discrete Laplace operator and of the uniform measure? Does
3867: the spectrum remains pure point or not?

4132: are homogeneous polynomials of degree d in the variables (z0 , . . . , zk ). Suppose that

4134: space Pk such that the following diagram commutes:
4135:                                              R
4136:                                   Ck+1 −−−−→ Ck+1
...
4139:                                    y          y
4140:                                               f
4141:                                       Pk −−−−→ Pk
4142: The map f can be defined only on the set Pk \ I where I = π{z, R(z) = 0}. The set I
4143: is called the set of indeterminacy points of f and is an analytic subset of codimension
4144: at least 2 (indeed if all the Ri ’s vanish on an analytic hypersurface then a polynomial
4145: can be factorized in R). Usually one writes f in homogeneous coordinates as
4146:                                    f = [R0 , . . . , Rk ].
4147: The function f is a rational map of Pk (a precise meaning to this notion is given at
4148: the beginning of appendix C) and the polynomial map R, which is called the lift of f
4149: to Ck+1 , exists and is unique (up to a multiplicative constant) for any rational map
4150: of Pk . The degree of f is defined as the degree of R, i.e. d. The map f is holomorphic
4151: on Pk if and only if I = ∅. The map f is said to be dominating if its differential
4152: is generically surjective. If f is dominating and if S is a positive closed current of
4153: bidegree (1, 1) with potential u on Ck+1 we define the pull-back f ∗ S as the current
4154: with potential u ◦ R.
4155: 
4156:   At a point p ∈ I the image of f can be defined as a subset of Pk : let Bp be the
4157: subset of Pk defined by
4158:                             Bp = ∩ǫ>0 f (B(p, ǫ) \ I)
4159: where B(p, ǫ) is the ball of center p and radius ǫ. Then Bp is an analytic subset
...
4162: of dimension p can be sent into a subvariety of dimension strictly smaller. These
4163: phenomenons are new compared to the situation of 1-dimensional complex dynamics.
4164: This results in the fact that the degree of the iterates of f do not necessarily grow
4165: like dn . Let us explain this clearly: suppose, for example, that a hypersurface V is
4166: sent by f on a point of indeterminacy. Then the map R2 is null on V and a hence a
4167: polynomial can be factorized in R2 . The degree of f 2 is then smaller than degree(f )2
4168: since f 2 is lifted to a polynomial map with degree smaller than that of R2 . In general,
4169: we can always write
4170:                                       Rn = hn Rn ,
4171: where hn is a homogeneous polynomial and Rn a homogeneous polynomial map, with
4172: no common factor, of degree dn = dn − deg(hn ). The map Rn is a reduced lift of
4173: f n , thus the degree of f n is dn . We say that there is decreaseness in the degree if
4174: dn < dn .

4199: B.2. Green function and Green current. The algebraically stable case.—
4200: Let f be a rational map, algebraically stable, with degree d. We denote by In the set
4201: of indeterminacy points of f n , we have In ⊂ Im for n ≤ m, and we set I∞ = ∪∞   n=1 In .
4202: We recall the following definitions.
4203:     – A point p in Pk \ I∞ is in the Fatou set of f if there exists a neighborhood U of
4204:                      n
4205:       p such that f|U   is equicontinuous.
4206:     – The Julia set is the complement of the Fatou set.
4207:     – A point p is normal if there exist neighborhoods U of p and V of I such that
4208:       f n (U ) ∩ V = ∅ for all n. We denote by N the set of normal points.
4209: Denote by Gn : Ck+1 → R ∪ {−∞} the psh function
4210: (108)                    Gn (z) = log kRn (z)k, z ∈ Ck+1 .
4211: Remark that G0 is by definition the potential of the Fubini-Study form ω on Pk and
4212: that Gn is the potential of the pull-back Sn = (f n )∗ ω.

4279: C. Iteration of meromorphic maps on compact complex manifolds
4280: C.1. Definitions, indeterminacy points. — Let X and Y be compact complex
4281: manifolds. Denote by π1 : X × Y → X and π2 : X × Y → Y the projection on
4282: the first and second components of X × Y . A meromorphic function f : X → Y is
4283: defined by its graph Γf ⊂ X × Y , an irreducible subvariety of X × Y for which the
4284: first projection is a proper modification, i.e. such that there exists a proper subvariety
4285: V ⊂ X such that π1 is a biholomorphism from Γf \ π1−1 (V ) to X \ V . We denote
4286: by If ⊂ X the set of points of indeterminacy, i.e. the set of points where π1 has no
4287: local inverse. The set If is an analytic subset of codimension at least 2. Of course,
4288: at a point x ∈ X \ If the image by f is defined by f (x) = π2 (π1−1 (x)). When x is an
...
4285: V ⊂ X such that π1 is a biholomorphism from Γf \ π1−1 (V ) to X \ V . We denote
4286: by If ⊂ X the set of points of indeterminacy, i.e. the set of points where π1 has no
4287: local inverse. The set If is an analytic subset of codimension at least 2. Of course,
4288: at a point x ∈ X \ If the image by f is defined by f (x) = π2 (π1−1 (x)). When x is an
4289: indeterminacy point the image by f is an analytic set defined by f (x) = π2 (π1−1 ({x})).
4290: The map f is holomorphic on X \ If . The map is said to be dominating if π2 is
4291: surjective.
4292:     If g : Y → Z is another meromorphic map then the graph Γg◦f is defined as the
4293: closure:
4294:                     Γg◦f = {(x, g(f (x))), x ∈ X \ If , f (x) ∈ Y \ Ig }
4295: One can also consider the graph
4296:           Γg ◦ Γf = {(x, z), ∃y ∈ Y such that (x, y) ∈ Γf and (y, z) ∈ Γg }.
4297: 
4298: Proposition A.5. — (cf [12], proposition 1.5) The equality Γg◦f = Γg ◦ Γf is sat-
4299: isfied if and only if Γg ◦ Γf is irreducible and this is true if and only if there is no
4300: hypersurface V ⊂ X such that f (V ) ⊂ Ig .
4301: 
4302: Definition A.1. — A dominating meromorphic map f : X → X is said to be
4303: analytically stable if Γf ◦ Γf n = Γf n+1 , i.e. there does not exist an hypersurface
4304: V ⊂ X and an integer n such that f n (V ) ⊂ If .

121:     We can extend the counting function Hn for semi-magic squares in the following way. Define
122: a semi-magic hypercube (also called a quasi-magic hypercube) to be a d-dimensional n × · · · × n
123: array of nd nonnegative integers that sum to the same number t parallel to   Pany axis; that is, if we
124: denote the entries of the array by mj1 ...jd (1 ≤ jk ≤ n), then we require njk =1 mj1 ...jd = t for all
125: k = 1, . . . , d. Again we count all such cubes in terms of d, n, and t; we denote the corresponding
126: enumerating function by Hnd (t). So Hn2 = Hn , whose properties are stated in Theorem 1. Except
127: for the case d = n = 3, which seems to have appeared first in [5], we could not find any other
128: references. We prove the following result for general d.
129: Theorem 3 The function Hnd (t) is a quasi-polynomial in t of degree (n − 1)d that satisfies the
130: identities
131:       Hnd (−n − t) = (−1)n−1 Hnd (t) ,            Hnd (−1) = Hnd (−2) = · · · = Hnd (−n + 1) = 0 .
132:     We prove Theorem 2 in Sections 2 and 3. To be able to compute the counting functions Mn ,
133: Sn , and Pn for specific n, we need the periods of these quasi-polynomials. We describe in Section 4
134: methods for finding these periods and hence for the actual computation of Mn , Sn , and Pn . Finally,
135: we prove Theorem 3 in Section 5. All of our methods are based on the idea that one can interpret
136: the various counting functions as enumerating integer points (“lattice points”) in certain polytopes.

306: Proposition 7. For every integer k and a graph G of degeneracy d there is
307: a maximal collection C of edge disjoint k-stars on G such that each vertex of
308: G is an endvertex to at most d + k − 1 stars in C.

310: Case k = 2 was done by Füredi and Lehel [4]. We are using downdegree
311: instead of updegree since this feels more natural to us. Let us choose an
312: ordering v1 , v2 , . . . , vn of vertices of G0 such that the (maximum) downdegree
313: ←−

315: vertices vj , j < i, over all i = 1, 2, . . . , n, equals d = dg (G).
316:

318: stars whose central vertex is smaller in the given ordering than any of its
319: endvertices, and then extend it to a maximal collection of edge-disjoint k-
320: stars. Then u ∈ G appears as an endvertex of a star of the first kind, or as
321: such endvertex of a star of the second kind which is greater than its centre
322:          ←−
323: at most ∆ (G) times. It appears as an endvertex smaller than the centre of
324: a star of the second kind at most k − 1 times since otherwise we could form
...

637: Conjecture. For every integer k and a graph G of degeneracy d there is a
638: maximal collection C of edge disjoint paths of length 2k on G such that each
639: vertex of G is an endvertex to at most ck d paths in C.
640: 
641: This cannot hold for odd-length paths, as can be seen by taking, for instance
642: paths of length 3 and G = K2,m , where m is large. Case k = 1 of the
643: Conjecture is the special case of Proposition 7; it was first proved by Füredi
644: and Lehel [4]. We think, we can also prove the Conjecture for k = 2.
645: 
646: More generally, can 2k-path in the statement of the Conjecture (or Proposi-
647: tion 7) be replaced by a tree, in which all distances betweeen the leaves are
648: even?

633: Conjecture. For every integer k and a graph G of degeneracy d there is a
634: maximal collection C of edge disjoint paths of length 2k on G such that each
635: vertex of G is an endvertex to at most ck d paths in C.
636: 
637: This cannot hold for odd-length paths, as can be seen by taking, for instance
638: paths of length 3 and G = K2,m , where m is large. Case k = 1 of the
639: Conjecture is the special case of Proposition 7; it was first proved by Füredi
640: and Lehel [4]. We think, we can also prove the Conjecture for k = 2.
641: 
642: More generally, can 2k-path in the statement of the Conjecture (or Proposi-
643: tion 7) be replaced by a tree, in which all distances betweeen the leaves are
644: even?

379: 
380: Recall that a k-star is a copy of K1,k .
381: Proposition 9. For every integer k and a graph G of degeneracy d there is
382: a maximal collection C of edge disjoint k-stars on G such that each vertex of
383: G is an endvertex to at most d + k − 1 stars in C.
384: 
385: Case k = 2 was done by Füredi and Lehel [4]. We are using downdegree
386: instead of updegree since this feels more natural to us. Let us choose an
387: ordering v1 , v2 , . . . , vn of vertices of G0 such that the (maximum) downdegree
388: ←−
389: ∆ (G0 ), defined as the maximum of the number of edges from a vertex vi to
390: vertices vj , j < i, over all i = 1, 2, . . . , n, equals d = dg (G).
391: 
392: Let us construct C as follows: take a maximal collection of edge-disjoint k-
393: stars whose central vertex is smaller in the given ordering than any of its
394: endvertices, and then extend it to a maximal collection of edge-disjoint k-
395: stars. Then u ∈ G appears as an endvertex of a star of the first kind, or as
396: such endvertex of a star of the second kind which is greater than its centre
397:          ←−
398: at most ∆ (G) times. It appears as an endvertex smaller than the centre of
399: a star of the second kind at most k − 1 times since otherwise we could form
...
402: first kind.
403:                                          ←
404:                                          −
405: It follows that u can appear at most ∆ (G) + k − 1 = d + k − 1 times
406: as an endvertex of a star in C,Swhich proves Proposition 9. Note that the
407: maximality of C implies ∆(G \ C) ≤ k − 1.

411: 6     Construction of a Hypergraph and its Colour-
412:       ing
413: 
414: Recall that we are given an H-packing P = {H1 , H2 , . . . , Hm } on n vertices
415: and G0 = (Kn ) \ ∪i=1,...,m E(Hi ) is our uncovered graph.
416: 
417: In this section we shall give a construction of a certain hypergraph M on a
418: vertex set of G0 along with its edge-colouring; we shall need it in order to
419: extend P to a packing on a larger set of vertices, in which the uncovered
420: graph will have a small transversal.
421: 
422:                                        10
...
431: sizes of the bipartition classes. For convenience we can assume that s ≥ t,
432: perhaps choosing another v.
433: 
434: By Proposition 9 there is a collection C of disjoint k-stars on G0 with the
435: property that each vertex of G0 is an endvertex to at most dg (G0 )+k−1 stars
436: in C. Define a multi-k-graph (k-uniform hypergraph with several edges on
437: the same set of vertices allowed) called M as follows: for every star of C there
438: is a k-edge containing precisely the leaves of the star. The maximum degree
439: ∆(M) (i.e. the maximum number of edges containing any given vertex) is
440: bounded by dg (G0 ) + k − 1 ≤ c3 ∗ ex (n, H)/n, where c3 is a positive constant
441: depending only on H. We shall denote edges of M by (c, e) where c ∈ G0 is
442: the centre of the respective star and e is the hyperedge consisting precisely
443: of the leaves of the star.
444: 
445: Let us introduce an edge-colouring on M so that each colour class forms a
446: vertex-disjoint collection of hyperedges. Since every hyperedge intersects at
447: most k(∆(M)−1) other hyperedges, it can be done, using at most k(∆(M)−
448: 1) + 1 = c4 ∗ ex (n, H)/n colours: let us colour greedily as many hyperedges
449: with colour 1 as we can, then with colour 2 and so on (again c4 is a positive
450: constant depending only on H).

16:                                                Fix n > 28. Let
17: 
18:                                             L := {0, 1, 2, 5, 9, 10, 11, 12, 13}
19:                                             U := {n − 14, n − 13, n − 12, n − 11, n − 10, n − 6, n − 5, n − 3, n − 2, n − 1}
20:                                               = n − {1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14}

22:                                             Note that U − n + 9 = {−5, −4, −3, −2, −1, 3, 4, 6, 7, 8} is the complement of
23:                                             L in the interval [−5, 13]. Let R be a uniformly chosen subset of [14, b − 15].
24:                                             We will show that the set
25: 
26:                                                                          A := L ∪ R ∪ U ⊆ [0, n − 1]
27: 
28:                                             has A + A = [0, 2n − 2] \ {8} with high probability (at least 31/32), while
29:                                             neither of ±(n − 9) is in A − A. In other words, the set A is likely to have
30:                                             more sums than differences.

50:     T. Tao gave the first construction that generates large MSTD sets [com-
51: municated to the author through M. Nathanson], and M. Nathanson has since
52: produced several parameterized families of MSTD sets. I refer the reader to
53: M. Nathanson’s article (arXiv:math.NT/0604340) for further background on
54: this problem.
55:     In contrast to the paucity of MSTD sets among small subsets of [n], we
56: show that a positive proportion of all subsets of [n] are MSTD sets. Precisely,
57: we show that for n > 28 there at least
58: 
59:                                    10−9 · 2n
60: 
61: subsets of [n] that are MSTD sets. Computations indicate that there are in
62: fact roughly 0.00045 · 2n MSTD sets, so the constant 10−9 may only be 5
63: orders of magnitude off. We have not optimized the constant 10−9 derived
64: from our argument.
65:     These two results may indicate the presence of a threshhold. Set pn to
66: vary with n, and define n independent random variables Xi , with Xi = 1 with
67: probability pn and Xi = 0 with probability 1 − pn . This defines a random
68: set A := {i ∈ [n] : Xi = 1}. The observations above can be rephrased: if
69: pn ≪ n−3/4 , then A is an MSTD set with probability 0 (as n → ∞). We
70: show that if pn = 1/2, then A is an MSTD set with positive probability (as

URL https://arxiv.org/pdf/math/0608131v3 FILE sources/24996d33ef92dcf0.txt
39:                                             Definition. A finite set S is difference-dominant if |S − S| > |S + S|, sum-dominant if |S + S| >
40:                                             |S − S|, and sum-difference-balanced if |S + S| = |S − S|.
41:                                             Nathanson [7] calls sum-dominant sets “MSTD” sets, short for “More Sums Than Differ-
42:                                             ences”. We refer the reader to [6,?\protect\vrulewidth0pt\protect\href{http://arxiv.org/abs/math/06
43:                                             for the history of this problem.
44:                                                  Our main theorem shows that, perhaps contrary to intuition, all three types of set in
45:                                             the above definition are ubiquitous.

297:     We turn now to three lemmas describing the probabilities that particular differences
298: are missing from A − A, where A is chosen randomly from one of our classes. A new
299: obstacle appears: while the random variables X j Xk− j controlling the presence of the sum
300: k in A + A are always mutually independent, the same is not true of the random vari-
301: ables X j Xk+ j controlling the presence of the difference k in A − A, at least when k is small
302: enough that j, k + j, and 2k + j can all lie between 0 and n − 1. Fortunately, when k is this
303: small the probabilities in question are already minuscule, so a simple argument provides
304: a serviceable bound (Lemma 10 below).

557: 4 Average values
558: In this section, we prove Theorem 3 by calculating the average values of |S − S| and
559: |S + S| as S ranges over an arithmetic progression P of length n. Since the problem is
560: invariant under dilations and translations, it suffices to prove the theorem in the case
561: P = {0, 1, 2, . . . , n − 1}.
562:      We begin by addressing the average cardinality of the sumset S + S. In fact, we can
563: give an exact formula for the average size of the sumset, or equivalently for the sum of the
564: sizes of all sumsets as S ranges over subsets of {0, 1, 2, . . . , n − 1}. The reason we can do so
565: is essentially because of the linearity of expectations of random variables.

573:                                                                                                                        (1)
574:     S ⊆{0,...,n −1}

991: For any particular pair j, k, if a single finite configuration of edges could be found that
992: omitted exactly j possible sums and k possible differences, the methods of this paper would
993: then show that ρ j,k > 0 (technically, that the analogous expression with lim inf n→∞ in place
994: of limn→∞ is positive).
995:    The last remark suggests as well the following open problem, for which a simple proof
996: might exist, though we have not been able to find one.
997: 
998: Conjecture 20. For any nonnegative integers j and k with k even, there exists a positive integer
999: n, and a set S ⊂ {0, 1, 2, . . . , n − 1} with 0 ∈ S and n − 1 ∈ S, such that |S + S| = 2n − 1 − j
1000: and |S − S| = 2n − 1 − k.
1001: 
1002: Hegarty points out that his methods from [2] can establish both Conjecture 19 and Conjec-
1003: ture 20 in the case j ≥ k/2.
1004:     We know [1, 5] that essentially all subsets of {0, 1, 2, . . . , n − 1} of cardinality O(n1/4 )
1005: are Sidon sets and hence difference-dominant sets. More generally, we can show (perhaps
1006: in a sequel paper) that if m = o(n1/2 ), then almost all subsets of {0, 1, 2, . . . , n − 1} of
1007: cardinality m are difference-dominant sets.
1008:     This result may indicate the presence of a threshhold. Set pn to vary with n, and
1009: define n independent random variables Xi , with Xi = 1 with probability pn . This defines
1010: a random set A := {i ∈ {0, 1, 2, . . . , n − 1} : Xi = 1}. The observations above can then be
1011: rephrased in the following way: if pn = o(n−1/2 ), then A is a difference-dominant set with
...
1011: rephrased in the following way: if pn = o(n−1/2 ), then A is a difference-dominant set with
1012: probability 1 (as n → ∞). We showed in this article that if pn = 1/2, then A is a sum-
1013: dominant set with positive probability (as n → ∞), and our result is easily extended to
1014: pn = c > 0. An important unanswered question is “Which sequences pn generate a sum-
1015: dominant set with positive probability?” Perhaps our last conjecture captures the correct
1016: notion:
1017: 
1018: Conjecture 21. For each n ≥ 1, let Xn,0 , Xn,1 , . . . , Xn,n−1 be independent identically distributed
1019: random variables, and set An := {i : 0 ≤ i < n, Xn,i = 1}. If both | An | → ∞ and | An |/n → 0
1020: with probability 1, then the probability that An is difference-dominant also goes to 1.
1021:

10:                                                   Abstract. Let R = K[x1 , . . . , xn ] be a polynomial ring in n variables over a
11:                                                   field K and I be a matroidal ideal of degree d. Let astab(I) and dstab(I) be the
12:                                                   smallest integers l and k, for which Ass(I l ) and depth(R/I k ) stabilize, respectively.
13:                                                   In this paper, we show that astab(I), dstab(I) ≤ min{d, ℓ(I)}, where ℓ(I) is the
14:                                                   analytic spread of I. Furthermore, by a counterexample we give a negative answer
15:                                                   to the conjecture of Herzog and Qureshi [11] about stability of matroidal ideals.
16: 
17: 
18: 
19: 
20:                                                                                     Introduction
21:                                               Throughout this paper, we assume that R = K[x1 , . . . , xn ] is the polynomial ring
22:                                            in n variables over a field K with the maximal ideal m = (x1 , . . . , xn ) and I is a
23:                                            monomial ideal of R such that G(I) is the unique minimal monomial generators set
24:                                            of I. Let Ass(I) be the set of associated prime ideals of R/I. Brodmann [2] showed
25:                                            that there exists an integer l such that Ass(I t ) = Ass(I l ) for all t ≥ l. The smallest
26:                                            such integer l is called the index of Ass-stability of I, and denoted by astab(I).
27:                                            Moreover, Ass(I l ) is called the stable set of associated prime ideals of I and it is
28:                                            denoted by Ass∞ (I). Brodmann [3] also showed that there exists an integer k such
29:                                            that depth R/I s = depth R/I k for all s ≥ k. The smallest such integer k is called the
30:                                            index of depth stability of I and denoted by dstab(I). Herzog and the second author
...
52: orem 2.5] or [18, Theorem 2.3]); (4) according to [11] and [12], every polymatroidal
53: ideal satisfying the persistence property and non-increasing depth functions, that
54: is, if I is a polymatroidal ideal then, for all l, k, there is the following sequences:
55: Ass(I l ) ⊆ Ass(I l+1 ) and depth(R/I k+1 ) ≤ depth(R/I k ), these properties, in general,
56: for all square-free monomial ideals has a negative answer (see [17, Example 2.9]);
57: (5) for every polymatroidal ideal I, we have astab(I), dstab(I) < ℓ(I), where ℓ(I)
58: is the analytic spread of I (see [11, Theorem 4.1]); (6) if I is a polymatroidal ideal,
59: then limk→∞ depth R/I k = n − ℓ(I) (see [12, Corollary 3.5]); (7) if I is a matroidal
60: ideal of degree d, then astab(I) = 1 if and only if dstab(I) = 1 (see [16, Theorem
61: 3.3]).
62:    Herzog and Qureshi [11] raised the following conjectured:
63: Conjecture 0.1. Let I be any polymatroidal ideal. Does astab(I) = dstab(I)?
64:    This conjecture in the following cases has a positive answer: (1) if I is a polyma-
65: troidal ideal of degree 2, ([15, Theorem 2.12]); (2) if I is a matroidal ideal of degree
66: 3, ([16, Corollary 3.13]); (3) if I is a transversal polymatroidal ideal, [12, Corollaries
67: 4.6, 4.14]; (4) if I is a Veronese type polymatroidal ideal [12, Corollary 5.7], then
68: astab(I) = dstab(I). This conjecture, in general, has a negative answer, see [15,
69: Examples 2.21, 2.22]. However, it is still remained for all matroidal ideals.
70:    The following question asked by Karimi and the second author [15]:
71: Question 0.2. Let I be any matroidal ideal. Then can we have astab(I), dstab(I) ≤
72: d, where d is the degree of I?
...
75: Ass (I), then the question has a positive answer, (see [15, Corollary 2.13] and [16,
76: Proposition 3.8]). Also, if I is a transversal matroidal ideal or if I is a Veronese type
77: matroidal ideal, then again this question has a positive answer (see ([12, Corollaries
78: 4.6, 4.14, 5.7]).
79:    In this paper, by a counterexample, we show that the Conjecture 0.1 in general for
80: all matroidal ideals has a negative answer. Moreover, we give an affirmative answer
81: to the Question 0.2, that is, astab(I), dstab(I) ≤ d for all matroidal ideals.
82:    For each unexplained notion or terminology, we refer the reader to [8]. The explicit
83: examples were performed with the help of the computer algebra system Macaulay2
84: [6].

1:                                                     Biregularity in Sidorenko’s Conjecture
2: 
3: 
4: 
5: 
6: arXiv:2108.06599v1 [math.CO] 14 Aug 2021
7:                                                    Leonardo N. Coregliano∗                Alexander A. Razborov†
8:                                                                            October 18, 2021
9: 
10: 
11:                                                                                   Abstract
12:                                                        Sidorenko’s conjecture says that the minimum density of a bigraph
13:                                                    G in a bigraphon W of a given edge density is attained when W is
14:                                                    a constant function. We reduce Sidorenko’s conjecture to the case
15:                                                    when the target bigraphon W is biregular. With this biregularity
16:                                                    result and some ideas of its proof, we also obtain simple proofs in the
17:                                                    asymmetric setting of several results previously shown in the symmetric
...
15:                                                    when the target bigraphon W is biregular. With this biregularity
16:                                                    result and some ideas of its proof, we also obtain simple proofs in the
17:                                                    asymmetric setting of several results previously shown in the symmetric
18:                                                    setting. Furthermore, we also show that bigraphs that have a special
19:                                                    type of tree decomposition, called reflective tree decomposition, satisfy
20:                                                    Sidorenko’s conjecture. This both unifies and generalizes the notions of
21:                                                    strong tree decompositions and N -decompositions from the literature.
22: 
23: 
24:                                            1       Introduction
25:                                            In [Sid91] (see also [Sid93]), Sidorenko conjectured that if Ω = (X, µ) and
26:                                            Λ = (Y, ν) are probability spaces, W : X × Y → R+ is a bounded measurable
27:                                            function (a bigraphon), and G = (V1 , V2 , E) is a bipartite graph with a given
28:                                            bipartition (V1 , V2 ) (a bigraph), then
29: 
30:                                                                           t(G, W ) ≥ t(ρ, W )e(G) ,                            (1)
31:                                                ∗
32:                                                University of Chicago, lenacore@uchicago.edu
33:                                                †
34:                                                University of Chicago, razborov@math.uchicago.edu, and Steklov Mathematical
35:                                            Institute, Moscow
...
56: 
57:                           t(G, W ) ≥ cG · t(ρ, W )e(G) .                    (3)
58: 
59: With this tensor power trick, Sidorenko showed [Sid91, Theorem 10] that his
60: conjecture is equivalent to a conjecture by Simonovits [Sim84, Conjecture 8].
61: Since Simonovits’s Conjecture is a weak version of another joint conjec-
62: ture with Erdős [ES84, Conjecture 2] on supersaturation (see also [Sim84,
63: Conjecture 7]), Sidorenko’s Conjecture is sometimes referred to as Erdős–
64: Sidorenko–Simonovits Conjecture, possibly with some permutation of these
65: names. While quite easy to prove (see Lemma 4.1 below for a general version),
66: this tensor power trick has been essential to several results in the literature,
67: see [CFS10, LS11, KLL16, CKLL18b, CKLL18a, Sid21] for some examples.
68:     From an easy adaptation of the graphon theory (see [Lov12] for an in-
69: troduction to the topic) to the case of bigraphs, (3) is in turn equivalent
70: to
71: 
72:                            t(G, H) ≥ cG · t(ρ, H)e(G)                       (4)
73: 
74: for every bigraph H = (U1 , U2 , F ), where
75: 
76:                                     def    |Hom(G, H)|
...
79: 
80: and Hom(G, H) is the set of all bigraph homomorphisms from G to H,
81: i.e., functions f : V1 ∪ V2 → U1 ∪ U2 such that f (Vi ) ⊆ Ui (i = 1, 2) and
82: (v, w) ∈ E =⇒ (f (v), f (w)) ∈ F .
83:      In fact, Sidorenko’s Conjecture is often studied under the further assump-
84: tion that W is symmetric (i.e., Ω = Λ and W (x, y) = W (y, x) for every
85: x, y ∈ X), in which case G and H become ordinary graphs (but G is still
86: 
87:                                             2
88: 
89: bipartite). It was proved in [CKLL18b, Lemma 3.3 and 3.4] that for the
90: symmetric version of Sidorenko’s Conjecture, it is sufficient to show (4) under
91: the further assumption that all degrees of H are between dave (H)/8 and
92: 2 · dave (H), where dave (H) (= t(ρ, H) · |V (H)|) is the average degree of H.
93:      In this paper we show an asymmetric “limit” version of this. The limit
94: part is new also in the symmetric setting but the proofs become much easier;
95: we will discuss how to adjust them in Section 8.
96:      More specifically, our main result (Theorem 3.1) says that in order to show
97: that a bigraph is Sidorenko, it is sufficient to show (3) under the additional
98: assumption that
99:                Z                     Z
...
114: (Theorem 3.2), but we can prove a weak converse: if G0 is the amalgamation
115: of k copies of G along the same vertex, then G is Sidorenko if and only if G0
116: is Sidorenko (Theorem 3.3).
117:     When studying (1), Sidorenko in fact introduced a stronger (a priori)
118: conjecture [Sid91, Equation (2)] that in particular implies
119:                      Z Z                        e(G)/|V1 (G)|       !|V1 (G)|

128: version of this implication in the “ordinary” setting; namely, we show that
129: 
130:                                            3

132: every Sidorenko bigraph G = (V1 , V2 , E) in which all vertices of V1 have degree
133: at least d satisfies t(G, W ) ≥ t(K1,d , W )e(G)/d (Theorem 3.4).
134:     Finally, by using the biregularity assumption we are able to unify and
...
133: at least d satisfies t(G, W ) ≥ t(K1,d , W )e(G)/d (Theorem 3.4).
134:     Finally, by using the biregularity assumption we are able to unify and
135: generalize (Theorem 3.5) the results of [CKLL18b, Theorem 1.2] on strong
136: tree decompositions and of [CL17, Theorem 5.12] on N -decompositions as par-
137: ticular cases of what we call reflective tree decompositions (see Definition 2.2);
138: this result holds in both the non-symmetric and symmetric settings.
139:     This paper is organized as follows. In Section 2 we establish necessary
140: notation. In Section 3 we state our results. In Section 4 we present the main
141: lemma used in the proofs. In Section 5 we prove our biregularity result. In
142: Section 6, we prove the aforementioned applications of our biregularity result
143: and our main lemma to amalgamations and the strengthened K1,d version
144: of Sidorenko’s Conjecture. In Section 7 we prove the result on reflective
145: tree decompositions. In Section 8 we show how to adapt the material from
146: Sections 4, 5 and 7 to the symmetric setting. We finish the paper with a brief
147: discussion and some open problems in Section 9.

312: 2.4      Weak domination and reflective tree decomposi-
313:          tions
314: Definitions in this section are needed only for Theorem 3.5.
315: 
316:                                                  7
317: 
318:    Inspired by [CL17], we give the following definition of weak domination
319: between bigraphs.
320: Definition 2.1. Let G1 and G2 be bigraphs. We say that G1 weakly dominates
321: G2 if
322:                           t(G1 , W )       t(G2 , W )
323:                                  e(G )
324:                                        ≥
325:                         t(ρ, W ) 1       t(ρ, W )e(G2 )
326: for every biregular non-zero bigraphon W . We say that a bigraph G is
327: induced-Sidorenko if it weakly dominates all of its induced subgraphs.
328: Remark 1. In [CL17, §5.2], domination between bigraphs G1 and G2 is de-
...
329: fined by the requirement t(G1 , W )1/e(G1 ) ≥ t(G2 , W )1/e(G2 ) for every bigraphon
330: W . It is easy to see that as long as e(G1 ) ≥ e(G2 ) and G2 is Sidorenko (which
331: is the case we are mostly interested in), domination implies weak domination.
332: That explains our choice of the terminology. Let us also note that our main
333: result, Theorem 3.1, readily implies that if G1 weakly dominates G2 and G2
334: is Sidorenko then G1 is Sidorenko as well.
335:     Recall that for a bigraph G, the 2-core of G is a maximal connected
336: subgraph in which all vertices have degree at least 2. When G is connected,
337: it contains only one 2-core, which we denote C2 (G). It can be obtained by
338: progressively removing, in an arbitrary order, vertices of degree less than 2
339: until no such vertices remain.
340:     For a flag F = (G, θ) with G connected, we define the 2-core C2 (F ) as
341: the flag of the form F 0 = (G0 , θ), where G0 is the maximal subgraph in which
342: all vertices that are not in im(θ) have degree at least two; this can of course
343: be obtained by progressively removing vertices of degree less than 2 that are
344: not in im(θ).
345: Remark 2. Since in a biregular bigraphon W , we have t(G, W ) = t(G −
346: v, W )t(ρ, W )dG (v) whenever dG (v) ≤ 1, it follows that G weakly dominates H
347: if and only if C2 (G) weakly dominates C2 (H).
348:     We now define a generalization of the notions of strong tree decomposi-
349: tions [CKLL18b, §1] and N -decompositions [CL17, §5.3], which themselves
...
347: if and only if C2 (G) weakly dominates C2 (H).
348:     We now define a generalization of the notions of strong tree decomposi-
349: tions [CKLL18b, §1] and N -decompositions [CL17, §5.3], which themselves
350: are generalizations of the usual notion of tree decompositions [Hal76, RS84].
351: Definition 2.2. Given a connected non-trivial bigraph G, a reflective tree
352: decomposition of G is a tree T such that
353: 
354:    i. We have V (T ) ⊆ 2V (G) and V (G) = U ∈V (T ) U .
355:                                          S
356: 
357: 
358: 
359:                                          8
360: 
361:     ii. For every (v, w) ∈ E(G), there exists U ∈ V (T ) such that v, w ∈ U .
362: 
363:     iii. For every U1 , U2 ∈ V (T ) and every U3 ∈ V (T ) in the unique path from
364:          U1 to U2 in T , we have U1 ∩ U2 ⊆ U3 .
365: 
366:     iv. For every {U1 , U2 } ∈ E(T ) we have C2 (FU1 U2 ) ∼ = C2 (FU2 U1 ), where
367:                def
...
379:                                              def
380: tence of an automorphism of the flag F = (C2 (G|U1 ∪U2 ), U1 ∩ U2 ) that maps
381: U1 ∩ V (|F |) to U2 ∩ V (|F |).
382:     Items (i), (ii) and (iii) alone say that T is a usual tree decomposition.
383: Strong tree decompositions of [CKLL18b, §1] are precisely reflective tree
384: decompositions whose core is empty (i.e., G|U is a tree for every U ∈ V (T )) and
385: N -decompositions of [CL17, §5.3] are obtained by replacing the requirement
386: C2 (FU1 U2 ) ∼
387:              = C2 (FU2 U1 ) in (iv) with FU1 U2 ∼
388:                                                 = FU2 U1 instead (this forces all G|U
389: for U ∈ V (T ) to be isomorphic to a fixed bigraph N ).

392: 3      Main results
393: In this section we present our main results.
394: Theorem 3.1. Let G be a bigraph. If there exists cG > 0 such that t(G, W ) ≥
395: cG ·t(ρ, W )e(G) for every biregular bigraphon W , then G is a Sidorenko bigraph.
396: Theorem 3.2. If F1 and F2 are left (or right) 1-flags such that |F1 | and |F2 |
397: are Sidorenko bigraphs, then |F1 t F2 | is a Sidorenko bigraph.
398:     As we mentioned in the introduction, Theorem 3.2 can be extracted
399: from [LS11, Lemmas 3.2 and 3.4 and Theorem 5], but we will provide a much
...
405: Theorem 3.3. Let F be a left 1-flag and k ∈ N+ . Then |F | is a Sidorenko
406: bigraph if and only if |F tk | is a Sidorenko bigraph.
407: Theorem 3.4. If G is a Sidorenko bigraph with δ1 (G) ≥ d, then t(G, W ) ≥
408: t(K1,d , W )e(G)/d for every bigraphon W .
409: Theorem 3.5. If T is a reflective tree decomposition of a connected non-
410: trivial bigraph G whose core H weakly dominates G|U1 ∩U2 for every {U1 , U2 } ∈
411: E(T ), then G weakly dominates H. In particular, if H is a Sidorenko bigraph,
412: then G is also a Sidorenko bigraph.
413:     Note that in Theorem 3.5 above, if the core H is an induced-Sidorenko
414: bigraph, then both the condition that it weakly dominates G|U1 ∩U2 for every
415: {U1 , U2 } ∈ E(T ) and the fact that H is a Sidorenko bigraph follow (see
416: Remark 2). Hence in that case we can conclude that G is a Sidorenko
417: bigraph.
418:     As we mentioned in Remark 3, the notions of strong tree decompositions
419: and N -decompositions are particular cases of reflective tree decompositions.
420: The corresponding results can be retrieved from Theorem 3.5 above as follows.
421: For [CKLL18b, Theorem 1.2], any two forests weakly dominate each other
422: for obvious reasons, which implies that strongly tree decomposable bigraphs
423: are Sidorenko bigraphs. For [CL17, Theorem 5.12], by [Hat10, Theorem 2.14],
424: every weakly norming bigraph N dominates any of its (not necessarily induced)
425: subgraphs, so it is an induced-Sidorenko bigraph, hence any N -decomposable
...

1074: W follows by applying the Dominated Convergence Theorem to the same
1075: inequality for W as  → 0.

1077: prove (19) for every W ∈ W up to a multiplicative constant that does not

1239: Lemma 4.1 still holds in the symmetric setting. For Lemma 4.2, we need to
1240: make some adjustments.

1242: be a left 1-flag such that G is both left and right d-regular and let  > 0.
1243:    Then for every graphon W : Ω × Ω → R+ over Ω = (X, µ), there exists a

1325: 
1326: 9     Conclusion and open problems
1327: In this paper, we have shown how to reduce Sidorenko’s Conjecture to biregular
1328: bigraphons (or regular graphons in the symmetric case). We have also shown
1329: that this reduction and the construction of Lemma 4.2 can be used to obtain
1330: simple proofs of some properties of the class of Sidorenko bigraphs.
1331:     The proofs in Section 6 heavily rely on the fact that the two sides of
1332: the bigraphs and bigraphons can be manipulated independently. It is then
1333: natural to ask if Theorems 3.3 and 3.4 hold in the symmetric setting as well
1334: (we remind the reader that the symmetric analogue of Theorem 3.2 follows
1335: from [LS11, Lemmas 3.2 and 3.4 and Theorem 5], but the proof is much more
1336: involved).
1337:     In another direction, Conlon–Kim–Lee–Lee [CKLL18a] also provided a
1338: higher-order version of their strong tree decompositions, which is reminiscent
1339: (but yields a completely different class of symmetrically Sidorenko bigraphs)
1340: of Szegedy’s conditionally independent coupling constructions [Sze15]. While
1341: we believe that a higher-order version of the reflective tree decompositions
1342: result should also hold (more specifically by using the same definition of
1343: higher-order decompositions and simply replacing level 0 with reflective tree
...
1347:                                          29
1348: 
1349: G|U1 U2 is a forest for each {U1 , U2 } ∈ E(T ) and we would like to ask instead
1350: if this restriction can be replaced by some weak domination restriction as in
1351: reflective tree decompositions. One stepping stone toward this goal is the
1352: following natural generalization of Theorem 3.5.
1353: Conjecture 1. If T is a reflective tree decomposition of a connected non-
1354: trivial bigraph G whose core H weakly dominates G|U1 ∩U2 for every {U1 , U2 } ∈
1355: E(T ), then for every non-empty V ⊆ V (T ), G weakly dominates G|SU ∈V U .
1356:     Theorem 3.5 is the particular case of the conjecture above when V consists
1357: of a single vertex of T .

1:                                                     Biregularity in Sidorenko’s Conjecture
2: 
3: 
4: 
5: 
6: arXiv:2108.06599v2 [math.CO] 25 Aug 2021
7:                                                    Leonardo N. Coregliano∗                Alexander A. Razborov†
8:                                                                             August 27, 2021
9: 
10: 
11:                                                                                  Abstract
12:                                                        Sidorenko’s Conjecture says that the minimum density of a bigraph
13:                                                    G in a bigraphon W of a given edge density is attained when W
14:                                                    is a constant function. A consequence of a result by B. Szegedy is
15:                                                    that it is enough to show Sidorenko’s Conjecture under the further
16:                                                    assumption that W is biregular. In this paper, we retrieve this result
17:                                                    with a more elementary proof. With this biregularity result and
...
15:                                                    that it is enough to show Sidorenko’s Conjecture under the further
16:                                                    assumption that W is biregular. In this paper, we retrieve this result
17:                                                    with a more elementary proof. With this biregularity result and
18:                                                    some ideas of its proof, we also obtain simple proofs of several other
19:                                                    results related to Sidorenko’s Conjecture. Furthermore, we also show
20:                                                    that bigraphs that have a special type of tree decomposition, called
21:                                                    reflective tree decomposition, satisfy Sidorenko’s conjecture. This both
22:                                                    unifies and generalizes the notions of strong tree decompositions and
23:                                                    N -decompositions from the literature.
24: 
25: 
26:                                            1       Introduction
27:                                            In [Sid91] (see also [Sid93]), Sidorenko conjectured that if Ω = (X, µ) and
28:                                            Λ = (Y, ν) are probability spaces, W : X × Y → R+ is a bounded measurable
29:                                            function (a bigraphon), and G = (V1 , V2 , E) is a bipartite graph with a given
30:                                            bipartition (V1 , V2 ) (a bigraph), then
31: 
32:                                                                           t(G, W ) ≥ t(ρ, W )e(G) ,                           (1)
33:                                                ∗
34:                                                University of Chicago, lenacore@uchicago.edu
35:                                                †
...
57: 
58:                           t(G, W ) ≥ cG · t(ρ, W )e(G) .                    (3)
59: 
60: With this tensor power trick, Sidorenko showed [Sid91, Theorem 10] that his
61: conjecture is equivalent to a conjecture by Simonovits [Sim84, Conjecture 8].
62: Since Simonovits’s Conjecture is a weak version of another joint conjec-
63: ture with Erdős [ES84, Conjecture 2] on supersaturation (see also [Sim84,
64: Conjecture 7]), Sidorenko’s Conjecture is sometimes referred to as Erdős–
65: Sidorenko–Simonovits Conjecture, possibly with some permutation of these
66: names. While quite easy to prove (see Lemma 4.1 below for a general version),
67: this tensor power trick has been essential to several results in the literature,
68: see [CFS10, LS11, KLL16, CKLL18b, CKLL18a, Sid21] for some examples.
69:     From an easy adaptation of the graphon theory (see [Lov12] for an in-
70: troduction to the topic) to the case of bigraphs, (3) is in turn equivalent
71: to
72: 
73:                            t(G, H) ≥ cG · t(ρ, H)e(G)                       (4)
74: 
75: for every bigraph H = (U1 , U2 , F ), where
76: 
77:                                     def    |Hom(G, H)|
...
79:                                           |U1 ||V1 | · |U2 ||V2 |
80: 
81: and Hom(G, H) is the set of all bigraph homomorphisms from G to H,
82: i.e., functions f : V1 ∪ V2 → U1 ∪ U2 such that f (Vi ) ⊆ Ui (i = 1, 2) and
83: (v, w) ∈ E =⇒ (f (v), f (w)) ∈ F . In fact, Sidorenko’s Conjecture is often
84: studied under the further assumption that W is symmetric (i.e., Ω = Λ and
85: W (x, y) = W (y, x) for every x, y ∈ X), in which case G and H become
86: ordinary graphs (but G is still bipartite).
87: 
88:                                             2
89: 
90:     It was proved in [Sze15b, Theorem 4] that it is sufficient to show (4) under
91: the further assumption that H is edge-vertex transitive (i.e., the natural
92: actions of the automorphism group of H on the sets V1 (H), V2 (H) and E(H)
93: are transitive). In this paper we recover an important consequence of this
94: result through a different, more elementary, method. More specifically, we
95: prove (Theorem 3.1) that in order to show that a bigraph is Sidorenko, it is
96: sufficient to show (3) under the additional assumption that W is biregular in
97: the sense
98:                Z                     Z
99:                   W (x0 , y) dν(y) =   W (x, y0 ) dµ(x) = t(ρ, W )           (5)
...
109: of k copies of G along the same vertex, then G is Sidorenko if and only
110: if G0 is Sidorenko (Theorem 3.3). Of course, these two results also follow
111: from [Sze15b].
112:     When studying (1), Sidorenko in fact introduced a stronger (a priori)
113: conjecture [Sid91, Equation (2)] that in particular implies
114:                      Z Z                    e(G)/|V1 (G)|      !|V1 (G)|
115:         t(G, W ) ≥            W (x, y) dν(y)                dµ(x)          .
116:                        X    Y
117: 
118: One way of interpreting the right-hand side is as t(K1,d , W )e(G)/d , where K1,d
119:                                                           def
120: is the (left) d-star bigraph, except that in the above d = e(G)/|V1 (G)|, which
121: is not necessarily an integer. Our methods allow us to retrieve a weaker
122: version of this implication in the “ordinary” setting; namely, we show that
123: every Sidorenko bigraph G = (V1 , V2 , E) in which all vertices of V1 have degree
124: at least d satisfies t(G, W ) ≥ t(K1,d , W )e(G)/d (Theorem 3.4).
125:     Finally, by using the biregularity assumption we are able to unify and
126: generalize (Theorem 3.5) the results of [CKLL18b, Theorem 1.2] on strong
127: tree decompositions and of [CL17, Theorem 5.12] on N -decompositions as par-
128: ticular cases of what we call reflective tree decompositions (see Definition 2.2);
129: this result holds in both the non-symmetric and symmetric settings.
...
134: notation. In Section 3 we state our results. In Section 4 we present the main
135: lemma used in the proofs. In Section 5 we prove our biregularity result. In
136: Section 6, we prove the aforementioned applications of our biregularity result
137: and our main lemma to amalgamations and the strengthened K1,d version
138: of Sidorenko’s Conjecture. In Section 7 we prove the result on reflective
139: tree decompositions. In Section 8 we show how to adapt the material from
140: Sections 4, 5 and 7 to the symmetric setting. We finish the paper with a brief
141: discussion and some open problems in Section 9.

145:                                                             def

147:                    def                                                        def
148: integers and N+ = N \ {0} for positive integers. For n ∈ N, we let [n] =
149: {1, . . . , n}. We also let R be the set of real numbers and R+ the set of
150: non-negative real numbers. Given a set V , we denote its power set by
151:    def
152: 2V = {W | W ⊆ V }.

305: G as of a bipartite graph since the choice of bipartition does not affect this

308: 
309: 2.4      Weak domination and reflective tree decomposi-
310:          tions
311: Definitions in this section are needed only for Theorem 3.5.
312:    Inspired by [CL17], we give the following definition of weak domination
313: between bigraphs.
314: 
315: 
316: 
317:                                                         7
318: 
319: Definition 2.1. Let G1 and G2 be bigraphs. We say that G1 weakly dominates
320: G2 if
321:                            t(G1 , W )       t(G2 , W )
322:                                         ≥
323:                          t(ρ, W )e(G1 )   t(ρ, W )e(G2 )
324: for every biregular non-zero bigraphon W . We say that a bigraph G is
325: induced-Sidorenko if it weakly dominates all of its induced subgraphs.
...
327: fined by the requirement t(G1 , W )1/e(G1 ) ≥ t(G2 , W )1/e(G2 ) for every bigraphon
328: W . It is easy to see that as long as e(G1 ) ≥ e(G2 ) and G2 is Sidorenko (which
329: is the case we are mostly interested in), domination implies weak domination.
330: That explains our choice of the terminology. Let us also note that our main
331: result, Theorem 3.1, readily implies that if G1 weakly dominates G2 and G2
332: is Sidorenko then G1 is Sidorenko as well.
333:     Recall that for a bigraph G, the 2-core of G is a maximal connected
334: subgraph in which all vertices have degree at least 2. When G is connected,
335: it contains only one 2-core, which we denote C2 (G). It can be obtained by
336: progressively removing, in an arbitrary order, vertices of degree less than 2
337: until no such vertices remain.
338:     For a flag F = (G, θ) with G connected, we define the 2-core C2 (F ) as
339: the flag of the form F 0 = (G0 , θ), where G0 is the maximal subgraph in which
340: all vertices that are not in im(θ) have degree at least two; this can of course
341: be obtained by progressively removing vertices of degree less than 2 that are
342: not in im(θ).
343: Remark 2. Since in a biregular bigraphon W , we have t(G, W ) = t(G −
344: v, W )t(ρ, W )dG (v) whenever dG (v) ≤ 1, it follows that G weakly dominates H
345: if and only if C2 (G) weakly dominates C2 (H).
346:     We now define a generalization of the notions of strong tree decomposi-
347: tions [CKLL18b, §1] and N -decompositions [CL17, §5.3], which themselves
...
345: if and only if C2 (G) weakly dominates C2 (H).
346:     We now define a generalization of the notions of strong tree decomposi-
347: tions [CKLL18b, §1] and N -decompositions [CL17, §5.3], which themselves
348: are generalizations of the usual notion of tree decompositions [Hal76, RS84].
349: Definition 2.2. Given a connected non-trivial bigraph G, a reflective tree
350: decomposition of G is a tree T such that
351:    i. We have V (T ) ⊆ 2V (G) and V (G) = U ∈V (T ) U .
352:                                          S
353: 
354:    ii. For every (v, w) ∈ E(G), there exists U ∈ V (T ) such that v, w ∈ U .
355:   iii. For every U1 , U2 ∈ V (T ) and every U3 ∈ V (T ) in the unique path from
356:        U1 to U2 in T , we have U1 ∩ U2 ⊆ U3 .
357: 
358:                                          8
359: 
360:     iv. For every {U1 , U2 } ∈ E(T ) we have C2 (FU1 U2 ) ∼ = C2 (FU2 U1 ), where
361:                def
362:         FUi Uj = (G|Ui , U1 ∩ U2 ) (we assume that each vertex of U1 ∩ U2 receives
363:         the same label in FU1 U2 as in FU2 U1 ).
364: 
365:    Condition (iv) above in particular implies that for every U1 , U2 ∈ V (T ),
...
374:                                              def
375: tence of an automorphism of the flag F = (C2 (G|U1 ∪U2 ), U1 ∩ U2 ) that maps
376: U1 ∩ V (|F |) to U2 ∩ V (|F |).
377:     Items (i), (ii) and (iii) alone say that T is a usual tree decomposition.
378: Strong tree decompositions of [CKLL18b, §1] are precisely reflective tree
379: decompositions whose core is empty (i.e., G|U is a tree for every U ∈ V (T )) and
380: N -decompositions of [CL17, §5.3] are obtained by replacing the requirement
381:              = C2 (FU2 U1 ) in (iv) with FU1 U2 ∼
382: C2 (FU1 U2 ) ∼                                  = FU2 U1 instead (this forces all G|U
383: for U ∈ V (T ) to be isomorphic to a fixed bigraph N ).
384: 
385: 
386: 3      Main results
387: In this section we present our main results.
388: Theorem 3.1. Let G be a bigraph. If there exists cG > 0 such that t(G, W ) ≥
389: cG ·t(ρ, W )e(G) for every biregular bigraphon W , then G is a Sidorenko bigraph.
390: Theorem 3.2. If F1 and F2 are left (or right) 1-flags such that |F1 | and |F2 |
391: are Sidorenko bigraphs, then |F1 t F2 | is a Sidorenko bigraph.
392:     The next theorem can be seen as a partial converse to Theorem 3.2.
393: Theorem 3.3. Let F be a left 1-flag and k ∈ N+ . Then |F | is a Sidorenko
394: bigraph if and only if |F tk | is a Sidorenko bigraph.
...
398: t(K1,d , W )e(G)/d for every bigraphon W .
399: 
400:                                          9
401: 
402: Theorem 3.5. If T is a reflective tree decomposition of a connected non-
403: trivial bigraph G whose core H weakly dominates G|U1 ∩U2 for every {U1 , U2 } ∈
404: E(T ), then G weakly dominates H. In particular, if H is a Sidorenko bigraph,
405: then G is also a Sidorenko bigraph.
406:     Note that in Theorem 3.5 above, if the core H is an induced-Sidorenko
407: bigraph, then both the condition that it weakly dominates G|U1 ∩U2 for every
408: {U1 , U2 } ∈ E(T ) and the fact that H is a Sidorenko bigraph follow (see
409: Remark 2). Hence in that case we can conclude that G is a Sidorenko
410: bigraph.
411:     As we mentioned in Remark 3, the notions of strong tree decompositions
412: and N -decompositions are particular cases of reflective tree decompositions.
413: The corresponding results can be retrieved from Theorem 3.5 above as follows.
414: For [CKLL18b, Theorem 1.2], any two forests weakly dominate each other
415: for obvious reasons, which implies that strongly tree decomposable bigraphs
416: are Sidorenko bigraphs. For [CL17, Theorem 5.12], by [Hat10, Theorem 2.14],
417: every weakly norming bigraph N dominates any of its (not necessarily induced)
418: subgraphs, so it is an induced-Sidorenko bigraph, hence any N -decomposable
...
1044: Since this is true for every bigraphon W , by Lemma 4.1 we conclude that
1045: t(G, W ) ≥ t(K1,d , W )e(G)/d , again for every W .
1046:

1048: 7     Reflective tree decompositions
1049: In this section we prove Theorem 3.5 on reflective tree decompositions.
1050: Proof of Theorem 3.5. Let W be the class of biregular bigraphons that are
1051: bounded away from zero. We claim that it is sufficient to show that
1052:                               t(G, W )       t(H, W )
1053:                                          ≥                                            (19)
1054:                             t(ρ, W )e(G)   t(ρ, W )e(H)
1055: only for W ∈ W. Indeed, if W is an arbitrary non-zero biregular bigraphon,
1056:                                     def
1057: then it can be approximated by W = W +  ∈ W ( > 0) and (19) for
1058: 
1059:                                           23
1060: 
1061: W follows by applying the Dominated Convergence Theorem to the same
1062: inequality for W as  → 0.
1063:      Since W is closed under tensor powers, by Lemma 4.1, it is enough to
1064: prove (19) for every W ∈ W up to a multiplicative constant that does not
...
1205:                         1
1206:           ≥                            · t(ρ, W )e(G)−e(H) · t(H, W ),
1207:               2e(T )+1 · v(T )e(T )
1208: 
1209: where the second inequality follows from (22) and since H weakly dominates
1210: each G|U1 ∩U2 . Therefore (19) holds by Lemma 4.1, so G weakly dominates H.
1211:     Finally, if further H is a Sidorenko bigraph, then by Theorem 3.1, G must
1212: also be a Sidorenko bigraph as it weakly dominates H.

1216: 8      The symmetric setting
1217: In this section, we briefly sketch how to adapt the results from Sections 4, 5
1218: and 7 to the symmetric setting. First we note that the tensor power trick of
1219: Lemma 4.1 still holds in the symmetric setting. For Lemma 4.2, we need to
1220: make some adjustments.
1221: Lemma 8.1 (Symmetric version of Lemma 4.2). Let d ∈ N+ , let F = (G, θ)
1222: be a left 1-flag such that G is both left and right d-regular and let  > 0.
1223:    Then for every graphon W : Ω × Ω → R+ over Ω = (X, µ), there exists a
1224: graphon W 0 : Ω0 × Ω0 → R+ such that the following hold.
1225:      i. We have ∆(F, W 0 ) ≤ (1 + ) · t(G, W 0 ).
...
1293: symmetrically Sidorenko bigraphs.
1294: 
1295: 
1296: 9    Conclusion and open problems
1297: In this paper, we have shown how to reduce Sidorenko’s Conjecture to biregular
1298: bigraphons (or regular graphons in the symmetric case). We have also shown
1299: 
1300:                                          28
1301: 
1302: that this reduction and the construction of Lemma 4.2 can be used to obtain
1303: simple proofs of some properties of the class of Sidorenko bigraphs.
1304:     The proofs in Section 6 heavily rely on the fact that the two sides of
1305: the bigraphs and bigraphons can be manipulated independently. It is then
1306: natural to ask if Theorem 3.4 holds in the symmetric setting as well (the
1307: symmetric analogues of Theorem 3.2 and 3.3 follow from a symmetric analogue
1308: of [Sze15b, Theorem 4]).
1309:     In another direction, Conlon–Kim–Lee–Lee [CKLL18a] also provided a
1310: higher-order version of their strong tree decompositions, which is reminiscent
1311: (but yields a completely different class of symmetrically Sidorenko bigraphs)
1312: of Szegedy’s conditionally independent coupling constructions [Sze15a]. While
1313: we believe that a higher-order version of the reflective tree decompositions
...
1311: (but yields a completely different class of symmetrically Sidorenko bigraphs)
1312: of Szegedy’s conditionally independent coupling constructions [Sze15a]. While
1313: we believe that a higher-order version of the reflective tree decompositions
1314: result should also hold (more specifically by using the same definition of
1315: higher-order decompositions and simply replacing level 0 with reflective tree
1316: decompositions), these higher-order decompositions have the restriction that
1317: G|U1 U2 is a forest for each {U1 , U2 } ∈ E(T ) and we would like to ask instead
1318: if this restriction can be replaced by some weak domination restriction as in
1319: reflective tree decompositions. One stepping stone toward this goal is the
1320: following natural generalization of Theorem 3.5.
1321: Conjecture 1. If T is a reflective tree decomposition of a connected non-
1322: trivial bigraph G whose core H weakly dominates G|U1 ∩U2 for every {U1 , U2 } ∈
1323: E(T ), then for every non-empty V ⊆ V (T ), G weakly dominates G|SU ∈V U .
1324:     Theorem 3.5 is the particular case of the conjecture above when V consists
1325: of a single vertex of T .

61: 1      Definition, examples and observations
62: A map ϕ : K → R2 of a graph K is approximable by embeddings, if for each ε > 0 there
63: is an ε-close to ϕ embedding f : K → R2 .

105: Example 1.1 (Sieklucki, 1969 [Si69]). (a) Any map I → I ⊂ R2 or T → I ⊂ R2 is approx-
106: imable by embeddings (where T is triod).

109:                                                            2
110: 
111:     (b) The standard d-winding S 1 → S 1 ⊂ R2 is approximable by embeddings if and only if
112: d ∈ {−1, 0, 1}. A map S 1 → S 1 ⊂ R2 is approximable by embeddings if and only if its degree
113: is −1, 0, 1.
114:     (c) An Euler path in the plane is approximable by embeddings if and only if it does not
115: have transversal self-intersections [Sk03’].
116: 
117:     The above examples show that approximability by embeddings is non-trivial to check, even
118: for paths.

175: Example 2.2. (a) ϕ′ = ϕ for the standard d-winding ϕ : S 1 → S 1 with d 6= 0.
176:    (b) ϕ′ is an embedding for any Euler path or cycle ϕ having no transversal self-intersections.
177:    (c) Let ϕ : S 1 → G be a cycle of k vertices in a graph G. Then either the domain of ϕ(k) is
178: empty or ϕ(k) is a standard winding of a non-zero degree. This degree can be considered as a
179: generalization of the degree of a map S 1 → S 1 .
180: 
181: Corollary 2.3 (P. F. Cortese, G. Di Battista, M. Patrignani, M. Pizzonia, 2009 [CBPP], cf.
182: [CEX, AAFT]). There are polynomial algorithms for
183:    • checking approximability of paths or cycles by embeddings.
184:    • finding the degree of the winding ϕ(k) for a cycle ϕ : S 1 → G of k vertices in a graph G.
185: 
186:     See also [Fu] on approximability by embeddings having a fixed isotopy class.

263: 6    The Hanani-Tutte obstruction
264: The Hanani-Tutte obstruction for planarity of graphs appeared in works of Hanani and Tutte
265: in 1934, 1970.
266: 
267:                                                 5
268: 
269:      The Hanani-Tutte obstruction for approximability of a broken line ϕ : I → R2 by
270: embeddings is that for each ε > 0 there exist a subdivision J of I and a general position broken
271: line f : J → R2 which is ε-close to ϕ and such that |f (e1 ) ∩ f (e2 )| is even for each disjoint
272: edges e1 , e2 of I. Note that
273:      • f (e1 ) and f (e2 ) are broken lines, not necessarily segments.
274:      • for small enough ε > we may assume that f (e1 ) ∩ f (e2 ) = ∅ whenever ϕ(e1 ) ∩ ϕ(e2 ) = ∅.
275:      The integer Hanani-Tutte obstruction is defined analogously, the only change is that instead

308: table” I ∗ put the number vf (i × j) = |f i ∩ f j| (mod 2).
309:    Cut I ∗ along the red edges, and let C1 , C2, . . . , Cn be all the obtained components such that
310: ∂Ck ∩ ∂I ∗ ⊂ I ∗ϕ .     P

316: 
317: One can easily show that v(ϕ) does not depend on the choice of f .
318:     Thus v(ϕ) = 0 is a necessary condition for approximability by embeddings.
319:     It is easy to check that v(ϕ) 6= 0 for a broken line ϕ : I → R2 containing a transversal
320: self-intersection.

331:     The analogue of this Corollary for closed broken line is false. The standard 3-winding is a
332: counterexample [RS, Fig. 5a]. Using ‘integration’ one can construct a counterexample with the
333: image a triod [FKMP, Fig. 18].
334: Problem 8.2. Is analogue of the Corollary true for the broken line replaced by a simplicial
335: map ϕ : K → R2 of any tree K?
336:     This is true when ϕ(K) ⊂ R [Fu14, Fu16].
337: Theorem 8.3 (M. Skopenkov, 2003 [Sk03’]). Let K be a graph with k vertices and without
338: vertices of degree > 3. A simplicial map ϕ : K → S 1 ⊂ R2 is approximable by embeddings if
339: and only if the van Kampen obstruction v(ϕ) = 0 and ϕ(k) does not contain standard windings
340: of odd degree d 6= 1.
341:     The derivative is defined analogously.
342: Problem 8.4. Is analogue of the Theorem true for an arbitrary graph K and S 1 replaced by
343: an arbitrary graph G?
344:      A positive answer implies a positive answer to the previous problem.
345:      One can define the van Kampen obstruction for disjoinability. However, the above example
346: of M. Skopenkov shows that its triviality is not sufficient for disjoinability, even for K = L = I,
347: i.e., for broken lines.

409: 11      Higher-dimensional generalization
410: A map f : K → M between polyhedra (=bodies of simplicial complexes) is said to be embed-
411: dable in Rm if there exists an embedding ψ : M → Rm for which ψ ◦ f is approximable by
412: embeddings.
413: Theorem 11.1. (a) For each n every map f : I n → I n is embeddable in R2n (Sieklucki, 1969
414: [Si69]).
415:     (b) For each n > 1 every map f : T n → T n between n-dimensional tori is embeddable in
416:   2n
417: R (Keesling-Wilson, 1985 [KW]).
418:     (c) For each n > 1 every map f : S n → S n is embeddable in R2n (Akhmetiev 1996, Melikhov,
419: 2004 [Ak96, Me04]).

460: 13     Generalizations to r-fold points
461: Problem 13.1 (Gromov, 2010 [Gr10]). Is it correct that if r is not a prime power, then for
462: each compact subset K of Rm for some m, having Lebesgue dimension dim K = (r − 1)n, there
463: is a continuous map K → Rnr each of whose point preimages contains less than r points?
464:     The answer is ‘yes’ for polyhedra K and each n ≥ 2 [MW, AMSW]. We conjecture that the
465: answer is ‘no’ in general.
466:     Like embeddability of compacta is being related to approximability of maps from polyhedra
467: by embeddings, Gromov’s problem is related to approximability of maps from polyhedra by
468: maps without r-fold points.
469:     Let K be a finite simplicial complex. A map f : K → Rm is an almost r-embedding if
470: f (σ1 ) ∩ · · · ∩ f (σr ) = ∅ whenever σ1 , . . . , σr are pairwise disjoint simplices of K.
471:     The van Kampen obstructions v(K) for almost r-embeddability of an n(r − 1)-complex K
472: to Rnr is defined analogously to above (in the lecture or in [MW]).
473: Theorem 13.2 (I. Mabillard and U. Wagner, 2015, S. Avvakumov, I. Mabillard, A. Skopenkov
474: and U. Wagner, 2015 [MW, AMSW]). Suppose that n, r ≥ 2, n + r ≥ 5. An n(r − 1)-complex
475: is almost r-embeddable in Rnr if and only if the van Kampen obstruction is zero.
476: Problem 13.3. Are analogous results true for approximability by almost r-embeddings, or by
477: maps f : K1 ⊔ . . . ⊔ Kr → Rnr such that f (K1 ) ∩ . . . ∩ f (Kr ) = ∅?

176:            2. Criteria for approximability by embeddings of paths
177:   Let us state criteria for approximability by embeddings of a simplicial path or cycle in
178: the plane. These criteria assert that, in some sense, transversal self-intersections are the
179: main obstructions to approximability by embeddings. The criteria are stated in terms of
180: the derivative [Mi97] which we define later.
181:   This section is not used in the rest of this paper (except for §3.3).
182: Theorem 2.1 (P. Minc, 1997, M. Skopenkov, 2003 [Mi97, Sk03]). (I) A polygonal line
183: (i.e. PL map) ϕ : I → R2 with k vertices is approximable by embeddings if and only if
184: for each i = 0, . . . , k the i-th derivative ϕ(i) does not have transversal self-intersections.
185:    (S) A closed polygonal line ϕ : S 1 → R2 with k vertices is approximable by embeddings
186: if and only if for each i = 0, . . . , k the i-th derivative ϕ(i) neither contains transversal
187: self-intersections nor is the standard winding of degree d 6∈ {−1, 0, 1}.
188:   Motivated by Theorem 3.3 below, we conjecture that the analogues of these criteria for
189: simplicial maps ϕ : K → R2 of connected graphs K ∼
190:                                                  6= I, S 1 are false.
...

202:                         Figure 3. Derivatives of graphs and paths
203: 
204:   Let us define the derivative (= the line graph) G′ of a graph G. The vertex set of
205: the graph G′ is the edge set of G. For an edge a ⊂ G denote by a′ ∈ G′ the corresponding
206: 
207: STABILITY OF INTERSECTIONS OF GRAPHS IN THE PLANEAND THE VAN KAMPEN OBSTRUCTION
208:                                                                               5
209: 
210: vertex. Vertices a′ and b′ of G′ are joined by an edge if and only if the edges a and b are
211: adjacent in G.
212:    Now let ϕ be a path in the graph G given by the sequence of vertices x1 , . . . , xk ∈ G,
213: where xi and xi+1 are joined by an edge. Then (x1 x2 )′ , . . . , (xk−1 xk )′ is a sequence of
214: vertices of G′ . In this sequence replace consecutive same vertices by a single vertex. The
215: obtained sequence of vertices determines a path ϕ′ in the graph G′ called pre-derivative
216: of the path ϕ.
217:    A 5-od (the cone over 5 points) is a planar graph whose derivative is the Kuratowsky
218: graph, which is not planar. But if G ⊂ R2 and the path ϕ does not have transversal self-

262:                             3. The van Kampen obstruction
263: 3.1. The Hanani-Tutte obstruction. The Hanani-Tutte obstruction for planarity of
264: graphs appeared in works of Hanani and Tutte in 1934, 1970.
265:    The Hanani-Tutte obstruction for approximability of a polygonal line ϕ : I → R2
266: by embeddings is the following necessary condition:
267:    for each ε > 0 there exist a subdivision J of I and a general position polygonal line
268: f : J → R2 which is ε-close to ϕ and such that |f (e1 ) ∩ f (e2 )| is even for each disjoint
269: edges e1 , e2 of I.
270:    Note that

335: map ϕ : K → R2 of any graph K is defined analogously to §3.2 [RS, §1], [Sk03].
336:    The analogue of Corollary 3.1 for closed polygonal line is false. The standard 3-winding
337: is a counterexample [RS, Fig. 5a]. Using ‘integration’ one can construct a counterexample
338: with the image a triod [FKMP, Fig. 18].
339: Conjecture 3.2. [RS, Conjecture 1.8] [Sk03, Conjecture 1.6] Is analogue of Corollary
340: 3.1 true for the polygonal line replaced by a simplicial map ϕ : K → R2 of any tree K?
341:   This conjecture holds when ϕ(K) ⊂ R [Fu14, Fu16]. A positive solution for general
342: case is announced in [FK]; some of the mathematicians who posed the conjecture do not
343: recognize that proof as complete.
344: Theorem 3.3 (M. Skopenkov, 2003 [Sk03]). Let K be a graph with k vertices and without
345: vertices of degree > 3. A simplicial map ϕ : K → S 1 ⊂ R2 is approximable by embeddings
346: if and only if the van Kampen obstruction v(ϕ) = 0 and ϕ(k) does not contain standard
347: windings of odd degree d 6= 1.
348:   Here the derivative ϕ(k) is defined analogously to §2.
349: Problem 3.4. Is analogue of Theorem 3.3 true for an arbitrary graph K and S 1 replaced
350: by an arbitrary graph G?
351:    A positive answer implies a positive solution of Conjecture 3.2.
352:    One can define the van Kampen obstruction for disjoinability (see §1.4) However, Ex-
353: ample 2.4 shows that its triviality is not sufficient for disjoinability, even for K = L = I,
354: i.e., for polygonal lines.
