423:                      4. Higher dimensional generalizations
424: 4.1. Approximability by embeddings in higher dimensions. A map f : K → M
425: between polyhedra (=bodies of simplicial complexes) is said to be embeddable in Rm if
426: there exists an embedding ψ : M → Rm for which ψ ◦ f is approximable by embeddings.
427: Theorem 4.1. (a) For each n every map f : I n → I n is embeddable in R2n (Sieklucki,
428: 1969 [Si69]).
429:   (b) For each n > 1 every map f : T n → T n between n-dimensional tori is embeddable
...
477: Problem 4.7 (Gromov, 2010 [Gr10]). Is it correct that if r is not a prime power, then for
478: each compact subset K of Rm for some m, having Lebesgue dimension dim K = (r − 1)n,
479: there is a continuous map K → Rnr each of whose point preimages contains less than r
480: points?
481:    We conjecture that the answer is ‘no’.
482:    Embeddability of compacta is related to approximability of maps from polyhedra by
483: embeddings. Analogously, Gromov’s problem is related to approximability of maps from
484: polyhedra by maps without r-fold points.
485:    Let K be a finite simplicial complex. A map f : K → Rm is an almost r-embedding
486: if f (σ1 ) ∩ · · · ∩ f (σr ) = ∅ whenever σ1 , . . . , σr are pairwise disjoint simplices of K.
487: Theorem 4.8 (I. Mabillard and U. Wagner, 2015, S. Avvakumov, I. Mabillard, A.
488: Skopenkov and U. Wagner, 2015 [MW, AMSW]). If n ≥ 2 and r is not a prime power,
489: then any n(r − 1)-complex is almost r-embeddable in Rnr .
490:    The van Kampen obstruction for almost r-embeddability of an n(r − 1)-complex to Rnr
491: is defined analogously to §3.2 above [MW].
492: Theorem 4.9 (I. Mabillard and U. Wagner, 2015, S. Avvakumov, I. Mabillard, A.
493: Skopenkov and U. Wagner, 2015 [MW, AMSW]). Suppose that n, r ≥ 2, n + r ≥ 5. An
494: n(r − 1)-complex is almost r-embeddable in Rnr if and only if the van Kampen obstruction
495: is zero.
496: Problem 4.10. Are analogous results true for approximability by almost r-embeddings,
497: or by maps f : K1 ⊔ . . . ⊔ Kr → Rnr such that f (K1 ) ∩ . . . ∩ f (Kr ) = ∅?

URL https://arxiv.org/pdf/0801.4393v1 FILE sources/8d019201b60024fa.txt
74:    One may wonder whether various algebraic objects associated to a subspace
75: arrangements are combinatorial invariants. Let K be a base field of characteristic
76: 0, and denote the coordinate ring of V by K[V ]. Terao defined the module of
77: derivations D(A) along a hyperplane arrangement A (see [42]). An arrangement is
78: called free if D(A) is a free K[V ]-module. Terao has conjectured that “freeness” is a
79: combinatorial property, i.e., whether D(A) is free is determined by its rank function.
80: Terao showed that free arrangements have the property that their characteristic
81: polynomial factors into linear polynomials (see [42]). One should point out that for
82: example the Hilbert series of the module D(A) is not a combinatorial invariant.
83:    In a recent paper, the author found an algebraic object which is a combinatorial
84: invariant for subspaceQarrangements. Let Jx ⊆ K[V ] be the vanishing ideal of
85: Vx ⊆ V and let J = x∈X Jx be the product ideal. The author showed in [12]
86: that the Hilbert series H(J, t) of J is a combinatorial invariant. For hyperplane
87: arrangements the Hilbert series of J is always equal to td /(1 − t)n and is therefore
88: not an interesting invariant. Let W be an arbitrary vector space and denote its
89: dual by W ⋆ . We can tensor all the spaces with W ⋆ . So let Jx (W ) ⊆ K[V       ⋆
90:                                                                         Q ⊗ W ] be
91:                                              ⋆           ⋆
92: the vanishing ideal of the subspace Vx ⊗ W of V ⊗ W and J(W ) = x∈A Jx (W ).
93: Then the Hilbert series H(J(W ), t) is an interesting invariant, even for hyperplane
94: arrangements. Moreover, since we have an action of GL(W ) on all the rings and
...
197: quasi-symmetric functions. But unlike F [X], it can distinguish between a loop and
198: an isthmus. Moreover, G[X] specializes to the Billera-Jia-Reiner quasi-symmetric
199: function F [X] as well as to H[X](q, t). We will also show that G[X] has the valu-
200: ative property with respect to polymatroid polytope decompositions in Section 8.
201: We question whether G[X] might be universal with this property.

205:             2. Symmetric functions associated to polymatroids
206:     In this section we will define the invariants H[X](q, t) and P[X].
207: 2.1. Discrete polymatroids.
208: Definition 2.1. A (discrete) polymatroid is a pair X := (X, rk) where X is a finite
209: set, and rk : Pow(X) → N = {0, 1, 2, . . . } is a function satisfying
210:      (1) rk(∅) = 0;
211:      (2) rk(A) ≤ rk(B) if A ⊆ B (nondecreasing);
212:      (3) rk(A ∪ B) + rk(A ∩ B) ≤ rk(A) + rk(B) (submodular).
213:

1232: 7.1. The Hopf algebras Mat and PolyMat . Although most of the Hopf algebras
1233: in this section can be defined over the integers Z, we will choose to define them
1234: over Q for simplicity. In [38] the matroid Hopf algebra Mat was introduced (see
1235: also [9, 10, 11]). This construction easily generalizes to polymatroids.
1236:    Let us first introduce the Hopf algebra of polymatroids, PolyMat . For a poly-
1237: matroid X = (X, rk), we denote its isomorphism class by [X]. As a Q-vector space,
1238: PolyMat has a basis consisting of all isomorphism classes of polymatroids. We
1239: define a product by
1240:                                 [X] · [Y] := [X ⊕ Y].

1242:                                  X

1707: 7.8. Speyer’s invariant. For a matroid X David Speyer defined an interesting
1708: polynomial gX (t). It has the multiplicative property (gX1 ⊕X2 (t) = gX1 (t)gX2 (t)),
1709: it is invariant under matroid-duality and has various other nice properties.
1710: Conjecture 7.8. The invariant G specializes to Speyer’s invariant.

1959: where the polymatroid comes from a subspace arrangement, we gave interpretations
1960: of the coefficients of these symmetric functions in terms of the Hilbert series and
1961: the minimal free resolution of the associated product ideal, and in terms of the
1962: polarized Schur functor. We hope for simililar interpretations and nonnegativity
1963: results in the case where the polymatroid is not realizable (Conjecture 5.7). We
1964: also defined a quasi-symmetric function G[X]. This invariant has many interesting
1965: properties, and it specializes to P[X], H[X] and to the Billera-Jia-Reiner quasi-
1966: symmetric function F [X]. We would like to know whether G[X] specializes to
1967: Speyer’s invariant in [40] (Conjecture 7.8). The invariant G behaves valuatively
1968: with respect to (poly-)matroid base polytope decompositions. We wonder whether
1969: G is universal with this property (Conjecture 8.5).

4537:    Let us try to describe Z (k [ G ]) more explicitly. For this we recall some clas-
4538: sical notations from group theory:
4539: 
4540:   Definition 3.9.4. Let G be a group.
4541:     (a) Two elements x and y of G are said to be conjugate if there exists a g ∈ G
4542:   such that y = gxg−1 . In this case, we write x ∼ y.
4543:     (b) The binary relation ∼ on G is an equivalence relation, and is known as
4544:   conjugacy. Its equivalence classes are called the conjugacy classes of G. Thus,
4545:   each element of G belongs to exactly one conjugacy class.
4546:     (c) If C is a finite conjugacy class of G, then we define zC to be the sum
4547:    ∑ c in k [ G ]. This is called a conjugacy class sum of G. (We require C to be
4548:   c∈C
4549:   finite, since infinite sums do not make sense in k [ G ]. However, this will not
4550:   be important for us in the case G = Sn , since Sn is finite.)
4551: 
4552:   Here are some examples:
4553: 
4554:    • If G is an abelian group, then conjugacy in G is just equality (i.e., we have
4555:      x ∼ y if and only if x = y), so all the conjugacy classes are singletons
4556:      { x } (with x ∈ G), and thus the conjugacy class sums are just the standard
4557:      basis vectors x = ex of k [ G ].

5563: 3.10.4. Excedance and antiexcedance elements
5564: Here is something even stranger: an open problem (albeit one that has not been
5565: advertised very widely). We again begin with a combinatorial definition:
5566:   Definition 3.10.18. Let w ∈ Sn be a permutation. Then, we define the two
5567:   numbers
5568: 
5569:                exc w := (# of i ∈ [n] such that w (i ) > i )        and
5570:               anxc w := (# of i ∈ [n] such that w (i ) < i ) .
5571: 
5572:   These numbers exc w and anxc w are called the excedance number and the
5573:   antiexcedance number of w, respectively.
5574: 
5575: Symmetric group algebras, Spring 2024, version 2025-07-27                                   page 106
5576: 
5577: 
5578:   Example 3.10.19. Let w = oln (3524176). Then, exc w = 3 and anxc w = 3.

5594:   and X a,b = 0 for all remaining choices of a, b ∈ N.
5595: 
5596:   Conjecture 3.10.22. These elements X a,b for all a, b ∈ N commute (for fixed
5597:   n). In other words, X a,b Xc,d = Xc,d X a,b for all a, b, c, d ∈ N.
5598: 
5599:    This conjecture was inspired by a conversation with Theo Douvropoulos; I
5600: have checked it for all n ≤ 8.
5601:    We can restate this conjecture explicitly (by comparing coefficients, just as in
5602: Remark 3.10.11): It is saying that for any permutation w ∈ Sn and any numbers
5603: a, b, c, d ∈ N, we have
5604: 
5605:      (# of pairs (u, v) ∈ Sn × Sn with w = uv
5606:             and exc u = a and anxc u = b and exc v = c and anxc v = d)
5607:      = (# of pairs (u, v) ∈ Sn × Sn with w = vu
5608:             and exc u = a and anxc u = b and exc v = c and anxc v = d) .
5609: 
5610: (Indeed, this equality is what we obtain if we compare the coefficients of w on
5611: both sides of the equality X a,b Xc,d = Xc,d X a,b .)
5612:    For a change, the elements X a,b are not (in general) annihilated by polynomi-

7887: is actually one of the best ways to study groups and algebras. Viewing groups
7888: or algebras through the prism of their actions is how many of the above results
7889: were first discovered, and in some cases (e.g., for Theorem 3.10.10 (b)) it is the

7896: 4.1. Group actions and G-sets
7897:   Convention 4.1.1. For the entirety of Section 4.1, we let G be a group (not
7898:   necessarily finite).
7899: 
7900: 4.1.1. Motivation
7901: Here is a slightly provocative slogan: Group elements want to be permutations.
7902: I.e., a group wants to act.
7903:    This might sound strange. After all, we nowadays define groups as abstract
7904: structures consisting of disembodied objects that multiply via a multiplication
7905: table. But the elements of the (historically) first groups ever defined (by Galois
7906: in the early 19th century) were permutations of sets. That is, the first groups
7907: ever defined were subgroups of symmetric groups.
...
8625: and 2 have been chosen to disambiguate the “clones” coming from X from the
8626: “clones” coming from Y (so that, even if the sets X and Y have an element z
8627: in common, the two “clones” (z, 1) and (z, 2) will be distinguishable in X ⊔ Y).
8628: When the sets X and Y are themselves disjoint, we can (by abuse of notation)
8629: identify the clones ( x, 1) and (y, 2) with the elements x and y themselves, which
8630: allows us to pretend that X ⊔ Y is the literal union X ∪ Y in this case.

27251:    Theorem 5.17.19. Let k be a field of characteristic 0. Let G be a finite group.
27252:    Let J1 , J2 , . . . , Jm be all the irreducible representations of G over k, each listed
27253:    exactly once (up to isomorphism). Then:
27254:       (a) The elements X J1 , X J2 , . . . , X Jm of Z (k [ G ]) are linearly independent.
27255:       (b) If k is algebraically closed, then (X J1 , X J2 , . . . , X Jm ) is a basis of Z (k [ G ]).
27256: 
27257:   Theorem 5.17.19 (b) is proved in most texts on representation theory – e.g.,
27258: in [EGHetc11, Theorem 4.2.1]. A proof of the whole Theorem 5.17.19 (and, in
27259: fact, of a more general fact) can be found in [Lorenz18, Theorem 1.44]. (Note
27260: that both of these sources work not with the elements X Jk but rather with the
27261: characters χ Jk themselves, which belong to the vector space of class functions118
27262: on k [ G ]. This has the advantage of working in a more general setting than
27263: just group algebras. But this can easily be translated into our language when
27264: dealing with a group algebra.)

28520: 5.17.8. Characters of small symmetric groups
28521: Theorem 5.17.28 provides an easy way to compute the elements Xλ when k =
28522: Q. Using Lemma 5.17.33, we can then extend these formulas for Xλ to k = Z
28523: and thence to arbitrary rings k. Then we can compute all character values
28524: χS λ ( g) by taking appropriate coefficients of Xλ .
28525:   We shall now give tables of these character values for the first five symmetric
28526: groups S0 , S1 , S2 , S3 , S4 . The tables should be read as follows:
28527:

28529:      the partitions λ of n. We denote the character χS λ of a Specht module S λ
28530:      by χλ . Note that χ(n) = χtriv (see Example 5.17.7) and χ(1n ) = χsign (see
28531:      Example 5.17.8).

81: T with ℓ(T ) leaves, ℓ(T2)+1 is the brushing number when ℓ(T ) is odd. However, when ℓ(T )
82: is even the brushing number is either ℓ(T2 ) or ℓ(T2 ) + 1, implying that trees with an even
83: number of leaves are divided into two groups depending on their brushing number. Penso,
84: Rautenbach and Ribeiro de Almeida subsequently proved that trees with ℓ(T ) leaves and
85: brushing number ℓ(T2 ) can be recognised efficiently [14].
86:     In the present paper (which is based on thesis given in [10]) we generalise the notion
87: of brushing to directed graphs. In Section 2, we formulate the basic rules of brushing for
88: directed graphs, along with defining necessary concepts and notation. In Section 3, the
89: 
90: 
91:                                               2
92: 
93: brushing number of directed acyclic graphs is discussed in detail. We prove that every
94:                                                                 2
95: transitive tournament on n ≥ 3 vertices has brushing number ⌊ n4 ⌋ (see Theorem 3.3). We
96: use this result to establish an upper bound on the brushing number of directed acyclic
97: graphs in general (see Theorem 3.9). In Section 4, we take several other types of directed
98: graphs into consideration and exactly determine the brushing numbers of complete directed
99: graphs, rooted trees, and rotational tournaments. We also show that other concepts like
100: path decomposition and the transpose of a directed graph are associated with the brushing
101: number. In the final section we present some discussion and questions for future inquiry.

109: v clean v, and each outgoing arc incident with v is traversed by at least one brush that is
110: fired from v, thereby cleaning the dirty outgoing arcs that were incident with v. At the end
111: of the time step, each brush from the firing vertex v moves to the vertex adjacent to v at the
112: endpoint of the arc it traversed. Excess brushes are allowed to remain at v. Arcs are allowed
113: to be traversed by more than one brush, and multiple brushes can simultaneously traverse
114: an arc; however each arc can be traversed during at most one step of the cleaning process.
115: We allow vertices to fire one at a time in a sequence. In this model we do not fire multiple
116: vertices that are ready to fire at the same time step (although a way of simultaneously firing
117: vertices when brushing undirected graphs is described as parallel dispersal mode in [12]).
118: Before beginning the process of cleaning we decide the sequence in which the vertices fire.
119: Since we allow multiple brushes to traverse an arc, it is possible that a vertex which fires
120: later in the sequence might accumulate brushes from vertices which have been fired earlier
121: in the sequence.
122:     As an example refer to the directed graph G in Figure 1. At the beginning of the process
123: of cleaning this graph G, place one brush at each of the vertices v1 , v2 and v3 . Then v1 , v2
124: and v3 fire sequentially and v4 accumulates three brushes. Next v4 fires and these three
125: brushes traverse the arc (v4 , v5 ). Then v5 receives three brushes and v5 fires, resulting in the
126: vertices v6 , v7 and v8 getting one brush each. Then v6 , v7 and v8 fire sequentially; brushes
127: stay at these vertices as these are sink vertices. Thus we have cleaned the graph G with
128: three brushes.
129:     In general, the process of cleaning terminates when a clean graph is obtained, or else the

1206: a new cleaning model for brushing directed graphs; the number of brushes needed to clean
1207: a directed graph G under this restricted scenario would serve as an upper bound on our
1208: brushing number B(G).
1209:     In this paper our focus was on minimising the number of brushes that can be used to
1210: clean a given directed graph. It remains as an open problem to explore what is the most
1211: efficient cleaning sequence for a directed graph, that is the one that minimises the number of
1212: brushes used as well as minimises the time taken to clean the graph when allowing multiple
1213: vertices that are ready to fire to do so at the same time step. A way of firing brushes similar
1214: to this for undirected graphs is described as parallel dispersal mode in [12].

281: Note we do not require X = i∈[n] Ui ; indeed, ∅ ∈ code(U, X) if and only if i∈[n] Ui ( X. A
282: code C is called open convex if there exists a collection U of open convex sets Ui , i = 1, . . . , n,
283: and an open convex set X ⊆ Rd , such that C = code(U, X), for some d. We will refer to
284: open convex codes simply as convex codes.
285: Example 2.4. Let Ui denote the open half-space on the positive side of hyperplane Hi in
286: Figure 2(a), i.e. Ui = {x ∈ R2 | `i (x) > 0}. Then, code(U, Rn ) is the combinatorial code

288:   Morphisms of combinatorial codes were defined in [18] in terms of trunks. For σ ⊆ [n],
289: the trunk of σ in C is the set of codewords which contain σ,
290:                                     TkC (σ) := {τ ∈ C | σ ⊆ τ }.
291: A subset of C is a trunk if it is equal to TkC (σ) for some σ ⊆ [n] or if it is empty. A simple
292: trunk is the trunk of a singleton set. A map f : C → D is a morphism of codes if the
293: preimage of each trunk of D is a trunk of C . Any set of trunks T1 , . . . , Tm ⊆ C defines a
294: morphism by f (σ) := {i | σ ∈ Ti }, and any code morphism f : C → D can be obtained in
295: this way [18, Proposition 2.12]. The class of codes, together with these morphisms, forms
296: the category Code.
297:    A subset σ ⊆ [n] can be encoded as a point c ∈ Fn2 by setting ci = 1 for i ∈ σ and ci = 0
298:       / σ. Hence, a code C ⊆ 2[n] can equivalently be thought of as a variety C ⊆ Fn2 . The
...
819:    In [18], Jeffs shows that the image of a convex code under a code morphism, as well as any
820: trunk of a convex code, is itself a convex code. From this observation, he defines the poset
821: of isomorphism classes of codes PCode in which convex codes form a down-set: if D ≤ C
822: in PCode and C is convex, then so is D. In this section, we generalize this statement to
823: intersection-closed families, of which open convex subsets of Rd is one example. A family
824: F of subsets of a topological space is called intersection-closed if it is closed under finite
825: intersections and contains the empty set. We say that a neural code C is F-realizable if
826: C = code(U, X) for some U ⊆ F and X ∈ F. For instance, a neural code is convex if and
827: only if it is F realizable for the set F of convex open subsets of some Rd . Then, using this
828: results, we prove Theorem 2.
829:    We recall some relevant details. Two codes C and D are isomorphic if there is a bijective
830: code morphism f : C → D whose inverse is also a code morphism. Codes can be quasi-
831: ordered by setting D ≤ C if D = f (C ) for some code morphism f , or if D is a trunk of C .
832: The poset of isomorphism classes of codes induced by this order is denoted PCode .
833: Proposition 4.1. For any intersection closed family F, if C is F-realizable and D ≤ C ,
834: then D is F-realizable.
835: Proof. We first check the case D = f (C ). This closely follows the proof of Theorem 1.4 in
836: [18], since the only property of convex sets this proof uses is that the family of open convex
837: subsets of Rd is closed under finite intersection. We repeat the details here. Let C ⊆ 2[n] ,
838: D ⊆ 2[m] , and T1 , . . . , Tm be the trunks in C that define the morphism f : C → D. Let
839: U1 , . . . , Un ⊆ F be an F-realization of C .

1299: 6.1. Functorial questions. The maps W+ and L+ established analogies between structures
1300: of oriented matroids and neural codes. Topes and covectors are translated into the codewords,
1301: and signed circuits are mapped to the combinatorial relations. This leads us to the following
1302: natural question:
1303: Question 6.1. Do W+ and L+ map other matroid features to meaningful structures asso-
1304: ciated to neural codes? In particular, do the chirotope, rank function, and convex closure
1305: function have a natural interpretation when mapped to general neural codes?
1306:    The paper focused on the category of oriented matroids, since they have a well-established
1307: notion of morphisms (strong maps) and since they are extensively-studied. However, there
1308: is also a notion of “affine strong maps” defined in [15] that may serve to turn affine oriented
1309: matroids into a category. This might also admit a natural functor to neural codes. Ad-
1310: ditionally, the recently defined objects COM’s (which stands for both conditional oriented
1311: matroids and complexes of oriented matroids) [1] are a natural place to try to extend strong
1312: maps next.
1313: Question 6.2. Can affine oriented matroids with affine strong maps be embedded in Code?
1314: Can strong maps be defined for COM’s in such a way that the resulting category can be
1315: embedded in Code?
1316:  While strong maps are more frequently used as morphisms of oriented matroids, weak
1317: maps are the next best option.
1318: Question 6.3. Can the category of oriented matroids with morphisms given by weak maps
1319: be embedded in Code?
...
1322: 
1323:    Finally, we observe that codes lying below oriented matroids in PCode are of special
1324: interest. While general neural codes are not required to satisfy any axioms, the codes below
1325: oriented matroids may be more tractable to combinatorial description.
1326: Question 6.4. Can the class of neural codes below oriented matroids be characterized by a
1327: set of combinatorial axioms?
1328:    If this question is answered in the affirmative, then these codes can be thought of as
1329: “partial oriented matroids.” Suppose that C ⊆ 2[n] is a code and M is an oriented matroid
1330: on ground set [N ] such that C = f (L+ M); then, we obtain constraints on the set of covectors
1331: of M. Each included codeword σ ∈ C implies existence of a preimage covector in M, and
1332: each excluded codeword τ ∈  / C implies a set of forbidden covectors which may not be in M.
1333: The oriented matroids satisfying these constraints can then be said to be “completions” of
1334: the partial oriented matroid.
1335: 
1336: 6.2. Is the missing axiom of convex codes also lost forever? Just as we wish to
1337: characterize codes lying below oriented matroids with a set of combinatorial axioms, we
1338: might also wish to characterize convex codes using a set of combinatorial axioms. However,
1339: this is likely not possible. In [22], Mayhew, Newman, and Whittle show that “the missing
1340: axiom of matroid theory is lost forever.” Slightly more formally, they show that there is no
1341: sentence characterizing representability in the monadic second order language M S0 , which
1342: is strong enough to state the standard matroid axioms. Roughly, this means that there is no
...
1343: “combinatorial” characterization of representability, or no characterization of representability
1344: in the language of the other matroid axioms.
1345:    Because we have found strong connections between representability and convexity, it is
1346: natural to ask whether a similar statement can be proven for convex codes.
1347: Question 6.5. Is there a natural language in which we can state “combinatorial” properties
1348: of neural codes, in analogy with the M S0 for matroids? If so, is it possible to characterize
1349: convexity in this language?
1350: 6.3. Computational questions. While we have shown that the convex code decision prob-
1351: lem is ∃R-hard, we have not actually shown that the convex code decision problem lies in
1352: ∃R, or is even algorithmically decidable. A similar problem, that of determining whether a
1353: code has a good cover realization, is undecidable by [4, Theorem 4.5]. Here, the distinction
1354: between codes with good cover realizations and convex realizations may be significant. For
1355: instance, while there is an algorithm to decide whether, for any given d, a simplicial complex
1356: is the nerve of convex open subsets of Rd , for each d ≥ 5, it is algorithmically undecidable
1357: whether a simplicial complex is the nerve of a good cover in Rd [30].
1358:    We outline a possible path towards resolving [4, Question 4.5], which asks whether there
1359: is an algorithm which decides whether a code is convex. Our approach hinges on Theorem 2:
1360: a code is polytope convex if and only if it lies below a representable oriented matroid. A
1361: first step towards solving the convex code decision problem is answering the following open
1362: question:
1363: Question 6.6. Can every convex code be realized with convex polytopes?
...
1371:   If this conjecture holds, then we can replace the problem of determining whether a code is
1372: convex with the problem of determining whether a code lies below a representable matroid.
1373: We only need to enumerate matroids above the code, and then check these matroids for
1374: representability.
1375: Question 6.7. Given a code C , is there an algorithm to enumerate the set of oriented
1376: matroids M which lie above C ?
1377:   One way to find oriented matroids above a code C is to travel step-by-step up the poset
1378: PCode . While there is a straightforward algorithm to enumerate the O(n) codes which are
1379: covered by a code C ⊆ 2[n] in PCode [17], we do not know of a straightforward way to
1380: characterize the codes which cover C . If we can characterize these codes as well, we may be
1381: able to find a way to “climb up” towards an oriented matroid. Alternatively, we can use the
1382: “partial oriented matroid” perspective described above to obtain a set of constraints that
1383: must be obeyed by any oriented matroid above this code. Then we can look for a matroid
1384: satisfying these constraints.
1385:   Both of these approaches depend on the minimal size of the ground set of oriented matroids
1386: that lie above C in PCode . Let
1387:                                                              
1388:                               M (n) = max      min    |E(M)|
1389:                                        C ⊆2[n]   C ≤L (M)
1390:                                                  +

1401: be the smallest N such that any code C on n neurons below a representable oriented matroid
1402: lies below a representable oriented matroid with ground set of size at most N . Clearly,
1403: M (n) ≤ H(n), since any representable matroid is a matroid.
1404: Question 6.8. Describe the growth of M (n) and H(n) as functions of n. Are they equal?
1405:    Note that if H(n) is a computable function of n, and Question 6.6 is answered in the
1406: affirmative, then the convex code decision problem is decidable.
1407: 6.4. Other questions in geometric combinatorics. Many classic theorems about convex
1408: sets, such as Helly’s theorem, Radon’s theorem, and Caratheodory’s theorem, have oriented
1409: matroid analogues. In some way, we can view our Theorem 3 as an oriented matroid version
1410: of Jeffs’ sunflower theorem [17, Theorem 1.1]. The fact that the non-convex codes constructed
1411: from the sunflower theorem do not lie below oriented matroids shows us that there is some
1412: fact about oriented matroids underlying the sunflower theorem.
1413: Question 6.9. Is there a natural oriented matroid version of Jeffs’ sunflower theorem?
1414:   Proposition 4.3 stated that if M is an oriented matroid, the code L(M) has no local
1415: obstructions. That is, for any σ ∈ ∆(L+ M) \ L+ M, linkσ (∆(L+ M)) is contractible. This
1416: result can also be found in [10], where is is phrased as a result about the simplicial complex
1417: 
1418:                     ORIENTED MATROIDS AND COMBINATORIAL NEURAL CODES                                       29
1419: 
1420: ∆acyclic (M). Something stronger holds for representable oriented matroids: by [4, Theorem
...
1421: 5.10], if M is a representable oriented matroid, and σ ∈ ∆(L+ M)\L+ M, then linkσ (L+ M)
1422: must be collapsible. Expanding upon this work, [19] gives stronger conditions that the link
1423: of a missing codeword in a convex code must satisfy.
1424:    We ask whether this holds for all oriented matroids:
1425: Question 6.10. If M is an oriented matroid, and σ ∈ ∆(L+ M) \ L+ M, is linkσ (∆(L+ M))
1426: collapsible? More generally, which simplicial complexes can arise as linkσ (∆(L+ M)) for
1427: σ ∈ ∆(L+ M) \ L+ M?
1428:   If not, then the non-collapsibility of linkσ (∆(L+ M)) gives a new “signature” of non-
1429: representability.

124: of neural codes form a partially ordered set denoted PCode , introduced in [19]. Roughly,
125: C ≤ D if there is a way to construct a realization for C using a realization of D, in which
126: case we say C is a minor of D. We extend a result of [19] and use it to prove that all
127: codes lying below codes of representable oriented matroids have realizations with interiors
128: of convex polytopes, which we call open polytope convex. Further, the converse also holds:
129: Theorem 1. A code is open polytope convex if and only if it is a minor of L+ M for some
130: representable oriented matroid M.
131:    Since every code which has a convex realization in the plane has a realization with convex
132: polygons [4], this result gives a full characterization of planar convex codes in terms of
133: representable oriented matroids. In higher dimensions, it is not yet known whether every
134: convex code has a realization with convex polytopes. If this does hold, then Theorem 2
135: would give a full characterization of convex codes in terms of representable oriented matroids.
136: Without this result, we can still categorize non-convex codes: if a code is not convex, then
137: either it does not lie below any oriented matroid in PCode , or it lies below non-representable
138: matroids only. There are many known examples of non-convex codes [5, 6, 18, 19, 23], and
139: we show that many of these fall into the first category: they are non-convex because they
140: are not below any oriented matroids in PCode . For instance, codes with topological local
141: obstructions do not lie below oriented matroids. Furthermore, sunflower codes [18], a well
142: known family of non-convex codes with no local obstructions also do not lie below oriented
143: matroids.
144: Theorem 2. The non-convex codes with no local obstructions constructed via sunflowers

315: Note we do not require X = i∈[n] Ui ; indeed, ∅ ∈ code(U, X) if and only if i∈[n] Ui ( X.
316: A code C is called open convex if there exists a collection U = {U1 , . . . , Un } of open convex
317: sets and an open convex set X ⊆ Rd , such that C = code(U, X), for some d. We will refer
318: to open convex codes simply as convex codes.
319: Example 2.4. Let Ui denote the open half-space on the positive side of hyperplane Hi in
320: Figure 2(a), i.e. Ui = {x ∈ R2 | `i (x) > 0}. Then, code(U, Rn ) is the combinatorial code
321: with codewords as labeled in Figure 2(b).
322:   Morphisms of combinatorial codes were defined in [19] in terms of trunks. For σ ⊆ [n],
323: the trunk of σ in C is the set of codewords which contain σ,
324:                                      TkC (σ) := {τ ∈ C | σ ⊆ τ }.
325: A subset of C is a trunk if it is equal to TkC (σ) for some σ ⊆ [n] or if it is empty. A
326: simple trunk is the trunk of a singleton set. A map f : C → D is a morphism of codes if
327: the preimage of each trunk of D is a trunk of C . Any set of trunks T1 , . . . , Tm ⊆ C defines
328: a morphism f : C → 2[m] by f (σ) := {i | σ ∈ Ti }, and any code morphism f : C → D
329: can be obtained in this way [19, Proposition 2.12]. The class of codes, together with these
330: morphisms, forms the category Code.
331:    A subset σ ⊆ [n] can be encoded as a point c ∈ Fn2 by setting ci = 1 for i ∈ σ and ci = 0
332:       / σ. Hence, a code C ⊆ 2[n] can equivalently be thought of as a variety C ⊆ Fn2 . The
...
377:    In [19], Jeffs shows that the image of a convex code under a code morphism, as well as any
378: trunk of a convex code, is itself a convex code. From this observation, he defines the poset
379: of isomorphism classes of codes PCode in which convex codes form a down-set: if D ≤ C
380: in PCode and C is convex, then so is D. In this section, we generalize this statement to
381: intersection-closed families, of which open convex subsets of Rd is one example. A family
382: F of subsets of a topological space is called intersection-closed if it is closed under finite
383: intersections and contains the empty set. We say that a neural code C is F-realizable if
384: C = code(U, X) for some U ⊆ F and X ∈ F. For instance, a neural code is convex if and
385: only if it is F-realizable for the set F of convex open subsets of some Rd . Then, using this
386: result, we prove Theorem 2.
387:    We recall the relevant definitions. Two codes C and D are isomorphic if there is a bijective
388: code morphism f : C → D whose inverse is also a code morphism. If there is a sequence
389: of codes C = C0 , C1 , . . . , Ck = D such that each successive code is either the image of a
390: morphism from or a trunk of the preceding code, we say D is a minor of C [20]. Codes are
391: then quasi-ordered by setting D ≤ C if D is a minor of C . The poset of isomorphism classes
392: of codes induced by this order is denoted PCode .
393: Proposition 3.1. For any intersection closed family F, if C is F-realizable and D ≤ C ,
394: then D is F-realizable.
395: Proof. We first check the case D = f (C ). This closely follows the proof of Theorem 1.4 in
396: [19], since the only property of convex sets this proof uses is that the family of open convex
397: subsets of Rd is closed under finite intersection. We repeat the details here. Let C ⊆ 2[n] ,
...
472:    Armed with these results, we look at the codes of oriented matroids and those lying
473: below them. In particular, we examine the intersection-closed family of interiors of convex
474: polytopes in Rn . We say that a code C is polytope convex if there exists a collection of
475: interiors of convex polytopes P = {P1 , . . . , Pn } and a bounding convex polytope X such that
476: C = code(P, X). Note that polytope convex codes are open convex. Proposition 3.1 implies
477: that the image of any polytope code under a surjective morphism is also a polytope code.
478: Thus, since the codes of representable oriented matroids correspond to codes of hyperplane
479: arrangements, all codes which lie below a representable oriented matroid are polytope codes.
480: We prove the converse, showing that every polytope code is itself the image of the code of an
481: oriented matroid under some surjective morphism. This demonstrates that polytope codes
482: are a down-set generated by the set of representable oriented matroid codes.
483:    We begin by noting that codes below oriented matroids have no local obstructions. This
484: result appears in different language in [11]. We flesh this out.
485: Proposition 3.3. Let M be an oriented matroid. If C ≤ L+ M in PCode , C is a good cover
486: code, and thus has no local obstructions.
487: Proof. Edelman, Reiner, and Welker define a simplicial complex ∆acyclic (M) which is iden-
488: tical to ∆(L+ M) [11]. Proposition 11 and Lemma 13 of [11] establish that if σ ∈ ∆(L+ M) \
489: L+ M, then linkσ ∆(L+ M) is contractible. Thus, L+ M has no local obstructions, and is
490: therefore a good cover code. By Corollary 3.2, good cover codes form a down-set in PCode ,
491: so if C ≤ L+ M in PCode , then C has no local obstructions.                             
492: Theorem 2. A code C is polytope convex if and only if there exists a representable oriented

517:    In dimension two, every convex code admits a realization with convex polytopes [4]. Thus,
518: in dimension two, we can strengthen Theorem 2 as follows:
519: Corollary 3.4. A code C including the empty codeword is open convex with minimal
520: embedding dimension two if and only if there exists a representable affine oriented matroid
521: (M, g) of rank three such that C ≤ TkL+ (M) (g).
522: Proof. First suppose C is a planar convex code. By intersecting each of the convex sets
523: with the same sufficiently large ball, we obtain a realization of C by bounded convex sets.
524: Then by Theorem 1 of [4], C has a realization with interiors of convex polygons in R2 . These
525: convex polygons are intersections of half-spaces. Let (M, g) be the affine oriented matroid of
526: the corresponding affine, oriented hyperplane arrangement. Notice that M is representable
527: and rank three, since it arises from the centralization of a hyperplane arrangement in R2 .
528: The covectors in the affine space of (M, g) each contain g in their positive part. Then by
529: the argument used in the proof of Theorem 2, C ≤ TkL+ (M) (g).
530:    Now suppose that C ≤ TkL+ (M) (g), where (M, g) is a representable affine oriented matroid
531: of rank two. Then by Theorem 2, C has a realization with intersections of half spaces in R2 ,
532: and is thus convex with minimal embedding dimension two.                                    

534:                                         4. Non-convex codes
535:   In dimensions three or higher, it is unknown whether every convex code has a realization
...
704: given an alternate proof that these codes do not have realizations with interiors of convex
705: polytopes. This proof is significantly different in structure than the original proof that these
706: codes are not convex using Theorem 4.1, which is in turn proved by induction on dimension.
707: In contrast, our proof makes no reference to rank or dimension, and does not use induction.
708: While the codes {Cn }n≥2 are not open convex, they do have realizations with closed convex
709: sets, which can even be chosen to be (non-full dimensional) closed convex polytopes. Notice
710: that Theorem 2 establishes that if C has a realization with interiors of convex polytopes,
711: then C ≤ L+ M. However, the fact that a code has a realization with closed convex polytopes
712: does not guarantee this. Further, in showing that these codes do not lie below any oriented
713: matroids at all, we have established that, even while these codes are good cover codes, their
714: obstructions to convexity are somehow still topological in nature.
715: 4.2. Representability and convexity. Having exhibited that many well-known non-convex
716: codes do not lie below any oriented matroids at all, we now exhibit a family of non-convex
717: codes which lie below non-representable oriented matroids. For each uniform, rank 3 affine
718: oriented matroid (M, g), we construct a code C (M, g) which is convex if and only if M is
719: representable (recall a uniform oriented matroid is one in which all circuits have the same
720: cardinality). Moreover, this code is always the image of an oriented matroid under a code
721: morphism.
722:   Consider a uniform, affine oriented matroid (M, g) of rank 3. A pseudoline is a simple
723: curve L in R2 , unbounded in both directions, which partitions the plane into unbounded
724: pieces R2 = L+ t L t L− . By the topological representation theorem ([2, Section 1.3],[12]),
...
1492: Is it possible to give a similar characterization for convex neural codes, or at least for codes
1493: which lie below oriented matroids in PCode ? While general neural codes are not required to
1494: satisfy any axioms, the codes below oriented matroids may be more tractable to combinato-
1495: rial description.
1496: Question 6.1. Can the class of neural codes below oriented matroids be characterized by a
1497: set of combinatorial axioms?
1498:    The functorial view we introduced in Section 5 may be helpful in finding this characteri-
1499: zation. If this question is answered in the affirmative, then these codes can be thought of as
1500: “partial oriented matroids.” Suppose that C ⊆ 2[n] is a code and M is an oriented matroid
1501: on ground set [N ] such that C = f (L+ M); then, we obtain constraints on the set of covectors
1502: of M. Each included codeword σ ∈ C implies existence of a preimage covector in M, and
1503: each excluded codeword τ ∈   / C implies a set of forbidden covectors which may not be in M.
1504: The oriented matroids satisfying these constraints can then be said to be “completions” of
1505: the partial oriented matroid.
1506:    Just as we wish to characterize codes lying below oriented matroids with a set of combina-
1507: torial axioms, we might also wish to characterize convex codes using a set of combinatorial
1508: axioms. However, this is likely not possible. In [24], Mayhew, Newman, and Whittle show
1509: that “the missing axiom of matroid theory is lost forever.” Slightly more formally, they show
1510: that there is no sentence characterizing representability in the monadic second order language
1511: M S0 , which is strong enough to state the standard matroid axioms. Roughly, this means
1512: that there is no “combinatorial” characterization of representability, or no characterization
...
1512: that there is no “combinatorial” characterization of representability, or no characterization
1513: of representability in the language of the other matroid axioms.
1514:    Because we have found strong connections between representability and convexity, it is
1515: natural to ask whether a similar statement can be proven for convex codes.
1516: Question 6.2. Is there a natural language in which we can state “combinatorial” properties
1517: of neural codes, in analogy with the M S0 for matroids? If so, is it possible to characterize
1518: convexity in this language?
1519: 6.2. Computational questions. While we have shown that the convex code decision prob-
1520: lem is ∃R-hard, we have not actually shown that the convex code decision problem lies in
1521: ∃R, or is even algorithmically decidable. A similar problem, that of determining whether a
1522: code has a good cover realization, is undecidable by [5, Theorem 4.5]. Here, the distinction
1523: between codes with good cover realizations and convex realizations may be significant. For
1524: instance, while there is an algorithm to decide whether, for any given d, a simplicial complex
1525: 
1526:                  ORIENTED MATROIDS AND COMBINATORIAL NEURAL CODES                           32
1527: 
1528: is the nerve of convex open subsets of Rd , for each d ≥ 5, it is algorithmically undecidable
1529: whether a simplicial complex is the nerve of a good cover in Rd [35].
1530:    We outline a possible path towards resolving [5, Question 4.5], which asks whether there
1531: is an algorithm which decides whether a code is convex. Our approach hinges on Theorem 2:
1532: a code is polytope convex if and only if it lies below a representable oriented matroid. A
...
1531: is an algorithm which decides whether a code is convex. Our approach hinges on Theorem 2:
1532: a code is polytope convex if and only if it lies below a representable oriented matroid. A
1533: first step towards solving the convex code decision problem is answering the following open
1534: question:
1535: Question 6.3. Can every convex code be realized with convex polytopes?
1536:    In dimension two, this question has an affirmative answer, as a result, the class of planar
1537: convex codes is decidable [4]. If this holds in all dimensions, then our Theorem 2 becomes
1538: strengthened to the following:
1539: Conjecture. A code C is convex if and only if C ≤ L+ M for M a representable oriented
1540: matroid.
1541:   If this conjecture holds, then we can replace the problem of determining whether a code is
1542: convex with the problem of determining whether a code lies below a representable matroid.
1543: We only need to consider the set of minor-minimal matroids lying above the code, and check
1544: these matroids for representability.
1545: Question 6.4. Given a code C , is the set of minor-minimal matroids above C finite? If so,
1546: is there an efficient algorithm to enumerate them?
1547:   One way to find oriented matroids above a code C is to travel step-by-step up the poset
1548: PCode . While there is a straightforward algorithm to enumerate the O(n) codes which are
1549: covered by a code C ⊆ 2[n] in PCode [18], we do not know of a straightforward way to
1550: characterize the codes which cover C . If we can characterize these codes as well, we may be
1551: able to find a way to “climb up” towards an oriented matroid. Alternatively, we can use the
...

1570: be the smallest N such that any code C on n neurons below a representable oriented matroid
1571: lies below a representable oriented matroid with ground set of size at most N . Clearly,
1572: M (n) ≤ H(n), since any representable matroid is a matroid.
1573: Question 6.5. Describe the growth of M (n) and H(n) as functions of n. Are they equal?

1577:    Note that if H(n) is a computable function of n, and Question 6.3 is answered in the
1578: affirmative, then the convex code decision problem is decidable.
1579: 6.3. Other questions in geometric combinatorics. Many classic theorems about convex
1580: sets, such as Helly’s theorem, Radon’s theorem, and Caratheodory’s theorem, have oriented
1581: matroid analogues. In some way, we can view our Theorem 2 as an oriented matroid version
1582: of Jeffs’ sunflower theorem [18, Theorem 1.1]. The fact that the non-convex codes constructed
1583: from the sunflower theorem do not lie below oriented matroids shows us that there is some
1584: fact about oriented matroids underlying the sunflower theorem.
1585: Question 6.6. Is there a natural oriented matroid version of Jeffs’ sunflower theorem?
1586:    Proposition 3.3 stated that if M is an oriented matroid, the code L+ M has no local
1587: obstructions. That is, for any σ ∈ ∆(L+ M) \ L+ M, linkσ (∆(L+ M)) is contractible. This
1588: result can also be found in [11], where is is phrased as a result about the simplicial complex
1589: ∆acyclic (M). Something stronger holds for representable oriented matroids: by [5, Theorem
...
1590: 5.10], if M is a representable oriented matroid, and σ ∈ ∆(L+ M)\L+ M, then linkσ (L+ M)
1591: must be collapsible. Expanding upon this work, [22] gives stronger conditions that the link
1592: of a missing codeword in a convex code must satisfy.
1593:    We ask whether this holds for all oriented matroids:
1594: Question 6.7. If M is an oriented matroid, and σ ∈ ∆(L+ M) \ L+ M, is linkσ (∆(L+ M))
1595: collapsible? More generally, which simplicial complexes can arise as linkσ (∆(L+ M)) for
1596: σ ∈ ∆(L+ M) \ L+ M?
1597:   If not, then the non-collapsibility of linkσ (∆(L+ M)) gives a new “signature” of non-
1598: representability.
1599: 6.4. Functorial questions. The maps W+ and L+ established analogies between structures
1600: of oriented matroids and neural codes. Topes and covectors are translated into the codewords,
1601: and signed circuits are mapped to the combinatorial relations. This leads us to the following
1602: natural question:
1603: Question 6.8. Do W+ and L+ map other matroid features to meaningful structures asso-
1604: ciated to neural codes? In particular, do the chirotope, rank function, and convex closure
1605: function have a natural interpretation when mapped to general neural codes?
1606:    This paper focused on the category of oriented matroids, since they have a well-established
1607: notion of morphisms (strong maps) and since they are extensively studied. However, there
1608: is also a notion of “affine strong maps” defined in [16] that may serve to turn affine oriented
1609: matroids into a category. This might also admit a natural functor to neural codes. Ad-
1610: ditionally, the recently defined objects COM’s (which stands for both conditional oriented
...
1609: matroids into a category. This might also admit a natural functor to neural codes. Ad-
1610: ditionally, the recently defined objects COM’s (which stands for both conditional oriented
1611: matroids and complexes of oriented matroids) [1] are a natural place to try to extend strong
1612: maps next.
1613: Question 6.9. Can affine oriented matroids with affine strong maps be embedded in Code?
1614: Can strong maps be defined for COM’s in such a way that the resulting category can be
1615: embedded in Code?
1616:  While strong maps are more frequently used as morphisms of oriented matroids, weak
1617: maps are the next best option.
1618: Question 6.10. Can the category of oriented matroids with morphisms given by weak maps
1619: be embedded in Code?

16:                                          troids on a fixed ground set, and their unlabeled counterparts, the Z-modules of
17:                                          valuative invariants. We give explicit bases for these modules and for their dual
18:                                          modules generated by indicator functions of polytopes, and explicit formulas
19:                                          for their ranks. Our results confirm a conjecture of the first author that G is

25: 
26:                                          1. Introduction
27: 
28:                                              Matroids were introduced by Whitney in 1935 (see [31]) as a combinatorial
29:                                          abstraction of linear dependence of vectors in a vector space. Some standard
30:                                          references are [30] and [20]. Polymatroids are multiset analogs of matroids
31:                                          and appeared in the late 1960s (see [8, 12]). There are many distinct but
32:                                          equivalent definitions of matroids and polymatroids, for example in terms of
33:                                          bases, independent sets, flats, polytopes or rank functions. For polymatroids,
34:                                          the equivalence between the various definitions is given in [12]. We will stick to
35:                                          the definition in terms of rank functions:
36:                                          Definition 1.1. Suppose that X is a finite set (the ground set) and rk : 2X →
...

79:               T (X, rk) =        (x − 1)rk(X)−rk(A) (y − 1)|A|−rk(A) .
80:                                A⊆X
81: 
82: The Tutte polynomial is universal for all matroid invariants satisfying a deletion-
83: contraction formula. Speyer defined a matroid invariant in [26] using K-theory.
84: Billera, Jia and Reiner introduced a quasi-symmetric function F for matroids in
85: [2], which is a matroid invariant. This quasi-symmetric function is a powerful
86: invariant in the sense that it can distinguish many pairs of non-isomorphic
87: matroids. However, it does not specialize to the Tutte polynomial. The first
88: author introduced in [7] another quasi-symmetric function G. For some choice
89: of basis {Uα } of the ring of quasi-symmetric functions, G is defined by
90:                                         X

139:   The following theorem proves a conjecture of the first author in [7]:
140: Theorem 1.4. The G-invariant is universal for all valuative (poly)matroid in-
141: variants, i.e., the coefficients of G span the vector space of all valuative (poly)matroid
142: invariants with values in Q.
143: From G one can also construct a universal invariant for the covaluative property
144: which specializes to Speyer’s invariant.

3418: Appendix A. Equivalence of the weak and strong valuative property
3419: 
3420:     In this section we will prove that the weak valuative property and the strong
3421: valuative property are equivalent.
3422:     For a megamatroid polyhedron Π, let vert(Π) be the vertex set of the
3423: polyhedron. Let WMM (d, r) be the subgroup of ZMM (d, r) generated by all
3424: mval (Π; Π1 , . . . , Πk ) where Π = Π1 ∪ · · · ∪ Πk is a megamatroid polyhedron
3425: decomposition. Define WMM (d, r, V ) as the subgroup generated by all the
3426: mval (Π; Π1 , . . . , Πk ) where vert(Π) ⊆ V .
3427:     A megamatroid rk : 2d → Z ∪ ∞ is called bounded from above if rk(i) < ∞

331: astab(I).                                      
332:    Herzog and Qureshi [19] conjectured that astab(I) = dstab(I) for all polyma-
333: troidal ideal I. The following examples disprove Herzog and Qureshi’s conjecture,
334: in general.
335: Example 2.21. Let n = 4 and
336:           I = (x1 x2 x3 , x22 x3 , x2 x23 , x1 x2 x4 , x22 x4 , x2 x24 , x1 x3 x4 , x23 x4 , x3 x24 , x2 x3 x4 ).
337: Then dstab(I) = 1 and astab(I) = 2.
338: Proof. It is clear I is a polymatroidal ideal. By using [19, Theorem 4.1], we have
339: dstab(I) = 1 and astab(I) = 2, as desired.                      
340:    The following example is a generalization of Example 2.21.
341: Example 2.22. Let n ≥ 4 and I = (x1 x3 ...xn , x23 x4 ...xn , x3 x24 ...xn , ..., x3 x4 ...x2n ,
342: x1 x2 x4 ...xn , x22 x4 ...xn , ..., x2 x4 ...x2n , ..., x1 x2 ...xn−1 , x22 x3 ...xn−1 , ..., x2 x3 ...x2n−1 , x2 x3 ...xn ).
343: Then I is a polymatroidal ideal with dstab(I) = 1 and astab(I) = n − 2.
344: Proof. Since I = In−1;1,1,..,1+In−1;0,2,2,...,2, we easily conclude that I is a polymatroidal
345: ideal. Since I : x3 x4 ...xn = m, we have dstab(I) = 1. For all i = 1, 2, ..., n, I[i] is
346: a square-free Veronese ideal. Thus by [20, Corollary 5.7] astab(I[1]) = n − 2 and
347: astab(I[i]) = n − 3 for all i = 2, 3, ..., n. Therefore by using Proposition 2.9 we have
348: astab(I) = n − 2.                                            
349: Lemma 2.23. Let n = 5 and I ⊂ R be a matroidal ideal with gcd(I) = 1 and d = 3.
350: Then astab(I) 6= 1.
351: Proof. By contrary, we assume that astab(I) = 1. By Proposition 2.9, for all

URL https://web.mit.edu/yufeiz/www/papers/mstd_density.pdf FILE sources/8f44e019054871ab.txt
16:       precision.
17:           We also describe the structure of a random MSTD set S ⊆ {0, 1, . . . , n}. We formalize the in-
18:       tuition that fringe elements are most significant, while middle elements are nearly unrestricted.
19:       For instance, the probability that any “middle” element is in S approaches 1/2 as n → ∞, con-
20:       firming a conjecture of Miller, Orosz, and Scheinerman.
21:           In general, our results work for any specification on the number of missing sums and the
22:       number of missing differences of S, with MSTD sets being a special case.
23: 
24: 
25: 1    Introduction
26: A more sums than differences (MSTD) set is a finite set S of integers with |S + S| > |S − S|, where
27: the sum set S + S and the difference set S − S are defined as
28: 
29:                                       S + S = { s1 + s2 : s1 , s2 ∈ S },
30:                                       S − S = { s1 − s2 : s1 , s2 ∈ S }.
31: 
32: Since addition is commutative while subtraction is not, two distinct integers s1 and s2 generate
33: one sum but two differences. This suggests that S + S should “usually” be smaller than S − S.
34: Thus we expect MSTD sets to be rare.
35:     The first example of an MSTD was found by Conway in the 1960’s: {0, 2, 3, 4, 7, 11, 12, 14}.
36: The name MSTD was later given by Nathanson [8]. MSTD sets have recently become a popular
...
50: The first question was raised by Martin and O’Bryant [5]. Let ρn be the probability that a uniformly
51: chosen random subset of {0, 1, . . . , n} is an MSTD set. In [5] it was shown that ρn ≥ 2 × 10−7 for
52: all n ≥ 14. This is a surprising result since it is contrary to our original intuition that MSTD sets
53: should be rare. It is true that ρn = 0 for n ≤ 13, and ρn is then monotonically increasing at least
54: for n ≤ 26. From this data, Martin and O’Bryant conjectured that ρn approaches some limit and
55: then they estimated this limit using Monte Carlo experiments.
56: 
57: Conjecture 1.1 (Martin and O’Bryant [5]). As n → ∞, the proportion ρn of MSTD sets converges to a
58: limit about 4.5 × 10−4 .
59: 
60:    Previously it was not known whether ρn converges. In this paper, we show that ρn indeed
61: approaches some limit ρ. We also give a deterministic algorithm which can, in principle, compute
62: arbitrarily good lower and upper bounds for ρ.
63: 
64: Theorem 1.2. As n → ∞, the proportion ρn of MSTD sets converges to a limit ρ > 4.28 × 10−4 .
65: 
66:    Our numerical result is a significant improvement over Martin and O’Bryant’s 2 × 10−7 . Un-
67: fortunately, limits of computation prevent us from giving a good upper bound. However, if we
68: were to have unlimited computing power, then our method could give provable bounds for ρ up
69: to any desired precision.
70:    Our proof, like that of Martin and O’Bryant, is non-constructive. As for constructive results,
...

144: semigroups of a given genus [15].
145:       For a uniformly random subset S ⊆ {0, 1, . . . , n} conditioned on λ(S) ∈ Λ, our results imply
146: that the middle segment of S is close to being unrestricted. For instance, the probability that any
147: “middle” element is in S approaches 1/2 as n → ∞, thereby confirming (and generalizing) a
148: conjecture of Miller, Orosz, and Scheinerman [6]. Also, the expectation and variance of the size
149: of S are asymptotically the same as that of the binomial distribution on n + 1 elements. The size
150: distribution of S also follow a central limit theorem.
151:       This paper is organized as follows. We start by focusing exclusively on the MSTD problem.
152: In Section 2 we show that the limit ρ in Theorem 1.2 exists. In Section 3 we elaborate on issues
153: pertaining to computing lower and upper bounds for ρ. Next we move to the general case of
154: subsets S satisfying λ(S) ∈ Λ. In Section 4 we discuss how our methods for MSTD sets can be
155: modified to prove Theorem 1.3. In Section 5 we study the structure of a random set S satisfying
156: λ(S) ∈ Λ. Finally, in Section 6 we offer some concluding remarks.
157:

162: are only relevant to the computation.

985: The rightmost column sum in Table 1 represents an upper bound to the best possible lower bound
986: to ρ that we could obtain without increasing k. Unfortunately, each increment in k would increase
987: the total computation time by a factor of about four (mostly to due to the search for minimal MSTD
988: fringe pairs). Most of our computation time is spent on complete searches through all subsets of a
989: set (in computing the fringe pairs, Gj ( A; k ), and Bh ( A; k )), so perhaps it is worthwhile to come up
990: with more efficient search algorithms.

996: 
997: 4     Extensions to other sum-difference characterizations
998: We have just studied the probability that a random subset S ⊆ [0, n] is an MSTD set. What if we
999: ask finer questions, such as what is the probability that |S + S| − |S − S| = x, where x is some
1000: fixed integer? Or what is the probability that S is missing exactly s sums and d differences? It
1001: turns out that our methods can easily be adapted to deal with all these questions.
1002:       Recall from the introduction that
1003: 
1004:                              λ(S) = (2n + 1 − |S + S| , 2n + 1 − |S − S|)
1005: 
...
1009:                                           {S ⊆ [0, n] : λ(S) ∈ Λ}.
1010: 
1011: Let ρΛ
1012:      n be the probability that a uniform random subset S ⊆ [0, n ] falls into this collection. In this
1013: section we prove Theorem 1.3 showing that ρΛ                     Λ
1014:                                            n approaches a limit ρ as n → ∞. By choosing
1015: Λ = {(s, d) : s < d} we get the MSTD problem.
1016:       Most of the main ideas for the MSTD case carry over to the general case, so we just sketch the
1017: modifications. As with the MSTD problem, we also have a deterministic algorithm for computing
1018: arbitrarily good bounds for each limit, though we will not discuss in too much detail the compu-
1019: tational aspect as it is similar to Section 3. However, even in the case Λ = {(s, d) : s < d}, the
1020: general algorithm to be described is much slower than the more specialized algorithm for MSTD
1021: sets given earlier. Unlike in Section 3, we do not actually carry out the computations, so we make
1022: no effort in optimization.
1023:       The main difference between the solution of the MSTD case presented earlier and the solution
1024: to the general case is that we need to consider a more restrictive analogue of rich sets.
1025: 
1026: Definition 4.1. Let k and n be positive integers with 2k < n. Let S be a subset of [0, n]. We say that
1027: S is k-affluent if [k + 1, 2n − k − 1] ⊆ S + S and [−2n + k + 1, 2n − k − 1] ⊆ S − S.
1028: 
1029:       Whereas rich sets have all the middle sums present, affluent sets additionally have all the
...
1064: 
1065:                                         µ( A, B; k) = lim µn ( A, B; k).
1066:                                                         n→∞
1067: 
1068: The following proposition shows that the limit exists. The result is the analogue of Propositions
1069: 2.9 and 2.10.
1070: 
1071: Proposition 4.3. For every A, B ⊆ [0, k ], the limit µ( A, B; k ) = limn→∞ µn ( A, B; k ) exists.
1072: 
1073: Proof. Assume throughout that n > 2k and S is a uniform random subset of [0, n]. We say that S is
1074: k-quasi-affluent if

1408:       Now we give some results about the size of a random subset S ⊆ [0, n] satisfying λ(S) ∈ Λ.
1409: Because fringe elements do not contribute significantly to |S|, our intuition tells us that the size
1410: of the set should behave similar to an unrestricted binomial distribution. The next two results
1411: confirm this intuition. In the variance part of the next Proposition, we actually need to set the
1412: fringe event F in Theorem 5.1 to be something nontrivial, thereby using the full power of the
1413: theorem.

26:                                                 of st-numbering. Suppose that given some functions f and g, we want to
27:                                                 order the vertices of a graph such that every vertex v is preceded by at least
28:                                                 f (v) of its neighbors and succeeded by at least g(v) of its neighbors. We prove
29:                                                 that this problem is solvable in polynomial time if f g ≡ 0, but it becomes
30:                                                 NP-complete for f ≡ g ≡ 2. This answers a question of the first author posed
31:                                                 in 2009.
32: 
33: 
34:                                         1       Introduction
35:                                         In this paper G = (V, E) always denotes an undirected connected graph, parallel
36:                                         edges are allowed but loops are not. We use n = |V |, d(v) for the degree of a vertex
37:                                         v ∈ V and d(v, Y ) for the number of edges
38:                                                                                 P     going from v to Y . For a function
39:                                         f : V → N, we use the notation f (X) = x∈X f (x) for any X ⊆ V .
40:                                             In a digraph, the indegree (number of incoming arcs) of a vertex v (or of a set
41:                                         X ⊆ V ) is denoted by ̺(v) (or ̺(X), resp.) and the outdegree is denoted by δ(v)
42:                                         (or δ(X), resp.).
43:                                             The first named author proposed to study the complexity of the following prob-
44:                                         lem in 2009 in Category “Orientations” of EgresOpen [1].
45:                                             ∗
46:                                             Research is supported by a grant (no. K 109240) from the National Development Agency of
...
50:                                         under grant number LP2017-19/2017.
51: 
52:                                                                                        1
53: 
54: Problem 1 ([1]). Given G = (V, E) and s, t ∈ V and positive integers k, ℓ, decide
55: whether G has an acyclic orientation where for every vertex v ∈ V \ {s, t}, there
56: are k pairwise arc-disjoint directed paths from s to v, and ℓ pairwise arc-disjoint
57: directed paths from v to t.
58: 
59:     In this paper we settle the complexity of this problem by show that it is NP-
60: complete, already for k = ℓ = 2.
61:     A more general problem that plays a central role in this note, is the following.
62: We are given G = (V, E) and two functions f : V → N and g : V → N with
63: f (v) + g(v) ≤ d(v) for each v ∈ V . An orientation of G is called (f, g)-bounded if
64: f (v) ≤ ̺(v) ≤ d(v) − g(v) for each v ∈ V .
65: 
66: Problem 2 (Degree constrained acyclic orientation problem). Given G, f, g, decide
67: whether G has an acyclic (f, g)-bounded orientation.
68: 
69:     Note that f (v) is a lower bound for the indegree of v while g(v) is a lower bound
70: for the outdegree of v as we are dealing with orientations, so δ(v) = d(v) − ̺(v).
...
68: 
69:     Note that f (v) is a lower bound for the indegree of v while g(v) is a lower bound
70: for the outdegree of v as we are dealing with orientations, so δ(v) = d(v) − ̺(v).
71: 
72: Claim 1. Problem 1 is equivalent to the degree constrained acyclic orientation prob-
73: lem if f (v) = k and g(v) = ℓ for all v ∈ V \ {s, t}; and f (s) = g(t) = 0, g(s) =
74: f (t) = d(s).
75: 
76: Proof. If the prescribed pairwise arc-disjoint directed paths exist, then obviously
77: for every v ∈ V \ {s, t} we have ̺(v) ≥ k and δ(v) ≥ ℓ.
78:     Suppose we have an orientation G     ~ where all v ∈ V \ {s, t} have ̺(v) ≥ k and
79: δ(v) ≥ ℓ. This orientation defines a (not necessarily unique) topological order,
80: V = {v1 , . . . , vn }, such that for every directed edge vi vj we have i < j (we may
81: suppose v1 = s and vn = t). By symmetry and by Menger’s theorem, it is enough
82: to prove that for any X ⊆ V if s 6∈ X, then ̺(X) ≥ k. Let vi be the first vertex of
83: X. As s 6∈ X we have i > 1. Clearly ̺(X) ≥ ̺(vi ) ≥ k.                              

...

92: Condition 1. For every non-empty set X ⊆ V , there exists an x ∈ X which is a
93: potential source for X.
94: 
95: Theorem 2 (Folklore). There exists an acyclic orientation of G where ̺(v) ≥
96: f (v) for every v ∈ V if and only if Condition 1 is satisfied. Moreover, such an
97: orientation can be produced (and simultaneously Condition 1 can be checked) by a
98: greedy algorithm.
99: 
100: Proof. If the orientation exists, then for each X take x as the first vertex of it (by
101: a topological order). Clearly f (x) ≤ ̺(x) ≤ d(x, V \ X).
102: 
103: 
104:                                           2

106:     Suppose Condition 1 is satisfied. By applying it to X = V , we obtain a vertex
107: v1 with f (v1 ) = 0. Let G′ := G − v1 and for any v ∈ V \ {v1 } let f ′ (v) :=
108: max(f (v) − d(v1 , v), 0). Clearly Condition 1 is satisfied for the new G′ and f ′ ,
109: thus, by induction, there is an acyclic orientation of G′ where the indegrees are
110: lower-bounded by f ′ . The required orientation of G is given by orienting the edges
111: incident with v1 from v1 .

118: Theorem 3. If G = (V, E) and f (v)g(v) = 0 for every v ∈ V (i.e., every vertex
119: has either only a lower bound or only an upper bound on the indegree), then we can
120: decide in polynomial time whether there is an (f, g)-bounded acyclic orientation of
121: G.
122: Proof. Let A = {v ∈ V | f (v) = 0, g(v) > 0}, B = {v ∈ V | f (v) = 0, g(v) =
123: 0}, C = {v ∈ V | f (v) > 0, g(v) = 0}, by the condition of the theorem these
124: three sets partition V . Call an acyclic orientation ABC if there is a topological
125: order where the vertices of A come first, then the vertices of B, and finally the
126: vertices of C. It is easy to observe that if an (f, g)-bounded acyclic orientation of
127: G exists, then there is another one which is ABC. Let G′ = G[A], and f ′ (a) =
128: 0, g ′ (a) = max(g(a) − d(a, V \ A), 0) for a ∈ A. Furthermore let G′′ = G[C] and
129: f ′′ (c) = max(f (c) − d(c, V \ C), 0), g ′′ (c) = 0 for c ∈ C. By Theorem 2, we can
130: check in polynomial time whether there is an (f ′ , g ′)-bounded acyclic orientation of
131: G′ and whether there is an (f ′′ , g ′′)-bounded acyclic orientation of G′′ .        
132: 
133:     We call a vertex v strict if f (v) + g(v) = d(v). The next special case we study
134: is, when every vertex is strict.

133:     We call a vertex v strict if f (v) + g(v) = d(v). The next special case we study
134: is, when every vertex is strict.
135: Theorem 4. There exists an acyclic orientation of G where ̺(v) = f (v) for every
136: v ∈ V if and only if f (V ) = |E| and Condition 1 is satisfied. Moreover, such an
137: orientation can be produced (and simultaneously Condition 1 can be checked) by a
138: greedy algorithm.
139: Proof. Observe that if f (V ) = |E|, then ̺(v) ≥ f (v) for every v if and only if
140: ̺(v) = f (v) = d(v) − g(v) for every v.                                       

146: 3     NP-complete cases
147: Theorem 5. The degree constrained acyclic orientation problem (Problem 2) is
148: NP-complete.
149:     We prove a much stronger theorem about a very restricted version of Problem 2.
150: 
151:                                           3
...
149:     We prove a much stronger theorem about a very restricted version of Problem 2.
150: 
151:                                           3
152: 
153: Theorem 6. The degree constrained acyclic orientation problem is NP-complete
154: even if every vertex v is ̺-lower-bounded by 0 or 1 (i.e., f (v) ≤ 1 and g(v) = 0)
155: except one vertex y, which is strict (i.e., f (y) + g(y) = d(y)).
156: Proof. As the problem is obviously is in class NP, it is enough to show its hard-
157: ness. We reduce the well-known NP-complete problem Vertex-Cover [3] to this
158: restricted version. In the problem Vertex-Cover, a graph G = (V, E) and an
159: integer k ≤ |V | is given and we have to decide whether there is a set T ⊆ V with
160: |T | = k such that every edge has at least one endvertex in T .
161:      Given G = (V, E) and k, we need to construct G′ = (V ′ , E ′ ) and functions
162: f, g. Let V ′ = V ∪ E ∪ {y} (where y 6∈ V ∪ E). Let m = |E|, M = m + 1 and
163: E ′ = {ve | v ∈ V, e ∈ E, v is incident to e} ∪ {ye | e ∈ E} ∪ M{yv | v ∈ V }, where
164: the last edge-set is meant to have M parallel edges between y and any vertex v ∈ V .
165: The construction of G′ is finished, its degree-function is denoted by d′ .
166:      Let f (v) = 0 for v ∈ V , f (e) = 1 for e ∈ E and f (y) = m + kM. Let g(v) = 0 for
167: v ∈ V , g(e) = 0 for e ∈ E and g(y) = (n − k)M = d′ (y) − f (y). We need to prove
168: that G has a cover T of size k if and only if G′ has an acyclic orientation satisfying
169: these degree bounds. First suppose T is such a cover and let E2 ⊆ E denote the
...
189:     The graph G′ used in the proof is not simple. However, one can split every edge
190: e of G′ with a new vertex we and define f (we ) = g(we ) = 1. This gives the following
191: corollary.
192: Corollary 7. The degree constrained acyclic orientation problem for simple graphs
193: is NP-complete even if for every vertex v either f (v) + g(v) = d(v) (i.e., v is strict)
194: or f (v) ≤ 1 and g(v) = 0 (v is lower-bonded by 0 or 1).
195: Theorem 8. Problem 1 is NP-complete.
196: Proof. We reduce Problem 2 to Problem 1. Given G, f, g, we construct an instance
197: of Problem 1 as follows. First we fix k = ℓ as the maximum degree in G. We add
198: 
199:                                            4
200: 
201: two new vertices, s and t. Then for each vertex v we add k − f (v) parallel edges
202: between s and v, and we also add k − g(v) parallel edges between t and v. Let
203: G′ = (V ′ , E ′ ) denote the resulting graph (where V ′ = V ∪ {s, t}).
204:    By Claim 1, it is enough to show that the following are equivalent.
205: 
206:     • G′ has an acyclic orientation with source s and sink t where for every v ∈ V ,
207:       we have ̺′ (v) ≥ k and δ ′ (v) ≥ k.
208: 
209:     • G has an acyclic (f, g)-bounded orientation.
...
217: to t; resulting in an acyclic orientation of G′ . As ̺′ (v) = ̺(v) + k − f (v), we get
218: ̺′ (v) ≥ k. We also have δ ′ (v) = δ(v) + k − g(v) ≥ k.                             
219: 
220: 
221: 3.1      Problem 1 for small k values
222: We proved that Problem 1 is NP-complete if k and ℓ are part of the input. What
223: can we say about its status if k and ℓ are fixed small numbers?
224:    The first case is well known, it was solved in [2] where st-numbering was intro-
225: duced.
226: 
227: Theorem 9 ([2]). If k = ℓ is fixed to one, then we can answer Problem 1 in
228: polynomial time, namely there is a required orientation if and only if G + st is
229: biconnected.
230: 
231:     On the other hand, we can prove the following.
232: 
233: Theorem 10. Problem 1 is NP-complete for k = ℓ = 2.
234: 
235: Proof. The problem is obviously in NP. We will reduce the problem Not-all-
236: Equal 3SAT [3] to it. In the problem Not-all-Equal 3SAT there are variables
237: x1 , . . . , xn and clauses C1 , . . . , Cm , each clause contains exactly three literals (a literal
...
322: and outdegrees will not be affected. This way we can obtain a graph that is almost
323: simple - only edges adjacent to s or t might have multiplicity 2.
324:     By splitting s into two vertices, s1 and s2 , and similarly t into t1 and t2 , and
325: dividing the multiple edges among them, we obtain that the following problem is
326: NP-complete.
327: Problem 3. Given a simple graph G = (V, E) and s1 , s2 , t1 , t2 ∈ V , decide whether
328: there is an order of the vetrtices such that every vertex v ∈ V \ {s1 , s2 , t1 , t2 } has at
329: least 2 neighbors preceding it and also at least 2 neighbors succeedenig it.
330:     Some of the questions left open are the following.
331: Open Problem 1. Is Problem 1 in P for the special case k = 2 and ℓ = 1?
332: Open Problem 2. Is Problem 2 in P for the special case f (v) + g(v) ≤ 2 for all
333: v ∈V?
334: Open Problem 3. Is Problem 2 in P for the special case f (v) + g(v) ≤ 3 for all
335: v ∈V?
336: Open Problem 4. Is Problem 2 in P for the special case f (v)g(v) ≤ 1 for all
337: v ∈V?

11:                                                 set A ⊂ V is called quasi-kernel (quasi-sink) iff for each point v
12:                                                 there is a path of length at most 2 from some point of A to v (from
13:                                                 v to some point of A). Every finite directed graph has a quasi-
14:                                                 kernel. The plain generalization for infinite graphs fails, even for
15:                                                 tournaments. We investigate the following conjecture here: for any
16:                                                 digraph G = (V, E) there is a a partition (V0 , V1 ) of the vertex set
17:                                                 such that the induced subgraph G[V0 ] has a quasi-kernel and the
18:                                                 induced subgraph G[V1 ] has a quasi-sink.
